后来，在那间小屋赤裸裸的寂静里，她想起了此行没有办成的事。钱。她一如既往，一面承认它的必要，甚至承认它无可否认的好处，一面又把它的重要性撇在一边。它那种叫人抓不住的品性，她还不曾领教过。她总会找到什么活计的。也许图书馆。这个念头粘住了她。对，一定是图书馆。她懂书，也爱书。

她专注地站在那里，俯瞰楼下远处微微发亮的街道，人潮涌动，聚成一个个小小的漩涡，又散开来，各奔各的路。几分钟后她已站在门口，被一股按捺不住的、汇入人潮的欲望牵着。紫色的天上堆着颤巍巍的云，四处漂移，带着一种无尽的漫无目的。很像那千千万万匆匆赶路的人。望着这些，海尔加忽然发觉自己在想：他们是谁，做些什么，想些什么。那些暗色的血肉模子后面，都在转着什么念头。他们到底真的想事吗？可是，当她跨进那涌动的五彩人流时，一阵古怪的兴奋涌上心头，仿佛正尝着某种可口的异国吃食——甜面包配松露蘑菇浓汁——也许吧。而说来也怪，她还觉得，自己回到家了。她，海尔加·克雷恩，这个无家可归的人。

## VI / 六

===Original===
Helga woke to the sound of rain. The day was leaden gray, and misty black, and dullish white. She was not surprised, the night had promised it. She made a little frown, remembering that it was today that she was to search for work.

She dressed herself carefully, in the plainest garments she possessed, a suit of fine blue twill faultlessly tailored, from whose left pocket peeped a gay kerchief, an unadorned, heavy silk blouse, a small, smart, fawn-colored hat, and slim, brown oxfords, and chose a brown umbrella. In a nearby street she sought out an appealing little restaurant, which she had noted in her last night’s ramble through the neighborhood, for the thick cups and the queer dark silver of the Young Women’s Christian Association distressed her.

After a slight breakfast she made her way to the library, that ugly gray building, where was housed much knowledge and a little wisdom, on interminable shelves. The friendly person at the desk in the hall bestowed on her a kindly smile when Helga stated her business and asked for directions.
===Chinese===
海尔加在雨声里醒来。天是铅灰的，蒙黑的，泛着呆白的。她并不意外，昨夜就预告了这一天。她微微皱起眉，想起今天正是她要去找工作的日子。

她细细地穿戴起来，挑的是她所有衣物里最素净的：一套裁剪无可挑剔的细斜纹蓝呢套装，左口袋里探出一角鲜艳的丝巾，一件不带任何装饰的重磅真丝衬衫，一顶小巧时款的浅黄褐色帽子，一双纤窄的棕色牛津鞋，又拣了一把棕色的伞。她在附近一条街上寻出一家招人喜欢的小馆子——昨夜在附近闲逛时她记下了它——因为基督教女青年会那些厚墩墩的杯子和古怪的暗色银器让她发窘。

草草吃过早饭，她便往图书馆去——那座丑陋的灰色建筑，无尽的书架上盛着许多知识和一点点智慧。海尔加说明来意、请教路径时，门厅问询台那位和气的人赏了她一个善意的微笑。

===Original===
“The corridor to your left, then the second door to your right,” she was told.

Outside the indicated door, for half a second she hesitated, then braced herself and went in. In less than a quarter of an hour she came out, in surprised disappointment. “Library training”﻿—“civil service”﻿—“library school”﻿—“classification”﻿—“cataloguing”﻿—“training class”﻿—“examination”﻿—“probation period”﻿—flitted through her mind.

“How erudite they must be!” she remarked sarcastically to herself, and ignored the smiling curiosity of the desk person as she went through the hall to the street. For a long moment she stood on the high stone steps above the avenue, then shrugged her shoulders and stepped down. It *was* a disappointment, but of course there were other things. She would find something else. But what? Teaching, even substitute teaching, was hopeless now, in March. She had no business training, and the shops didn’t employ colored clerks or salespeople, not even the smaller ones. She couldn’t sew, she couldn’t cook. Well, she *could* do housework, or wait on table, for a short time at least. Until she got a little money together. With this thought she remembered that the Young Women’s Christian Association maintained an employment agency.
===Chinese===
人家告诉她：“左手边的走廊，然后右手第二扇门。”

在那扇指定的门外，她迟疑了半秒，随即鼓起勇气走了进去。不到一刻钟她就出来了，带着吃惊的失望。“图书馆业务培训”——“文官考试”——“图书馆学校”——“分类法”——“编目”——“培训班”——“考试”——“试用期”——一个个词从她心头掠过。

“他们该多有学问啊！”她自嘲地对自己说，穿过门厅走上大街时，对问询台那人含笑的好奇置之不理。她在大道上方高高的石阶上站了好一会儿，然后耸耸肩，走了下来。这*确实*叫人失望，可当然还有别的路。她会找到别的事的。可是什么呢？教书——连代课——三月里是无望了。她没受过商科训练，商店不雇有色人当店员、售货员，小店也不雇。她不会缝纫，不会烹饪。这个，家务她*倒是*能做，也能伺候人吃饭，至少短期内可以。直到攒下一点钱。想到这里，她记起基督教女青年会办着一个职业介绍所。

===Original===
“Of course, the very thing!” She exclaimed, aloud. “I’ll go straight back.”

But, though the day was still drear, rain had ceased to fall, and Helga, instead of returning, spent hours in aimless strolling about the hustling streets of the Loop district. When at last she did retrace her steps, the business day had ended, and the employment office was closed. This frightened her a little, this and the fact that she had spent money, too much money, for a book and a tapestry purse, things which she wanted, but did not need and certainly could not afford. Regretful and dismayed, she resolved to go without her dinner, as a self-inflicted penance, a well as an economy﻿—and she would be at the employment office the first thing tomorrow morning.

But it was not until three days more had passed that Helga Crane sought the Association, or any other employment office. And then it was sheer necessity that drove her there, for her money had dwindled to a ridiculous sum. She had put off the hated moment, had assured herself that she was tired, needed a bit of vacation, was due one. It had been pleasant, the leisure, the walks, the lake, the shops and streets with their gay colors, their movement, after the great quiet of Naxos. Now she was panicky.
===Chinese===
“对了，正是这个！”她出声喊道。“我这就直接回去。”

可是，天虽然还阴着，雨已经停了；海尔加没有回去，倒是在卢普区（Loop）闹哄哄的街市上漫无目的地逛了几个钟头。等她终于原路折回，办公时间已经过了，职业介绍所关了门。这叫她有点心慌；叫她心慌的还有一件事：她花了钱，花得太多了——买了一本书，一只织锦钱包，都是她想要的，却不是必需的，而且分明买不起。她又悔又怕，决意不吃晚饭，算作自我惩罚，也算节省——明天一早头一件事，就上职业介绍所去。

可是又过了整整三天，海尔加·克雷恩才去寻女青年会，或任何别的职业介绍所。那时纯粹是穷途末路把她逼去的，因为她的钱已缩到可怜巴巴的一点。她一直推着那可恨的时刻，宽慰自己说：累了，需要歇几天，也该歇几天了。纳克索斯那一片大静之后，这份闲暇、这些散步、这片湖、这些满街的色彩与流动的商店和街道，本是惬意的。如今她慌了。

===Original===
In the office a few nondescript women sat scattered about on the long rows of chairs. Some were plainly uninterested, others wore an air of acute expectancy, which disturbed Helga. Behind a desk two alert young women, both wearing a superior air, were busy writing upon and filing countless white cards. Now and then one stopped to answer the telephone.

“Y.W.C.A. employment.﻿ ﻿… Yes.﻿ ﻿… Spell it, please.﻿ ﻿… Sleep in or out? Thirty dollars?﻿ ﻿… Thank you, I’ll send one right over.”

Or, “I’m awfully sorry, we haven’t anybody right now, but I’ll send you the first one that comes in.”
===Chinese===
介绍所里，几个面目模糊的女人散坐在一排排长椅上。有的显然心不在焉，有的带着一脸急切的期待，这叫海尔加不安。一张桌子后面，两个机灵的年轻女子都摆着一副高高在上的神气，忙着填写、归档数不清的白色卡片，不时停下来一个接电话。

“女青年会职业介绍所。……是。……请拼一下。……住家还是不住家？三十块钱？……好，谢谢您，我马上派一个人过去。”

或者是：“真抱歉，我们眼下没有人，一来人我就头一个给您送去。”

===Original===
Their manners were obtrusively businesslike, but they ignored the already embarrassed Helga. Diffidently she approached the desk. The darker of the two looked up and turned on a little smile.

“Yes?” she inquired.

“I wonder if you can help me? I want work,” Helga stated simply.
===Chinese===
她们的举止一板一眼得扎眼，却没有理会早已窘迫的海尔加。她怯怯地挨近桌子。两人中肤色深些的那个抬起头，堆起一点笑。

“什么事？”她问。

“不知你们能不能帮我？我想找活儿。”海尔加直截了当地说。

===Original===
“Maybe. What kind? Have you references?”

Helga explained. She was a teacher. A graduate of Devon. Had been teaching in Naxos.

The girl was not interested. “Our kind of work wouldn’t do for you,” she kept repeating at the end of each of Helga’s statements. “Domestic mostly.”
===Chinese===
“也许行。哪种？你有荐书吗？”

海尔加解释说，她是个教师，德文（Devon）学校毕业，一直在纳克索斯教书。

那姑娘不感兴趣。“我们这类的活儿对你不合适，”海尔加每说完一句，她都这么重复一遍。“多半是家务。”

===Original===
When Helga said that she was willing to accept work of any kind, a slight, almost imperceptible change crept into her manner and her perfunctory smile disappeared. She repeated her question about the reference. On learning that Helga had none, she said sharply, finally: “I’m sorry, but we never send out help without references.”

With a feeling that she had been slapped, Helga Crane hurried out. After some lunch she sought out an employment agency on State Street. An hour passed in patient sitting. Then came her turn to be interviewed. She said, simply, that she wanted work, work of any kind. A competent young woman, whose eyes stared frog-like from great tortoiseshell-rimmed glasses, regarded her with an appraising look and asked for her history, past and present, not forgetting the “references.” Helga told her that she was a graduate of Devon, had taught in Naxos. But even before she arrived at the explanation of the lack of references, the other’s interest in her had faded.

“I’m sorry, but we have nothing that you would be interested in,” she said and motioned to the next seeker, who immediately came forward, proffering several much worn papers.
===Chinese===
等海尔加说她什么活儿都愿意干时，那姑娘的态度里爬进了一丝几乎察觉不到的变化，那敷衍的笑消失了。她又问了一遍荐书的事。得知海尔加没有，她尖刻地、一锤定音地说：“对不起，没有荐书我们从不往外派人。”

带着挨了一记耳光的感觉，海尔加·克雷恩匆匆出来了。胡乱吃过午饭，她去州街（State Street）上一家职业介绍所。耐着性子坐了一个钟头，轮到她面谈了。她简单地说，她想找活儿，什么活儿都行。一个能干的年轻女人——镜片是玳瑁大框，一双眼睛从后面鼓出来，活像青蛙——用估价的目光打量着她，问她的来历，从前和现在，也没忘了“荐书”。海尔加告诉她，自己是德文学校毕业，在纳克索斯教过书。可还没等她讲到没有荐书的缘由，对方的兴趣已经淡了。

“对不起，我们没有你会感兴趣的事，”她说着，向下一个求职者摆了摆手；那人立刻上前，递上好几张磨得起了毛边的纸。

===Original===
“References,” thought Helga, resentfully, bitterly, as she went out the door into the crowded garish street in search of another agency, where her visit was equally vain.

Days of this sort of thing. Weeks of it. And of the futile scanning and answering of newspaper advertisements. She traversed acres of streets, but it seemed that in that whole energetic place nobody wanted her services. At least not the kind that she offered. A few men, both white and black, offered her money, but the price of the money was too dear. Helga Crane did not feel inclined to pay it.

She began to feel terrified and lost. And she was a little hungry too, for her small money was dwindling and she felt the need to economize somehow. Food was the easiest.
===Chinese===
“荐书，荐书。”海尔加又怨又苦地想着，走出门，进了那条拥挤俗艳的大街，去找另一家介绍所——在那里，她这一趟同样是白跑。

这样的日子过了一天又一天，一星期又一星期。还有徒劳地翻报纸广告、答报纸广告。她跑遍了几英亩的街道，可在这整座生气勃勃的城里，似乎没有一个人要她效力。至少不要她愿意出的那一种力。倒有几个男人，白人黑人都有，要给她钱，只是那钱的代价太贵了。海尔加·克雷恩不打算付。

她开始觉得害怕，觉得没了着落。她还觉得有点饿，因为她那点钱越缩越小，她觉得总得省着点。饭食是最容易省的。

===Original===
In the midst of her search for work she felt horribly lonely too. This sense of loneliness increased, it grew to appalling proportions, encompassing her, shutting her off from all of life around her. Devastated she was, and always on the verge of weeping. It made her feel small and insignificant that in all the climbing massed city no one cared one whit about her.

Helga Crane was not religious. She took nothing on trust. Nevertheless on Sundays she attended the very fashionable, very high services in the Negro Episcopal church on Michigan Avenue. She hoped that some good Christian would speak to her, invite her to return, or inquire kindly if she was a stranger in the city. None did, and she became bitter, distrusting religion more than ever. She was herself unconscious of that faint hint of offishness which hung about her and repelled advances, an arrogance that stirred in people a peculiar irritation. They noticed her, admired her clothes, but that was all, for the self-sufficient uninterested manner adopted instinctively as a protective measure for her acute sensitiveness, in her child days, still clung to her.

An agitated feeling of disaster closed in on her, tightened. Then, one afternoon, coming in from the discouraging round of agencies and the vain answering of newspaper wants to the stark neatness of her room, she found between door and sill a small folded note. Spreading it open, she read:
===Chinese===
在找工作的当口，她还感到可怕的孤单。这孤单感在滋长，长到吓人的比例，围拢她，把她同四周的全部生活隔开。她垮了，动不动就要哭。在这整座层层攀高的城里，竟没有一个人对她有丝毫挂怀——这使她觉得自己渺小，微不足道。

海尔加·克雷恩不信教。她什么都不凭信。可每逢星期天，她还是去密歇根大道（Michigan Avenue）上那家非常时髦、仪式非常高的黑人圣公会教堂做礼拜。她盼着哪位好基督徒会跟她搭话，请她再来，或者客气地问一句她是不是初来乍到。谁也没有。她变得刻薄起来，比从前更不信教了。她自己没有察觉，她周身悬着一丝拒人千里的冷气，令人不敢亲近——一种傲气，在人心里激起一种说不清的恼怒。人们注意到她，称赞她的衣裳，可是仅此而已；因为那副自给自足、兴味索然的样子——那是她小时候为护住她那极度的敏感而本能披上的甲——至今还粘在她身上。

一种大祸临头的激动感觉围拢过来，越收越紧。后来，一天下午，她跑完一圈令人泄气的介绍所、答完一场空的白报纸广告，回到那间光秃秃的干净房间里，在门与门槛之间发现一张折起的小字条。展开来，她读到：

===Original===
> Miss Crane:
> Please come into the employment office as soon as you return.
> Ida Ross

Helga spent some time in the contemplation of this note. She was afraid to hope. Its possibilities made her feel a little hysterical. Finally, after removing the dirt of the dusty streets, she went down, down to that room where she had first felt the smallness of her commercial value. Subsequent failures had augmented her feeling of incompetence, but she resented the fact that these clerks were evidently aware of her unsuccess. It required all the pride and indifferent hauteur she could summon to support her in their presence. Her additional arrogance passed unnoticed by those for whom it was assumed. They were interested only in the business for which they had summoned her, that of procuring a traveling-companion for a lecturing female on her way to a convention.

“She wants,” Miss Ross told Helga, “someone intelligent, someone who can help her get her speeches in order on the train. We thought of you right away. Of course, it isn’t permanent. She’ll pay your expenses and there’ll be twenty-five dollars besides. She leaves tomorrow. Here’s her address. You’re to go to see her at five o’clock. It’s after four now. I’ll phone that you’re on your way.”
===Chinese===
> 克雷恩小姐：
> 请你一回来就到职业介绍办公室来一趟。
> 艾达·罗斯（Ida Ross）

海尔加对着这张字条端详了好一阵。她不敢抱希望。字条里包藏的种种可能让她有点歇斯底里。终于，洗去满街尘土之后，她下了楼，下到那个她头一回掂出自己“商业价值”之轻的房间。此后的一连串失败更添了她的无能之感，可她恼的是，这些职员显然知道她的不成功。她得唤起全部的骄傲和冷漠的矜持，才能在她们面前撑住。她那额外添上的傲气，那些它冲着摆的人根本没有看见。她们关心的只是叫她来的那桩事：为一个去开会演讲的女人找一名旅途陪同。

“她要的，”罗斯小姐告诉海尔加，“是个聪明人，能在火车上帮她把演讲稿理顺。我们马上就想到了你。当然，这不是长久的事。她会负担你的路费，另外还有二十五块钱。她明天动身。这是她的地址。你五点钟去见她。现在已经四点多了。我这就打电话说你已经在路上了。”

===Original===
The presumptuousness of their certainty that she would snatch at the opportunity galled Helga. She became aware of a desire to be disagreeable. The inclination to fling the address of the lecturing female in their face stirred in her, but she remembered the lone five-dollar bill in the rare old tapestry purse swinging from her arm. She couldn’t afford anger. So she thanked them very politely and set out for the home of Mrs. Hayes-Rore on Grand Boulevard, knowing full well that she intended to take the job, if the lecturing one would take her. Twenty-five dollars was not to be looked at with nose in air when one was the owner of but five. And meals﻿—meals for four days at least.

Mrs. Hayes-Rore proved to be a plump lemon-colored woman with badly straightened hair and dirty fingernails. Her direct, penetrating gaze was somewhat formidable. Notebook in hand, she gave Helga the impression of having risen early for consultation with other harassed authorities on the race problem, and having been in conference on the subject all day. Evidently, she had had little time or thought for the careful donning of the five-years-behind-the-mode garments which covered her, and which even in their youth could hardly have fitted or suited her. She had a tart personality, and prying. She approved of Helga, after asking her endless questions about her education and her opinions on the race problem, none of which she was permitted to answer, for Mrs. Hayes-Rore either went on to the next or answered the question herself by remarking: “Not that it matters, if you can only do what I want done, and the girls at the Y said that you could. I’m on the Board of Managers, and I know they wouldn’t send me anybody who wasn’t all right.” After this had been repeated twice in a booming, oratorical voice, Helga felt that the Association secretaries had taken an awful chance in sending a person about whom they knew as little as they did about her.

“Yes, I’m sure you’ll do. I don’t really need ideas, I’ve plenty of my own. It’s just a matter of getting someone to help me get my speeches in order, correct and condense them, you know. I leave at eleven in the morning. Can you be ready by then?﻿ ﻿… That’s good. Better be here at nine. Now, don’t disappoint me. I’m depending on you.”

As she stepped into the street and made her way skillfully through the impassioned human traffic, Helga reviewed the plan which she had formed, while in the lecturing one’s presence, to remain in New York. There would be twenty-five dollars, and perhaps the amount of her return ticket. Enough for a start. Surely she could get work there. Everybody did. Anyway, she would have a reference.
===Chinese===
她们那副吃定她会抢住这个机会的自作主张，刺痛了海尔加。她觉出一股想叫人下不来台的欲望。把那位演讲女性的地址摔回她们脸上的冲动在她心里蠢动，可她想起那只从臂上晃下的、古色古香的织锦钱包里孤零零的五块钱票子。她赔不起脾气。于是她客客气气地道了谢，动身去大林荫大道（Grand Boulevard）海斯-罗尔太太（Mrs. Hayes-Rore）的家，心里明明白白：只要那位演讲的女性肯要她，她是打定主意接下这份差事的。一个人手里统共只有五块钱的时候，二十五块是不容翘鼻子的。还有饭——至少四天的饭。

海斯-罗尔太太原来是个丰满的柠檬色女人，头发拉直得糟糕，指甲缝里有污垢。她那直直的、穿透人的目光颇有些吓人。她手里捏着笔记本，给海尔加的印象是：一早起来就同其他被种族问题折磨的权威们磋商，而且就此议题开了一整天的会。显然，她既没工夫也没心思去细心穿戴罩在身上的那身落了时尚五年的衣裳——那衣裳即使在崭新的年月也难得合她的身、称她的貌。她为人尖刻，还好刨根问底。问过海尔加数不清的问题——关于她的学历、她对种族问题的见解，哪一个都不容她回答，因为海斯-罗尔太太不是自顾自说下去，就是自己把问题答了：“这也不要紧，只要你干得了我要人干的事就行，女青年会的姑娘们说你行。我是管理委员会委员，我知道她们送来的人不会不行。”这一番话用洪亮的演说腔重复过两遍之后，海尔加只觉得，女青年会的干事们送出她这样一个她们知之甚少的人来，实在是冒了天大的险。

“好，我肯定你行。我其实用不着什么见解，我自己的见解多得很。无非是找个人帮我把演讲稿理顺，改一改，压一压，你明白的。我上午十一点动身。那时候你能准备好吗？……那好。最好九点就到这儿。可别叫我失望。我全指着你呢。”

她走进街市，熟练地穿过那股热烈的人流，一面在心里检视她方才当着那位演讲女性的面定下的盘算：留在纽约。手头会有二十五块，也许还有回程票那一笔。够起步了。她在那儿准能找到事。谁都能。再说，她好歹有荐书了。

===Original===
With her decision she felt reborn. She began happily to paint the future in vivid colors. The world had changed to silver, and life ceased to be a struggle and became a gay adventure. Even the advertisements in the shop windows seemed to shine with radiance.

Curious about Mrs. Hayes-Rore, on her return to the Y she went into the employment office, ostensibly to thank the girls and to report that that important woman would take her. Was there, she inquired, anything that she needed to know? Mrs. Hayes-Rore had appeared to put such faith in their recommendation of her that she felt almost obliged to give satisfaction. And she added: “I didn’t get much chance to ask questions. She seemed so﻿—er﻿—busy.”

Both the girls laughed. Helga laughed with them, surprised that she hadn’t perceived before how really likable they were.

“We’ll be through here in ten minutes. If you’re not busy, come in and have your supper with us and we’ll tell you about her,” promised Miss Ross.
===Chinese===
主意一定，她觉得自己再生了。她欢欢喜喜地用鲜艳的颜色涂抹起未来。世界变成了银色，生活不再是挣扎，成了一场快活的冒险。连商店橱窗里的广告，仿佛都在闪闪发光。

回到女青年会，出于对海斯-罗尔太太的好奇，她走进了职业介绍办公室，名义上是谢谢两位姑娘，并报告说那位重要人物肯用她了。她问，有什么她需要知道的吗？海斯-罗尔太太那样信赖她们的引荐，她几乎觉得非得让人满意不可。她补了一句：“我没怎么得着机会问问题。她好像那么——呃——忙。”

两个姑娘都笑了。海尔加也跟着笑，惊觉自己先前竟没看出她们其实多么可爱。

“我们十分钟后就完事。你要是没别的安排，进来跟我们一起吃晚饭，我们把她的情况讲给你听。”罗斯小姐许诺道。

## VII / 七

===Original===
Having finally turned her attention to Helga Crane, Fortune now seemed determined to smile, to make amends for her shameful neglect. One had, Helga decided, only to touch the right button, to press the right spring, in order to attract the jade’s notice.

For Helga that spring had been Mrs. Hayes-Rore. Ever afterwards on recalling that day on which with well-nigh empty purse and apprehensive heart she had made her way from the Young Women’s Christian Association to the Grand Boulevard home of Mrs. Hayes-Rore, always she wondered at her own lack of astuteness in not seeing in the woman someone who by a few words was to have a part in the shaping of her life.

The husband of Mrs. Hayes-Rore had at one time been a dark thread in the soiled fabric of Chicago’s South Side politics, who, departing this life hurriedly and unexpectedly and a little mysteriously, and somewhat before the whole of his suddenly acquired wealth had had time to vanish, had left his widow comfortably established with money and some of that prestige which in Negro circles had been his. All this Helga had learned from the secretaries at the Y. And from numerous remarks dropped by Mrs. Hayes-Rore herself she was able to fill in the details more or less adequately.
===Chinese===
命运女神终于把目光转向了海尔加·克雷恩，如今似乎铁了心要对她微笑，要为她先前可耻的怠慢作出补偿。海尔加断定：人只要摸对了按钮、按对了机关，就不愁引不起那婆娘的注意。

对海尔加来说，那个机关便是海斯-罗尔太太。此后每想起那一天——她揣着几近空瘪的钱包、怀着忐忑的心，从基督教女青年会走到大林荫大道海斯-罗尔太太家——她总是诧异自己眼力之钝：竟没有看出这个女人凭寥寥数语，就将在她一生的塑形里插上一手。

海斯-罗尔太太的亡夫，曾是芝加哥南区政治那块脏布上一根黑线；他走得急，走得突然，还带着几分蹊跷，又稍稍赶在他那笔骤然到手的财富来得及散尽之前，于是遗下的寡妇有钱安身，还承袭了他在黑人圈子里的一点威望。这些都是海尔加从女青年会的干事们那里听来的。再加上海斯-罗尔太太自己漏出的无数话头，她便能或多或少把细节填补齐全了。

===Original===
On the train that carried them to New York, Helga had made short work of correcting and condensing the speeches, which Mrs. Hayes-Rore as a prominent “race” woman and an authority on the problem was to deliver before several meetings of the annual convention of the Negro Women’s League of Clubs, convening the next week in New York. These speeches proved to be merely patchworks of others’ speeches and opinions. Helga had heard other lecturers say the same things in Devon and again in Naxos. Ideas, phrases, and even whole sentences and paragraphs were lifted bodily from previous orations and published works of Wendell Phillips, Frederick Douglass, Booker T. Washington, and other doctors of the race’s ills. For variety Mrs. Hayes-Rore had seasoned hers with a peppery dash of Du Bois and a few vinegary statements of her own. Aside from these it was, Helga reflected, the same old thing.

But Mrs. Hayes-Rore was to her, after the first short, awkward period, interesting. Her dark eyes, bright and investigating, had, Helga noted, a humorous gleam, and something in the way she held her untidy head gave the impression of a cat watching its prey so that when she struck, if she so decided, the blow would be unerringly effective. Helga, looking up from a last reading of the speeches, was aware that she was being studied. Her employer sat leaning back, the tips of her fingers pressed together, her head a bit on one side, her small inquisitive eyes boring into the girl before her. And as the train hurled itself frantically toward smoke-infested Newark, she decided to strike.

“Now tell me,” she commanded, “how is it that a nice girl like you can rush off on a wild-goose chase like this at a moment’s notice. I should think your people’d object, or’d make inquiries; or something.”
===Chinese===
在载她们去纽约的火车上，海尔加三下五除二就改完、压完了那几篇演讲稿——海斯-罗尔太太作为知名的“种族”女性、种族问题的权威，要在下周于纽约召开的全国黑人妇女俱乐部联合会年会的几个会上发表它们。这些演讲稿，不过是把别人的演讲和见解拼拼凑凑。同样的论调，海尔加在德文听过，在纳克索斯又听过。观点、词句，甚至整句整段，都是从温德尔·菲利普斯（Wendell Phillips）、弗雷德里克·道格拉斯（Frederick Douglass）、布克·T·华盛顿（Booker T. Washington）等种族病痛的良医们先前的演说和刊行的著作里整块搬来的。为了换换口味，海斯-罗尔太太又往她的稿子里撒了一撮杜波依斯（Du Bois）的胡椒，添了几句她自己酿的酸醋。除却这些，海尔加想，还是那一套老东西。

可是过了最初那段短短的尴尬期，海斯-罗尔太太在她眼里变得有趣起来。海尔加注意到，她那双黑眼睛又亮又好探人隐私，眼里含着一丝谐谑的光；她那颗蓬乱脑袋的架势，又给人一种猫盯着猎物的印象——只要她愿意出手，那一击便不会落空。海尔加从最后一遍校读稿子上抬起头，察觉自己正被人端详。她的雇主后仰而坐，指尖对指尖地抵着，头微微侧向一边，那双小巧好问的眼睛直钻着面前的姑娘。火车发疯似的朝烟雾弥漫的纽瓦克（Newark）猛冲，她决定出手了。

“现在告诉我，”她命令道，“像你这样一个好姑娘，怎么说走就走，跑去追这种没影儿的野鹅。我还当你的家里人会反对，或者会来问一问；总该有点动静。”

===Original===
At that command Helga Crane could not help sliding down her eyes to hide the anger that had risen in them. Was she to be forever explaining her people﻿—or lack of them? But she said courteously enough, even managing a hard little smile: “Well you see, Mrs. Hayes-Rore, I haven’t any people. There’s only me, so I can do as I please.”

“Ha!” said Mrs. Hayes-Rore.

Terrific, thought Helga Crane, the power of that sound from the lips of this woman. How, she wondered, had she succeeded in investing it with so much incredulity.
===Chinese===
听了这道命令，海尔加·克雷恩不由得垂下眼帘，掩住眼里升起的怒火。难道她要永远解释她的家里人——或者她的没有家里人？可她的回答还是够客气的，甚至还挤出一丝勉强的笑：“是这样，海斯-罗尔太太，我没有家里人。只有我一个人，所以我想怎样就怎样。”

“哈！”海斯-罗尔太太说。

这女人唇边发出的这一个音，竟有这样的威力，海尔加·克雷恩想。她纳闷，她是怎么做到让这一个音里装满那么多不信的。

===Original===
“If you didn’t have people, you wouldn’t be living. Everybody has people, Miss Crane. Everybody.”

“*I* haven’t, Mrs. Hayes-Rore.”

Mrs. Hayes-Rore screwed up her eyes. “Well, that’s mighty mysterious, and I detest mysteries.” She shrugged, and into those eyes there now came with alarming quickness an accusing criticism.
===Chinese===
“你要是没有亲眷，你就活不成了。人人都有亲眷，克雷恩小姐。人人都有。”

“*我*就没有，海斯-罗尔太太。”

海斯-罗尔太太眯起眼睛。“嗬，这可太玄了，我最讨厌玄虚。”她耸耸肩，那双眼睛里随即飞快地——快得吓人——蒙上一层责难的挑剔。

===Original===
“It isn’t,” Helga said defensively, “a mystery. It’s a fact and a mighty unpleasant one. Inconvenient too,” and she laughed a little, not wishing to cry.

Her tormentor, in sudden embarrassment, turned her sharp eyes to the window. She seemed intent on the miles of red clay sliding past. After a moment, however, she asked gently: “You wouldn’t like to tell me about it, would you? It seems to bother you. And I’m interested in girls.”

Annoyed, but still hanging, for the sake of the twenty-five dollars, to her self-control, Helga gave her head a little toss and flung out her hands in a helpless, beaten way. Then she shrugged. What did it matter? “Oh, well, if you really want to know. I assure you, it’s nothing interesting. Or nasty,” she added maliciously. “It’s just plain horrid. For me.” And she began mockingly to relate her story.
===Chinese===
“这不是玄虚，”海尔加辩护道，“是事实，还是顶不痛快的一件事实。也麻烦，”她轻轻笑了两声，因为不想哭出来。

这个折磨她的人忽然窘了起来，把她那双锐利的眼睛转向车窗。她似乎专注于滑过去的绵延数英里的红土。可过了一会儿，她温和地问：“你不想跟我讲讲，对吧？这事看来让你不好受。我对姑娘们是有兴趣的。”

海尔加心里着恼，可为了那二十五块钱，还死死攥着她的自制。她把头一甩，双手无可奈何地、认输似的一摊，又耸了耸肩。有什么要紧呢？“唉，好吧，您真想知道就说给您听。我担保，没什么有趣的。也不下流，”她存心使坏地补了一句。“只是不折不扣的难堪。对我来说。”于是她带着讥诮开讲她的身世。

===Original===
But as she went on, again she had that sore sensation of revolt, and again the torment which she had gone through loomed before her as something brutal and undeserved. Passionately, tearfully, incoherently, the final words tumbled from her quivering petulant lips.

The other woman still looked out of the window, apparently so interested in the outer aspect of the drab sections of the Jersey manufacturing city through which they were passing that, the better to see, she had now so turned her head that only an ear and a small portion of cheek were visible.

During the little pause that followed Helga’s recital, the faces of the two women, which had been bare, seemed to harden. It was almost as if they had slipped on masks. The girl wished to hide her turbulent feeling and to appear indifferent to Mrs. Hayes-Rore’s opinion of her story. The woman felt that the story, dealing as it did with race intermingling and possibly adultery, was beyond definite discussion. For among black people, as among white people, it is tacitly understood that these things are not mentioned﻿—and therefore they do not exist.
===Chinese===
可是讲着讲着，那股创痛的反叛感又涌了上来，她受过的那番折磨又赫然眼前，像一件蛮横而无妄的横祸。最后的话是从她颤抖的、使性子的唇边滚出来的——热烈地，含着泪，语无伦次。

那位太太仍旧望着窗外，显然对正穿行而过的这座泽西制造业城市灰扑扑的街区外观大有兴趣，为了看得更真，她把头扭得只剩一只耳朵和一小片腮帮子对着车里。

海尔加讲完之后那短短的静默里，两张本来毫无遮掩的脸似乎都硬了起来。简直像各人悄悄扣上了一副面具。姑娘想掩住她的心潮翻涌，想装出不在乎海斯-罗尔太太对她这段身世的看法。那位太太则觉得，这故事既涉及种族的掺杂，说不定还有奸情，是不便深谈的。因为在黑人中间，正如在白人中间一样，大家心照不宣：这类事情是不提的——因此也就不存在。

===Original===
Sliding adroitly out from under the precarious subject to a safer, more decent one, Mrs. Hayes-Rore asked Helga what she was thinking of doing when she got back to Chicago. Had she anything in mind?

Helga, it appeared, hadn’t. The truth was she had been thinking of staying in New York. Maybe she could find something there. Everybody seemed to. At least she could make the attempt.

Mrs. Hayes-Rore sighed, for no obvious reason. “Um, maybe I can help you. I know people in New York. Do you?”
===Chinese===
海斯-罗尔太太机灵地从这个悬乎乎的话题底下溜出来，换上一个更稳妥、更体面的话头，问海尔加回芝加哥以后打算做什么。心里可有什么成算？

看来海尔加没有。实情是，她一直在想留在纽约。也许她能在那儿找到什么。人人似乎都找得到。至少她可以试一试。

海斯-罗尔太太叹了口气，没什么明显的缘故。“嗯，也许我能帮你。我在纽约认识人。你呢？”

===Original===
“No.”

“New York’s the lonesomest place in the world if you don’t know anybody.”

“It couldn’t possibly be worse than Chicago,” said Helga savagely, giving the table support a violent kick.
===Chinese===
“不认识。”

“一个人也不认识的话，纽约是天底下最孤单的地方。”

“再糟也糟不过芝加哥，”海尔加狠狠地说，朝小桌子的支架狠狠踢了一脚。

===Original===
They were running into the shadow of the tunnel. Mrs. Hayes-Rore murmured thoughtfully: “You’d better come uptown and stay with me a few days. I may need you. Something may turn up.”

It was one of those vicious mornings, windy and bright. There seemed to Helga, as they emerged from the depths of the vast station, to be a whirling malice in the sharp air of this shining city. Mrs. Hayes-Rore’s words about its terrible loneliness shot through her mind. She felt its aggressive unfriendliness. Even the great buildings, the flying cabs, and the swirling crowds seemed manifestations of purposed malevolence. And for that first short minute she was awed and frightened and inclined to turn back to that other city, which, though not kind, was yet not strange. This New York seemed somehow more appalling, more scornful, in some inexplicable way even more terrible and uncaring than Chicago. Threatening almost. Ugly. Yes, perhaps she’d better turn back.

The feeling passed, escaped in the surprise of what Mrs. Hayes-Rore was saying. Her oratorical voice boomed above the city’s roar. “I suppose I ought really to have phoned Anne from the station. About you, I mean. Well, it doesn’t matter. She’s got plenty of room. Lives alone in a big house, which is something Negroes in New York don’t do. They fill ’em up with lodgers usually. But Anne’s funny. Nice, though. You’ll like her, and it will be good for you to know her if you’re going to stay in New York. She’s a widow, my husband’s sister’s son’s wife. The war, you know.”
===Chinese===
列车正钻进隧道的暗影。海斯-罗尔太太若有所思地低声道：“你最好跟我上城，在我那儿住几天。我兴许用得着你。说不定会有什么机会。”

那是恶劣透顶的一个早晨，风大，光亮。当她们从大车站的深处走出来时，在这座闪闪发亮的城市尖利的空气里，海尔加仿佛觉得有一股打着旋的恶意。海斯-罗尔太太关于此地可怕孤单的话从她心头射过。她感到了它咄咄逼人的不友好。连那些高楼、飞驰的出租车、打着旋的人潮，都像是蓄意为虐的显形。最初那一小会儿，她被镇住了，害怕了，想要折回那另一座城去——它虽然不亲切，到底不陌生。这座纽约，不知怎么显得更骇人、更轻蔑，在某种说不清的方式里，甚至比芝加哥更可怕、更无情。几乎是威胁。丑恶。是啊，也许她还是折回去的好。

这感觉过去了，在海斯-罗尔太太正说着的话带来的惊愕中溜走了。她那演说家的嗓门盖过了城市的轰鸣。“按理我真该从车站就给安妮打个电话。是说你的事。算了，不要紧。她有的是地方。一个人住一栋大房子，这在纽约的黑人可是少有的事。他们通常都塞满了房客。可安妮就是怪。人倒好。你会喜欢她的，你要是打算待在纽约，认识她对你说不定有好处。她是个寡妇，我丈夫的姐姐的儿媳妇。打仗，你知道的。”

===Original===
“Oh,” protested Helga Crane, with a feeling of acute misgiving, “but won’t she be annoyed and inconvenienced by having me brought in on her like this? I supposed we were going to the Y or a hotel or something like that. Oughtn’t we really to stop and phone?”

The woman at her side in the swaying cab smiled, a peculiar invincible, self-reliant smile, but gave Helga Crane’s suggestion no other attention. Plainly she was a person accustomed to having things her way. She merely went on talking of other plans. “I think maybe I can get you some work. With a new Negro insurance company. They’re after me to put quite a tidy sum into it. Well, I’ll just tell them that they may as well take you with the money,” and she laughed.

“Thanks awfully,” Helga said, “but will they like it? I mean being made to take me because of the money.”

“They’re not being made,” contradicted Mrs. Hayes-Rore. “I intended to let them have the money anyway, and I’ll tell Mr. Darling so﻿—after he takes you. They ought to be glad to get you. Colored organizations always need more brains as well as more money. Don’t worry. And don’t thank me again. You haven’t got the job yet, you know.”
===Chinese===
“哦，”海尔加·克雷恩提出异议，心里一阵强烈的不安，“可是这样把我不由分说塞给她，她不会又气又觉得添麻烦吗？我还以为我们去女青年会，或者旅馆，或者那样的地方呢。我们是不是真该停下来打个电话？”

摇晃的出租车里，身旁这位太太笑了，一种独特的、百折不回的、自有主张的笑，对海尔加·克雷恩的提议却不屑再予理会。她显然是个凡事说一不二惯了的人。她只顾接着谈别的打算。“我想我说不定能给你找点事。一家新开的黑人保险公司。他们正撺掇我往里投一笔不小的款子呢。好，我就跟他们说，这笔钱不妨连你一块儿收下，”说着她笑了。

“多谢了，”海尔加说，“可他们乐意吗？我是说，为了钱的缘故被迫把我收下。”

“谁被迫了，”海斯-罗尔太太反驳道。“那笔钱我本来也要给他们的，我就这么跟达林先生（Mr. Darling）说——等他把你收下之后。他们该高兴才对，居然能得到你。有色人的机构，脑子和票子一样，从来都缺。别担心。也别再谢我了。你还没到手那份差事呢，别忘了。”

===Original===
There was a little silence, during which Helga gave herself up to the distraction of watching the strange city and the strange crowds, trying hard to put out of her mind the vision of an easier future which her companion’s words had conjured up; for, as had been pointed out, it was, as yet, only a possibility.

Turning out of the park into the broad thoroughfare of Lenox Avenue, Mrs. Hayes-Rore said in a too carefully casual manner: “And, by the way, I wouldn’t mention that my people are white, if I were you. Colored people won’t understand it, and after all it’s your own business. When you’ve lived as long as I have, you’ll know that what others don’t know can’t hurt you. I’ll just tell Anne that you’re a friend of mine whose mother’s dead. That’ll place you well enough and it’s all true. I never tell lies. She can fill in the gaps to suit herself and anyone else curious enough to ask.”

“Thanks,” Helga said again. And so great was her gratitude that she reached out and took her new friend’s slightly soiled hand in one of her own fastidious ones, and retained it until their cab turned into a pleasant tree-lined street and came to a halt before one of the dignified houses in the center of the block. Here they got out.

In after years Helga Crane had only to close her eyes to see herself standing apprehensively in the small cream-colored hall, the floor of which was covered with deep silver-hued carpet; to see Mrs. Hayes-Rore pecking the cheek of the tall slim creature beautifully dressed in a cool green tailored frock; to hear herself being introduced to “my niece, Mrs. Grey” as “Miss Crane, a little friend of mine whose mother’s died, and I think perhaps a while in New York will be good for her”; to feel her hand grasped in quick sympathy, and to hear Anne Grey’s pleasant voice, with its faint note of wistfulness, saying: “I’m so sorry, and I’m glad Aunt Jeanette brought you here. Did you have a good trip? I’m sure you must be worn out. I’ll have Lillie take you right up.” And to feel like a criminal.
===Chinese===
有一小会儿沉默。海尔加索性让自己分神去看这座陌生的城、陌生的人群，努力把同伴的话唤起的那个较为顺遂的前景从心里赶出去；因为人家已经点明了，那眼下不过是个可能。

出了公园，拐上伦诺克斯大道（Lenox Avenue）宽阔的干道，海斯-罗尔太太用一种过分刻意随便的口气说：“还有，顺便说一句，我要是你，就不会提我家里人是白人。有色人理解不了这个，再说这终究是你自己的私事。等你活得跟我一样长，你就会明白：别人不知道的事，伤不着你。我就告诉安妮，你是我的一个朋友，母亲过世了。这样说你的身份尽够了，而且句句是实。我从不说谎。剩下的空儿，她自己爱怎么填就怎么填，随她填给谁，谁好奇谁问去。”

“谢谢。”海尔加又说了一遍。感激之情那样重，她竟伸出手，用自己一只素来挑剔的手握住这位新朋友微微沾污的手，一直握到出租车拐进一条宜人的林荫街，停在街区当中一座气派的房子跟前。她们在这里下了车。

此后多年，海尔加·克雷恩只消闭上眼睛，就能看见自己忐忑地站在那间奶油色的小门厅里，地上铺着厚厚的银色地毯；看见海斯-罗尔太太在那个穿一身凉沁沁的绿色定制连衣裙、装束得漂漂亮亮的高挑身影的脸颊上啄了一下；听见人家把自己介绍给“我的外甥媳妇格雷太太”，说这是“克雷恩小姐，我的一个小朋友，母亲去世了，我想她在纽约住一阵子会有好处”；觉出自己的手被同情地一把握住，听见安妮·格雷那悦耳的声音——带着一缕淡淡的怅惘——说：“真难过，也真高兴珍妮特舅妈（Aunt Jeanette）把你带到了这儿。一路还顺吗？你一定累坏了。我这就叫莉莉（Lillie）带你上楼去。”同时觉得自己像个罪犯。

## VIII / 八

===Original===
A year thick with various adventures had sped by since that spring day on which Helga Crane had set out away from Chicago’s indifferent unkindness for New York in the company of Mrs. Hayes-Rore. New York she had found not so unkind, not so unfriendly, not so indifferent. There she had been happy, and secured work, had made acquaintances and another friend. Again she had had that strange transforming experience, this time not so fleetingly, that magic sense of having come home. Harlem, teeming black Harlem, had welcomed her and lulled her into something that was, she was certain, peace and contentment.
The request and recommendation of Mrs. Hayes-Rore had been sufficient for her to obtain work with the insurance company in which that energetic woman was interested. And through Anne it had been possible for her to meet and to know people with tastes and ideas similar to her own. Their sophisticated cynical talk, their elaborate parties, the unobtrusive correctness of their clothes and homes, all appealed to her craving for smartness, for enjoyment. Soon she was able to reflect with a flicker of amusement on that constant feeling of humiliation and inferiority which had encompassed her in Naxos. Her New York friends looked with contempt and scorn on Naxos and all its works. This gave Helga a pleasant sense of avengement. Any shreds of self-consciousness or apprehension which at first she may have felt vanished quickly, escaped in the keenness of her joy at seeming at last to belong somewhere. For she considered that she had, as she put it, “found herself.”
Between Anne Grey and Helga Crane there had sprung one of those immediate and peculiarly sympathetic friendships. Uneasy at first, Helga had been relieved that Anne had never returned to the uncomfortable subject of her mother’s death so intentionally mentioned on their first meeting by Mrs. Hayes-Rore, beyond a tremulous brief: “You won’t talk to me about it, will you? I can’t bear the thought of death. Nobody ever talks to me about it. My husband, you know.” This Helga discovered to be true. Later, when she knew Anne better, she suspected that it was a bit of a pose assumed for the purpose of doing away with the necessity of speaking regretfully of a husband who had been perhaps not too greatly loved.

===Chinese===
自那个春日起——那一天，海尔加·克雷恩在海斯-罗尔太太（Mrs. Hayes-Rore）的陪同下离开芝加哥冷漠的无情，动身前往纽约——塞满了种种奇遇的一年已飞驰而过。她发现纽约并不那么无情，并不那么不友好，并不那么冷漠。在那里她快乐过，谋得了差事，结识了一批熟人，还交上了一位新朋友。她又体验了一回那种奇异的、令人脱胎换骨的经历——这一回不再是稍纵即逝——那种仿佛归家了的奇妙感觉。哈莱姆，人烟鼎沸的黑人哈莱姆，迎接了她，把她哄入了一种她确信无疑便是安宁与满足的境地。
凭海斯-罗尔太太的面子与举荐，她得以在那位精力充沛的女人参股的保险公司里谋得一份差事。又通过安妮，她得以结识并亲近一些趣味与想法和她相仿的人。他们世故而玩世的清谈，他们讲究铺张的宴会，他们衣饰与宅邸那种不事张扬的妥帖雅致，全都投合了她对时兴体面、对行乐享受的渴求。不久，她便能带着一丝自得的兴味，回想当初在纳克索斯包围着她的那种无时不在的羞辱与自惭形秽之感了。她的纽约朋友们对纳克索斯及其一切作为满怀轻蔑与讥嘲。这给了海尔加一种愉快的复仇之感。起初她或许有过的那一星半点的拘谨或惶惑，很快便烟消云散，消融在她那终于仿佛有了归属之地的强烈的喜悦里。因为她认定，用她自己的话说，她已经“找到了自我”。
安妮·格雷与海尔加·克雷恩之间，萌生出一种一见如故、格外投缘的友谊。起初海尔加还有几分不安，后来才松了一口气：母亲之死这个令人难堪的话题——海斯-罗尔太太在两人初次见面时曾故意提起——安妮从未再去碰它，只在当初颤声说过一句：“你不会同我谈这件事的，对吧？我连想到死都受不了。从来没有人同我谈这个。我的丈夫，你是知道的。”海尔加发现这话不假。后来，等她对安妮了解得深了些，她疑心那不过是摆出的一种姿态，为的是免去那份难处：不必再带着惋惜去谈起一位生前恐怕并未被深爱过的丈夫。

===Original===
After the first pleasant weeks, feeling that her obligation to Anne was already too great, Helga began to look about for a permanent place to live. It was, she found, difficult. She eschewed the Y as too bare, impersonal, and restrictive. Nor did furnished rooms or the idea of a solitary or a shared apartment appeal to her. So she rejoiced when one day Anne, looking up from her book, said lightly: “Helga, since you’re going to be in New York, why don’t you stay here with me? I don’t usually take people. It’s too disrupting. Still, it *is* sort of pleasant having somebody in the house and I don’t seem to mind you. You don’t bore me, or bother me. If you’d like to stay﻿—Think it over.”
Helga didn’t, of course, require to think it over, because lodgment in Anne’s home was in complete accord with what she designated as her “aesthetic sense.” Even Helga Crane approved of Anne’s house and the furnishings which so admirably graced the big cream-colored rooms. Beds with long, tapering posts to which tremendous age lent dignity and interest, bonneted old highboys, tables that might be by Duncan Phyfe, rare spindle-legged chairs, and others whose ladder backs gracefully climbed the delicate wall panels. These historic things mingled harmoniously and comfortably with brassbound Chinese tea-chests, luxurious deep chairs and davenports, tiny tables of gay color, a lacquered jade-green settee with gleaming black satin cushions, lustrous Eastern rugs, ancient copper, Japanese prints, some fine etchings, a profusion of precious bric-a-brac, and endless shelves filled with books.
Anne Grey herself was, as Helga expressed it, “almost too good to be true.” Thirty, maybe, brownly beautiful, she had the face of a golden Madonna, grave and calm and sweet, with shining black hair and eyes. She carried herself as queens are reputed to bear themselves, and probably do not. Her manners were as agreeably gentle as her own soft name. She possessed an impeccably fastidious taste in clothes, knowing what suited her and wearing it with an air of unconscious assurance. The unusual thing, a native New Yorker, she was also a person of distinction, financially independent, well connected and much sought after. And she was interesting, an odd confusion of wit and intense earnestness; a vivid and remarkable person. Yes, undoubtedly, Anne was almost too good to be true. She was almost perfect.

===Chinese===
开初那几个愉快的星期过后，海尔加觉得自己欠安妮的情分已经太重，便开始四处寻一个长久的住处。她发现这很不容易。她嫌女青年会（the Y）的宿舍太简陋、太没人情味、清规戒律又太多。带家具的房间她看不上，独住一套或与人合住一套公寓的念头也引不起她的兴致。所以有一天，安妮从书上抬起眼来，淡淡地说：“海尔加，你反正要在纽约住下，何不就住在我这儿？我平时是不收人住的。太搅扰人。不过，家里有个人倒也*确实*有几分叫人高兴，我似乎并不讨厌你。你不招我烦，也不来搅我。要是你愿意留下——那就考虑考虑吧。”海尔加一听，满心欢喜。
海尔加当然用不着考虑，因为寄居在安妮家中，与她自诩的“审美感”完全相合。连海尔加·克雷恩也不禁赞许安妮的宅子，以及那些把大间大间奶油色房间点染得如此雅致的家具。床柱修长渐细的卧床，悠悠岁月为它们平添了庄重与韵致；顶着“帽盖”的老式高脚柜；说不定出自邓肯·法伊夫（Duncan Phyfe）之手的桌子；稀见的细纺锤腿椅子；还有那些梯级靠背椅，椅背的横档优雅地攀上精巧的护墙板。这些古物，与包着黄铜护边的中国茶叶箱、奢华的深扶手椅和长靠榻、色彩明快的小桌、一张配着乌亮黑缎坐垫的碧玉漆绿长沙发椅、光泽莹润的东方地毯、古旧铜器、日本版画、几幅精美的蚀刻画、琳琅满目的珍玩摆设，以及一排排摆满书籍的书架，融洽而安适地交融在一起。
安妮·格雷本人呢，用海尔加的话说，“简直好得不像真的”。约莫三十岁，棕色肌肤的美人，生就一张金色的圣母般的脸，端凝、恬静、甜美，配着乌亮的黑发与黑眼睛。她的仪态就像人们传说中王后的仪态——王后们多半并非如此——雍容自持。她的举止同她那个温柔的名字一样，温文得叫人如沐春风。她在衣着上趣味无可挑剔、一丝不苟，深知什么于己相宜，穿起来带着一种浑然不觉的自信。难得的是，身为土生土长的纽约人，她还是个有声望的人物：经济独立，交游广阔，处处受人追捧。而且她为人有趣，是机智与极度的认真的一种奇特的杂糅；一个鲜明出众的人物。是的，无可置疑，安妮简直好得不像真的。她几乎是完美的。

===Original===
Thus established, secure, comfortable, Helga soon became thoroughly absorbed in the distracting interests of life in New York. Her secretarial work with the Negro insurance company filled her day. Books, the theater, parties, used up the nights. Gradually in the charm of this new and delightful pattern of her life she lost that tantalizing oppression of loneliness and isolation which always, it seemed, had been a part of her existence.
But, while the continuously gorgeous panorama of Harlem fascinated her, thrilled her, the sober mad rush of white New York failed entirely to stir her. Like thousands of other Harlem dwellers, she patronized its shops, its theaters, its art galleries, and its restaurants, and read its papers, without considering herself a part of the monster. And she was satisfied, unenvious. For her this Harlem was enough. Of that white world, so distant, so near, she asked only indifference. No, not at all did she crave, from those pale and powerful people, awareness. Sinister folk, she considered them, who had stolen her birthright. Their past contribution to her life, which had been but shame and grief, she had hidden away from brown folk in a locked closet, “never,” she told herself, “to be reopened.”
Some day she intended to marry one of those alluring brown or yellow men who danced attendance on her. Already financially successful, any one of them could give to her the things which she had now come to desire, a home like Anne’s, cars of expensive makes such as lined the avenue, clothes and furs from Bendel’s and Revillon Frères’, servants, and leisure.

===Chinese===
就这样安顿下来，安稳而舒适，海尔加很快便全身心地沉入纽约生活中那些令人忘忧的种种乐趣。她在那家黑人保险公司的秘书工作占满了白昼。书籍、剧院、宴会，耗尽了夜晚。渐渐地，在这幅崭新而美妙的生活图景的魅力之中，她摆脱了那种撩人的孤独与隔绝的重压——那仿佛始终是她生命里一部分的东西。
然而，哈莱姆那一幅幅绵延不绝的绚烂景象固然使她着迷、使她心荡神驰，白人纽约那种冷冰冰的疯狂奔忙却全然打动不了她。同成千上万别的哈莱姆居民一样，她光顾那边的商店、剧院、美术馆和餐馆，读那边的报纸，却从不把自己看作那头巨怪的一部分。而且她心满意足，毫无歆羡之意。对她来说，这个哈莱姆已经尽够了。对那个白人的世界——那么遥远，又那么切近——她只求冷漠相待，互不相干。不，那些苍白而有权势的人们留不留心她，她丝毫无所渴求。在她看来，那是些阴险的家伙，偷走了她与生俱来的权利。他们过去塞进她生活里的东西，除了耻辱便是悲伤；这些她已统统锁进一只上锁的壁橱，对棕色皮肤的人们藏了起来，“永远，”她对自己说，“不再打开。”
她打算有朝一日，嫁给那些围着她献殷勤的讨人喜欢的棕色或黄色男人中的一个。他们个个都已财力殷实，随便哪一个都能给她如今已然开始渴望的那些东西：一栋安妮那样的宅子，一辆沿着大道停成一排的那种名贵汽车，本德尔（Bendel's）和雷维永兄弟公司（Revillon Frères）的华服与皮草，仆佣，还有清闲。

===Original===
Always her forehead wrinkled in distaste whenever, involuntarily, which was somehow frequently, her mind turned on the speculative gray eyes and visionary uplifting plans of Dr. Anderson. That other, James Vayle, had slipped absolutely from her consciousness. Of him she never thought. Helga Crane meant, now, to have a home and perhaps laughing, appealing dark-eyed children in Harlem. Her existence was bounded by Central Park, Fifth Avenue, St. Nicholas Park, and One Hundred and Forty-fifth street. Not at all a narrow life, as Negroes live it, as Helga Crane knew it. Everything was there, vice and goodness, sadness and gayety, ignorance and wisdom, ugliness and beauty, poverty and richness. And it seemed to her that somehow of goodness, gayety, wisdom, and beauty always there was a little more than of vice, sadness, ignorance, and ugliness. It was only riches that did not quite transcend poverty.
“But,” said Helga Crane, “what of that? Money isn’t everything. It isn’t even the half of everything. And here we have so much else﻿—and by ourselves. It’s only outside of Harlem among those others that money really counts for everything.”
In the actuality of the pleasant present and the delightful vision of an agreeable future she was contented, and happy. She did not analyze this contentment, this happiness, but vaguely, without putting it into words or even so tangible a thing as a thought, she knew it sprang from a sense of freedom, a release from the feeling of smallness which had hedged her in, first during her sorry, unchildlike childhood among hostile white folk in Chicago, and later during her uncomfortable sojourn among snobbish black folk in Naxos.

===Chinese===
每当她的心思不由自主地——也不知怎么，总是常常——转到安德森博士那双若有所思的灰眼睛和他那些高蹈空想的“振兴”宏图上去时，她的额头总要厌烦地蹙起来。至于另外那个詹姆斯·维尔，早已从她的意识里滑落得干干净净。她从来不想起他。海尔加·克雷恩如今打定了主意，要在哈莱姆安一个家，或许还有几个爱笑的、招人疼的黑眼睛孩子。她的天地以中央公园、第五大道、圣尼古拉斯公园（St. Nicholas Park）和第一百四十五街为界。就黑人的过法而言，就海尔加·克雷恩所知的那种过法而言，这绝不是一种狭隘的生活。一切都尽在于此：罪恶与善良，悲哀与欢乐，愚昧与智慧，丑陋与美丽，贫穷与富足。而且在她的感觉里，善良、欢乐、智慧与美丽，不知怎么总要比罪恶、悲哀、愚昧与丑陋略多那么一点儿。唯独富足，还远远盖不过贫穷。
“可是，”海尔加·克雷恩说，“那又算得了什么？钱不是一切。甚至连一半的一半都算不上。何况我们这儿还有那么多别的——而且全凭我们自己。只有在哈莱姆之外、在那些人中间，钱才真正算得上一切。”
身在眼前这个惬意的现在，又望得见一个称心的将来的美妙远景，她心满意足，而且快乐。她不曾剖析过这份满足、这份快乐，只是隐隐约约地——不曾诉诸言辞，甚至不曾凝成思想这样一种切实的东西——知道它发源于一种自由之感，一种从小人物之感的解脱；那感觉曾一路围困着她：起初是在芝加哥满怀敌意的白人中间度过的那个可怜的、没有童趣的童年，后来是在纳克索斯那些势利的黑人中间那段局促不安的羁旅。

## IX / 九

===Original===
But it didn’t last, this happiness of Helga Crane’s.
Little by little the signs of spring appeared, but strangely the enchantment of the season, so enthusiastically, so lavishly greeted by the gay dwellers of Harlem, filled her only with restlessness. Somewhere, within her, in a deep recess, crouched discontent. She began to lose confidence in the fullness of her life, the glow began to fade from her conception of it. As the days multiplied, her need of something, something vaguely familiar, but which she could not put a name to and hold for definite examination, became almost intolerable. She went through moments of overwhelming anguish. She felt shut in, trapped. “Perhaps I’m tired, need a tonic, or something,” she reflected. So she consulted a physician, who, after a long, solemn examination, said that there was nothing wrong, nothing at all. “A change of scene, perhaps for a week or so, or a few days away from work,” would put her straight most likely. Helga tried this, tried them both, but it was no good. All interest had gone out of living. Nothing seemed any good. She became a little frightened, and then shocked to discover that, for some unknown reason, it was of herself she was afraid.
Spring grew into summer, languidly at first, then flauntingly. Without awareness on her part, Helga Crane began to draw away from those contacts which had so delighted her. More and more she made lonely excursions to places outside of Harlem. A sensation of estrangement and isolation encompassed her. As the days became hotter and the streets more swarming, a kind of repulsion came upon her. She recoiled in aversion from the sight of the grinning faces and from the sound of the easy laughter of all these people who strolled, aimlessly now, it seemed, up and down the avenues. Not only did the crowds of nameless folk on the street annoy her, she began also actually to dislike her friends.
Even the gentle Anne distressed her. Perhaps because Anne was obsessed by the race problem and fed her obsession. She frequented all the meetings of protest, subscribed to all the complaining magazines, and read all the lurid newspapers spewed out by the Negro yellow press. She talked, wept, and ground her teeth dramatically about the wrongs and shames of her race. At times she lashed her fury to surprising heights for one by nature so placid and gentle. And, though she would not, even to herself, have admitted it, she reveled in this orgy of protest.

===Chinese===
可是海尔加·克雷恩的这份幸福并没有持续多久。
春意一点一点显露出来，可是奇怪的是，这个季节的迷人风光——哈莱姆那些快活的居民如此热烈、如此铺张地迎候着的季节——在她心中激起的却只是烦乱不安。在她内心深处的某个角落里，蛰伏着不满。她开始对自己生活的丰盈失去信心，她心中对生活的憧憬开始褪去光华。随着时日累积，她对某种东西的渴求——某种依稀熟悉、却叫不出名字也无法固定下来细细审视的东西——变得几乎无法忍受。她经历了一阵阵铺天盖地的苦痛。她感到被禁闭了、被困住了。“也许我是累了，需要吃点补药，或者别的什么，”她这样想。于是她去看了医生，医生经过一番漫长而郑重的检查之后，说她什么毛病也没有，一点儿也没有。“换个环境，也许一个星期上下，或者离开工作几天，”多半就能让她恢复如常。海尔加试了这些法子，两样都试了，可是毫无起色。生活的一切意趣都已散尽。似乎什么都不对劲了。她有点儿害怕起来，随后又惊愕地发现，不知出于什么缘故，她怕的竟是她自己。
春天渐渐入了夏，起初懒懒的，随后便招摇起来。海尔加·克雷恩不知不觉间开始疏远那些曾令她如此快乐的交往。她越来越多地独自出游，到哈莱姆以外的地方去。一种疏离与孤单的感觉笼罩了她。随着天气日渐炎热、街头日渐拥挤，她心里生出一种反感。看到那些咧嘴嘻笑的脸，听到那些人轻松的笑声——如今他们似乎是在漫无目的地沿着大街来来去去——她便厌恶地掉开眼。街上那一群群无名之辈不仅使她恼火，她还当真开始讨厌起自己的朋友们来。
就连温柔的安妮也让她心烦。也许是因为安妮被种族问题迷住了心窍，还在不断给这份痴迷添柴加火。她场场不落地参加各种抗议集会，订阅所有满纸怨言的杂志，黑人黄色报刊喷吐出来的耸人听闻的报纸她一份不落地读。她慷慨激昂地谈着、哭着、咬着牙，诉说她这个种族的冤屈与耻辱。对于一个生性如此恬静温柔的人，她有时竟能把怒火煽到令人吃惊的高度。而且，尽管她决不会承认——哪怕对自己也不承认——她也确实沉醉在这场抗议的狂欢里。

===Original===
“Social equality,” “Equal opportunity for all,” were her slogans, often and emphatically repeated. Anne preached these things and honestly thought that she believed them, but she considered it an affront to the race, and to all the varicolored peoples that made Lenox and Seventh Avenues the rich spectacles which they were, for any Negro to receive on terms of equality any white person.
“To me,” asserted Anne Grey, “the most wretched Negro prostitute that walks One Hundred and Thirty-fifth Street is more than any president of these United States, not excepting Abraham Lincoln.” But she turned up her finely carved nose at their lusty churches, their picturesque parades, their naive clowning on the streets. She would not have desired or even have been willing to live in any section outside the black belt, and she would have refused scornfully, had they been tendered, any invitation from white folk. She hated white people with a deep and burning hatred, with the kind of hatred which, finding itself held in sufficiently numerous groups, was capable some day, on some great provocation, of bursting into dangerously malignant flames.
But she aped their clothes, their manners, and their gracious ways of living. While proclaiming loudly the undiluted good of all things Negro, she yet disliked the songs, the dances, and the softly blurred speech of the race. Toward these things she showed only a disdainful contempt, tinged sometimes with a faint amusement. Like the despised people of the white race, she preferred Pavlova to Florence Mills, John McCormack to Taylor Gordon, Walter Hampden to Paul Robeson. Theoretically, however, she stood for the immediate advancement of all things Negroid, and was in revolt against social inequality.
Helga had been entertained by this racial ardor in one so little affected by racial prejudice as Anne, and by her inconsistencies. But suddenly these things irked her with a great irksomeness and she wanted to be free of this constant prattling of the incongruities, the injustices, the stupidities, the viciousness of white people. It stirred memories, probed hidden wounds, whose poignant ache bred in her surprising oppression and corroded the fabric of her quietism. Sometimes it took all her self-control to keep from tossing sarcastically at Anne Ibsen’s remark about there being assuredly something very wrong with the drains, but after all there were other parts of the edifice.

===Chinese===
“社会平等”“人人机会均等”，是她的口号，被她一而再、再而三地铿锵有力地重复着。安妮宣讲这些主张，并且诚心诚意地以为自己信仰它们；可是她又认为，任何一个黑人若以平等之礼接待任何白人，那便是对这个种族的冒犯，也是对那些把伦诺克斯（Lenox）和第七大道装点成眼前这般繁华景致的各色人等的冒犯。
“在我看来，”安妮·格雷断言，“走在第一百三十五街上最下等的黑人妓女，也胜过这合众国的任何一位总统，亚伯拉罕·林肯（Abraham Lincoln）也不例外。”可是她却对黑人那些热气腾腾的教堂、别致好看的游行、街头天真的打诨嗤之以鼻。她不曾想要、甚至根本不愿意住在黑人聚居区以外的任何地段；白人若真递来请帖，她也会轻蔑地拒绝。她怀着深沉而炽烈的仇恨恨着白人——那种仇恨，一旦发觉自己聚积在足够庞大的群体之中，便有朝一日会在某个重大挑衅之下，骤然燃成凶险的恶焰。
可是她却模仿白人的衣着、白人的举止、白人雍容优雅的生活方式。她一面高声宣扬一切黑人事物纯粹无杂的好处，一面却不喜欢这个种族的歌谣、舞蹈，还有那种软绵绵含混不清的谈吐。对这一切，她只报以轻蔑的鄙夷，有时还掺着一丝淡淡的觉得好笑。正像被她瞧不起的白人一样，比起弗洛伦斯·米尔斯（Florence Mills）她更偏爱帕夫洛娃（Pavlova），比起泰勒·戈登（Taylor Gordon）更偏爱约翰·麦科马克（John McCormack），比起保罗·罗伯逊（Paul Robeson）更偏爱沃尔特·汉普登（Walter Hampden）。然而在理论上，她主张立刻提升一切黑人事物，反抗社会的不平等。
海尔加原本觉得有趣：像安妮这样几乎不受种族偏见沾染的人，竟有这么一股种族热忱，还有她那些自相矛盾处。可是突然间，这一切叫她腻烦透了，她想摆脱这没完没了的絮叨——絮叨白人的荒唐、白人的不公、白人的愚蠢、白人的恶毒。它搅动旧日的记忆，刺探隐秘的伤口，那剧烈的创痛在她心里生出令人愕然的郁结，侵蚀着她恬静无为的心境。有时她得用尽全部自制力，才忍住没有尖刻地把易卜生（Ibsen）那句话扔回给安妮——说什么无疑一定是下水道出了大毛病，可大厦毕竟还有别的部分。

===Original===
It was at this period of restiveness that Helga met again Dr. Anderson. She was gone, unwillingly, to a meeting, a health meeting, held in a large church﻿—as were most of Harlem’s uplift activities﻿—as a substitute for her employer, Mr. Darling. Making her tardy arrival during a tedious discourse by a pompous saffron-hued physician, she was led by the irritated usher, whom she had roused from a nap in which he had been pleasantly freed from the intricacies of Negro health statistics, to a very front seat. Complete silence ensued while she subsided into her chair. The offended doctor looked at the ceiling, at the floor, and accusingly at Helga, and finally continued his lengthy discourse. When at last he had ended and Helga had dared to remove her eyes from his sweating face and look about, she saw with a sudden thrill that Robert Anderson was among her nearest neighbors. A peculiar, not wholly disagreeable, quiver ran down her spine. She felt an odd little faintness. The blood rushed to her face. She tried to jeer at herself for being so moved by the encounter.
He, meanwhile, she observed, watched her gravely. And having caught her attention, he smiled a little and nodded.
When all who so desired had spouted to their hearts’ content﻿—if to little purpose﻿—and the meeting was finally over, Anderson detached himself from the circle of admiring friends and acquaintances that had gathered around him and caught up with Helga halfway down the long aisle leading out to fresher air.
“I wondered if you were really going to cut me. I see you were,” he began, with that half-quizzical smile which she remembered so well.

===Chinese===
正是在这段躁动难安的日子里，海尔加与安德森博士重逢了。她不情愿地去出席一个会——一个卫生会，在一座大教堂里举行——哈莱姆的大多数“振兴”活动皆如此——是替她的雇主达林先生（Mr. Darling）去的。她姗姗来迟，那时一位面色姜黄、自命不凡的医生正发表着一篇冗长乏味的演讲；被她从瞌睡里惊醒的引座员——他正睡得香甜，从黑人卫生统计的种种繁难中解脱出来——气呼呼地把她领到了最前排的一个座位。她落了座，全场随即一片肃静。那位受了冒犯的医生看看天花板，看看地板，又责难似的看看海尔加，终于又续上他那篇长篇大论。待他总算讲完，海尔加才敢把目光从他汗涔涔的脸上挪开四下张望，心头突然一激灵：罗伯特·安德森就坐在离她最近的人当中。一阵奇异的、并非全然不快的震颤顺着她的脊背流下去。她感到一阵说不出的轻微晕眩。血涌上了她的脸。她想嘲笑自己，竟为这次重逢这般心神摇荡。
她发觉，与此同时他正神情肃然地注视着她。待捕捉到她的目光，他微微一笑，点了点头。
等所有有意发言的人都痛痛快快地讲够了兴——如果说没什么用处的话——会议终于散了，安德森从围拢在他身边的那一圈敬慕他的朋友熟人中间脱出身来，在那条通往外面新鲜空气的长长通道的半道上追上了海尔加。
“我还纳闷你是不是真要跟我一刀两断呢。看来是真断了。”他开口说，脸上挂着那她记忆犹新的半带揶揄的微笑。

===Original===
She laughed. “Oh, I didn’t think you’d remember me.” Then she added: “Pleasantly, I mean.”
The man laughed too. But they couldn’t talk yet. People kept breaking in on them. At last, however, they were at the door, and then he suggested that they share a taxi “for the sake of a little breeze.” Helga assented.
Constraint fell upon them when they emerged into the hot street, made seemingly hotter by a low-hanging golden moon and the hundreds of blazing electric lights. For a moment, before hailing a taxi, they stood together looking at the slow moving mass of perspiring human beings. Neither spoke, but Helga was conscious of the man’s steady gaze. The prominent gray eyes were fixed upon her, studying her, appraising her. Many times since turning her back on Naxos she had in fancy rehearsed this scene, this re-encounter. Now she found that rehearsal helped not at all. It was so absolutely different from anything that she had imagined.
In the open taxi they talked of impersonal things, books, places, the fascination of New York, of Harlem. But underneath the exchange of small talk lay another conversation of which Helga Crane was sharply aware. She was aware, too, of a strange ill-defined emotion, a vague yearning rising within her. And she experienced a sensation of consternation and keen regret when with a lurching jerk the cab pulled up before the house in One Hundred and Thirty-ninth Street. So soon, she thought.

===Chinese===
她笑了。“哦，我没想到你还记得我呢。”接着又补上一句：“我是说，记得还挺愉快。”
男人也笑了。可他们一时还说不上话。总有人来打断他们。不过他们终于到了门口，于是他提议两人合乘一辆出租汽车，“好吹一点小风”。海尔加应允了。
走到外面那条炎热的大街上，两人都拘谨起来。一轮低垂的金月和几百盏白炽的电灯，把街道映得仿佛愈发炙热。招呼出租车之前，他们并肩站了片刻，望着那一团缓缓蠕动的、汗涔涔的人群。谁都没有开口，可海尔加觉察到那男人凝定的注视。那双突出的灰眼睛牢牢盯住她，端详她，掂量她。自从背弃纳克索斯以来，她曾在想象中一遍遍排演过这一幕，这场重逢。此刻她发现，排演全然无济于事。它与她想象过的一切都截然不同。
在敞篷出租车里，他们谈些无关个人的话题，书籍、地方、纽约的迷人、哈莱姆的迷人。可是在这番闲谈底下，还潜着另一场对话，海尔加·克雷恩对它敏锐地有所觉察。她还觉察到一种奇异的、轮廓不清的情绪，一种隐隐的渴望在她心中升起。当车身猛地一顿，停在第一百三十九街那栋房子前时，她心里掠过一阵慌乱和深切的怅惘。这么快就到了，她想。

===Original===
But she held out her hand calmly, coolly. Cordially she asked him to call some time. “It is,” she said, “a pleasure to renew our acquaintance.” Was it, she was wondering, merely an acquaintance?
He responded seriously that he too thought it a pleasure, and added: “You haven’t changed. You’re still seeking for something, I think.”
At his speech there dropped from her that vague feeling of yearning, that longing for sympathy and understanding which his presence evoked. She felt a sharp stinging sensation and a recurrence of that anger and defiant desire to hurt which had so seared her on that past morning in Naxos. She searched for a biting remark, but, finding none venomous enough, she merely laughed a little rude and scornful laugh and, throwing up her small head, bade him an impatient good night and ran quickly up the steps.
Afterwards she lay for long hours without undressing, thinking angry self-accusing thoughts, recalling and reconstructing that other explosive contact. That memory filled her with a sort of aching delirium. A thousand indefinite longings beset her. Eagerly she desired to see him again to right herself in his thoughts. Far into the night she lay planning speeches for their next meeting, so that it was long before drowsiness advanced upon her.

===Chinese===
可她还是平静地、冷冷地伸出手去。她客客气气地请他改日来坐。“能够重续我们的相识，”她说，“是一桩乐事。”仅止于相识吗？她心里犯着嘀咕。
他一本正经地回答说，他也觉得是一桩乐事，又添了一句：“你没有变。我想，你还在寻寻觅觅着什么。”
听了这话，那种隐隐的渴望，那种因他在场而勾起的、对同情与理解的渴念，从她身上倏然褪尽了。她感到一阵尖锐的刺痛，那股怒气、那股反抗的伤人欲望又卷土重来——当年在纳克索斯的那个早晨，正是它把她灼得那么狠。她搜罗一句刻薄话，却找不出一句够恶毒的，于是只发出一声粗鲁而轻蔑的浅笑，昂起她小小的头，不耐烦地对他道了晚安，快步跑上台阶去了。
过后她衣裳也不脱，一躺就是好几个钟头，翻来覆去想着愤怒的、自责的心事，回想并重温着先前那场火爆的交锋。那段记忆使她充满一种隐隐作痛的迷狂。千百种说不清的渴望向她袭来。她急切地想再见他一面，好在他的心目中为自己挽回形象。直到夜深许久，她还躺着为下一回见面打腹稿，以致睡意迟迟才向她进袭。

===Original===
When he did call, Sunday, three days later, she put him off on Anne and went out, pleading an engagement, which until then she had not meant to keep. Until the very moment of his entrance she had had no intention of running away, but something, some imp of contumacy, drove her from his presence, though she longed to stay. Again abruptly had come the uncontrollable wish to wound. Later, with a sense of helplessness and inevitability, she realized that the weapon which she had chosen had been a boomerang, for she herself had felt the keen disappointment of the denial. Better to have stayed and hurled polite sarcasms at him. She might then at least have had the joy of seeing him wince.
In this spirit she made her way to the corner and turned into Seventh Avenue. The warmth of the sun, though gentle on that afternoon, had nevertheless kissed the street into marvelous light and color. Now and then, greeting an acquaintance, or stopping to chat with a friend, Helga was all the time seeing its soft shining brightness on the buildings along its sides or on the gleaming bronze, gold, and copper faces of its promenaders. And another vision, too, came haunting Helga Crane; level gray eyes set down in a brown face which stared out at her, coolly, quizzically, disturbingly. And she was not happy.
The tea to which she had so suddenly made up her mind to go she found boring beyond endurance, insipid drinks, dull conversation, stupid men. The aimless talk glanced from John Wellinger’s lawsuit for discrimination because of race against a downtown restaurant and the advantages of living in Europe, especially in France, to the significance, if any, of the Garvey movement. Then it sped to a favorite Negro dancer who had just then secured a foothold on the stage of a current white musical comedy, to other shows, to a new book touching on Negroes. Thence to costumes for a coming masquerade dance, to a new jazz song, to Yvette Dawson’s engagement to a Boston lawyer who had seen her one night at a party and proposed to her the next day at noon. Then back again to racial discrimination.
Why, Helga wondered, with unreasoning exasperation, didn’t they find something else to talk of? Why must the race problem always creep in? She refused to go on to another gathering. It would, she thought, be simply the same old thing.

===Chinese===
三天后的星期日他真的来访，她把他推给了安妮，自己出门去了，推说有个约——在那之前她本无意赴的那个约。直到他进门的那一刻，她都全无避开的念头，可是有个什么，某个倔强抗命的小鬼，把她从他身边撵走了，虽然她渴望留下。那股按捺不住的伤人欲望又猝然袭来。事后，怀着一种无能为力、势所必然之感，她明白自己选的武器原来是一柄回旋镖，因为拒不赴约的失望之痛，她自己也尝了个真切。倒不如留下来，冲他掷几句客客气气的讥诮。那样她至少能享受看他皱一皱眉的快乐。
她带着这股心绪走到街角，拐上了第七大道。午后阳光的暖意虽然温柔，却仍把这条大街亲吻成一片奇妙的明光与色彩。海尔加一路时而招呼一声熟人，时而停下来同朋友聊上几句，目光却始终流连在那柔和明亮的光辉上——它洒在两旁的建筑物上，洒在漫步的人们那泛着光泽的古铜色、金色、赤铜色的脸膛上。还有另一个幻影也来纠缠海尔加·克雷恩：一双平直的灰眼睛，嵌在一张棕色的面孔里，冷冷的、带点揶揄的、扰人心神地凝望着她。她并不快乐。
那场她一时拿定主意要去的茶会，她发觉乏味得叫人无法忍受：寡淡的饮品，沉闷的谈话，愚蠢的男人。漫无边际的闲谈，从约翰·韦林格（John Wellinger）因种族身份起诉下城一家餐馆歧视的官司，谈到住在欧洲尤其是法国的种种好处，又谈到加维运动（Garvey movement）的意义——倘若当真有什么意义的话。接着话头又窜到一位人们喜爱的黑人舞者身上，他刚刚在时下一出白人音乐喜剧的舞台上站住了脚；再谈到别的演出、一本写到黑人的新书。然后又谈到即将举行的一场化装舞会的行头、一支新出的爵士歌、伊薇特·道森（Yvette Dawson）与一位波士顿律师的订婚——那人在一次晚会上见过她一面，第二天中午便向她求婚了。然后又兜回种族歧视。
为什么，海尔加不无莫名地恼火地想，他们就找不出点别的来谈谈？为什么种族问题总要钻出来？她拒绝再到另一处聚会去。去了，她想，也不过是老一套。

===Original===
On her arrival home she was more disappointed than she cared to admit to find the house in darkness and even Anne gone off somewhere. She would have liked that night to have talked with Anne. Get her opinion of Dr. Anderson.
Anne it was who the next day told her that he had given up his work in Naxos; or rather that Naxos had given him up. He had been too liberal, too lenient, for education as it was inflicted in Naxos. Now he was permanently in New York, employed as welfare worker by some big manufacturing concern, which gave employment to hundreds of Negro men.
“Uplift,” sniffed Helga contemptuously, and fled before the onslaught of Anne’s harangue on the needs and ills of the race.

===Chinese===
回到家，她发现屋子一片漆黑，连安妮也不知去了哪里，心里的失望超出了她愿意承认的程度。那天夜里她本想同安妮谈谈，听听她对安德森博士的看法。
第二天，正是安妮告诉她，他已经辞去了纳克索斯的工作；或者不如说，是纳克索斯舍弃了他。对于纳克索斯所施行的教育而言，他太开明、太宽纵了。如今他在纽约长住下来，受雇于一家雇用着数百名黑人工人的大制造公司，当一名福利干事。
“‘振兴’，”海尔加轻蔑地嗤了一声，趁安妮关于这个种族的贫弊疾苦的长篇大论倾泻下来之前，赶紧逃开了。

## X / 十

===Original===
With the waning summer the acute sensitiveness of Helga Crane’s frayed nerves grew keener. There were days when the mere sight of the serene tan and brown faces about her stung her like a personal insult. The carefree quality of their laughter roused in her the desire to scream at them: “Fools, fools! Stupid fools!” This passionate and unreasoning protest gained in intensity, swallowing up all else like some dense fog. Life became for her only a hateful place where one lived in intimacy with people one would not have chosen had one been given choice. It was, too, an excruciating agony. She was continually out of temper. Anne, thank the gods! was away, but her nearing return filled Helga with dismay.
Arriving at work one sultry day, hot and dispirited, she found waiting a letter, a letter from Uncle Peter. It had originally been sent to Naxos, and from there it had made the journey back to Chicago to the Young Women’s Christian Association, and then to Mrs. Hayes-Rore. That busy woman had at last found time between conventions and lectures to readdress it and had sent it on to New York. Four months, at least, it had been on its travels. Helga felt no curiosity as to its contents, only annoyance at the long delay, as she ripped open the thin edge of the envelope, and for a space sat staring at the peculiar foreign script of her uncle.

===Chinese===
随着残夏消磨，海尔加·克雷恩那被磨得脆弱的神经愈发敏锐了。有些日子，仅仅看到周围那些安详的黄褐、棕褐色的脸，就像受到人身侮辱一样刺痛她。他们那种无忧无虑的笑声，激起她向他们尖叫的欲望：“傻瓜，傻瓜！愚蠢的傻瓜！”这股炽烈而全无理智的抗辩愈发强烈，像浓雾一般吞没了一切。生活于她成了一个可憎的地方——人在这里，与种种若让自己挑拣便决计不会选中的人们朝夕相处。这还是一种撕心的痛楚。她动辄动怒。安妮，谢天谢地，出门在外了，可她归期渐近，又让海尔加满心惶惶。
一个闷热的日子，她又热又丧气地到了班上，发现有一封信在等着她——彼得舅父（Uncle Peter）来的信。这信起初寄到纳克索斯，又从那里辗转回到芝加哥基督教女青年会（Young Women’s Christian Association），再到海斯-罗尔太太（Mrs. Hayes-Rore）手里。那位忙碌的太太总算在集会与演讲的间隙挤出时间重新写了地址，把它转来纽约。这一路，它至少走了四个月。海尔加对信的内容毫不好奇，只恼恨它迟迟才到。她撕开信封的薄边，呆坐了一阵，望着舅父那笔独特的异国字迹。

===Original===
> It is now over a year since you made your unfortunate call here. It was unfortunate for us all, you, Mrs. Nilssen, and myself. But of course you couldn’t know. I blame myself. I should have written you of my marriage.
> I have looked for a letter, or some word from you; evidently, with your usual penetration, you understood thoroughly that I must terminate my outward relation with you. You were always a keen one.
> Of course I am sorry, but it can’t be helped. My wife must be considered, and she feels very strongly about this.
> You know, of course, that I wish you the best of luck. But take an old man’s advice and don’t do as your mother did. Why don’t you run over and visit your Aunt Katrina? She always wanted you. Maria Kirkeplads, No. 2, will find her.
> I enclose what I intended to leave you at my death. It is better and more convenient that you get it now. I wish it were more, but even this little may come in handy for a rainy day.
> Best wishes for your luck.
> Peter Nilssen

===Chinese===
> 你那回不幸到我们这里来，至今已一年多了。那对我们大家都是不幸的——对你，对尼尔森太太（Mrs. Nilssen），对我。当然，你那时无从知晓。怪我自己。我本该把我的婚事写信告诉你的。
> 我一直盼着一封信，或者你的只言片语；显然，以你一贯的敏锐，你完全明白，我必须同你断绝表面的亲属关系。你从来就是个伶俐人。
> 我自然很抱歉，可这也是没法子的事。总得顾及我的妻子，在这件事上她的态度十分坚决。
> 你当然知道，我愿你万事顺遂。不过听老头子一句劝，别照你母亲那样行事。你何不径自去看望你的卡特琳舅妈？她一向疼你。到玛丽亚·基尔克普拉兹（Maria Kirkeplads）二号就能找到她。
> 随信附上一笔我原打算身后留给你的钱。你现下就拿到，更好也更省事。但愿它能再多些，不过哪怕这一点点，或可备你不时之需。
> 祝你好运。
> 彼得·尼尔森（Peter Nilssen）

===Original===
Beside the brief, friendly, but none the less final, letter there was a check for five thousand dollars. Helga Crane’s first feeling was one of unreality. This changed almost immediately into one of relief, of liberation. It was stronger than the mere security from present financial worry which the check promised. Money as money was still not very important to Helga. But later, while on an errand in the big general office of the society, her puzzled bewilderment fled. Here the inscrutability of the dozen or more brown faces, all cast from the same indefinite mold, and so like her own, seemed pressing forward against her. Abruptly it flashed upon her that the harrowing irritation of the past weeks was a smoldering hatred. Then, she was overcome by another, so actual, so sharp, so horribly painful, that forever afterwards she preferred to forget it. It was as if she were shut up, boxed up, with hundreds of her race, closed up with that something in the racial character which had always been, to her, inexplicable, alien. Why, she demanded in fierce rebellion, should she be yoked to these despised black folk?
Back in the privacy of her own cubicle, self-loathing came upon her. “They’re my own people, my own people,” she kept repeating over and over to herself. It was no good. The feeling would not be routed. “I can’t go on like this,” she said to herself. “I simply can’t.”
There were footsteps. Panic seized her. She’d have to get out. She terribly needed to. Snatching hat and purse, she hurried to the narrow door, saying in a forced, steady voice, as it opened to reveal her employer: “Mr. Darling, I’m sorry, but I’ve got to go out. Please, may I be excused?”
At his courteous “Certainly, certainly. And don’t hurry. It’s much too hot,” Helga Crane had the grace to feel ashamed, but there was no softening of her determination. The necessity for being alone was too urgent. She hated him and all the others too much.

===Chinese===
在这封简短、客气、却依然带着诀别意味的信旁边，附有一张五千美元的支票。海尔加·克雷恩最初的感觉是不真实。这感觉几乎立刻变成了如释重负，变成了解脱。它比支票所许诺的、免于眼前生计之忧的那点保障还要强烈。钱本身，对海尔加来说仍算不得十分重要。可是过后，她到协会的大总办公室办事时，那困惑的迷惘消散了。在这里，那十几张棕色面孔的莫测高深——全都出自同一个说不清的模子，又都那么像她自己的脸——仿佛一齐向她逼压过来。她猛然醒悟：过去这几个星期那折磨人的烦躁，原来是一股闷烧的仇恨。紧接着，她又被另一种情绪压倒——那么真切、那么尖利、那么痛得可怕，以致此后她宁愿永远把它忘掉。那感觉就像她被关起来、装进匣子，同她那个种族的几百号人锁在一处，同种族性格中那种在她看来始终费解、始终异己的东西锁在一处。为什么，她在狂烈的反抗中诘问，她就该同这些被人瞧不起的黑人拴在一处？
回到自己那间小隔间的清静里，自我憎厌攫住了她。“他们是我自己的人，我自己的人，”她一遍又一遍地向自己重复。没有用。那情绪驱不散。“我不能这样下去了，”她对自己说，“我实在不能了。”
传来了脚步声。恐慌攫住了她。她非出去不可。她太需要独处了。她一把抓起帽子和手包，匆匆走向那扇窄门，门开处露出她的雇主，她用强作镇定的声音说：“达林先生，对不起，我得出去一趟。请您准个假，行吗？”
他客客气气地应道“当然，当然。别急，天实在太热了”，海尔加·克雷恩还不至于不知羞，心里泛起一阵愧意，可她的决心丝毫没有动摇。独处的需要太迫切了。她太恨他，也太恨其余所有的人了。

===Original===
Outside, rain had begun to fall. She walked bareheaded, bitter with self-reproach. But she rejoiced too. She didn’t, in spite of her racial markings, belong to these dark segregated people. She was different. She felt it. It wasn’t merely a matter of color. It was something broader, deeper, that made folk kin.
And now she was free. She would take Uncle Peter’s money and advice and revisit her aunt in Copenhagen. Fleeting pleasant memories of her childhood visit there flew through her excited mind. She had been only eight, yet she had enjoyed the interest and the admiration which her unfamiliar color and dark curly hair, strange to those pink, white, and gold people, had evoked. Quite clearly now she recalled that her Aunt Katrina had begged for her to be allowed to remain. Why, she wondered, hadn’t her mother consented? To Helga it seemed that it would have been the solution to all their problems, her mother’s, her stepfather’s, her own.
At home in the cool dimness of the big chintz-hung living-room, clad only in a fluttering thing of green chiffon, she gave herself up to daydreams of a happy future in Copenhagen, where there were no Negroes, no problems, no prejudice, until she remembered with perturbation that this was the day of Anne’s return from her vacation at the seashore. Worse. There was a dinner-party in her honor that very night. Helga sighed. She’d have to go. She couldn’t possibly get out of a dinner-party for Anne, even though she felt that such an event on a hot night was little short of an outrage. Nothing but a sense of obligation to Anne kept her from pleading a splitting headache as an excuse for remaining quietly at home.
Her mind trailed off to the highly important matter of clothes. What should she wear? White? No, everybody would, because it was hot. Green? She shook her head, Anne would be sure to. The blue thing. Reluctantly she decided against it; she loved it, but she had worn it too often. There was that cobwebby black net touched with orange, which she had bought last spring in a fit of extravagance and never worn, because on getting it home both she and Anne had considered it too décolleté, and too outré. Anne’s words: “There’s not enough of it, and what there is gives you the air of something about to fly,” came back to her, and she smiled as she decided that she would certainly wear the black net. For her it would be a symbol. She was about to fly.

===Chinese===
外面下起了雨。她光着头走在雨里，满心苦涩的自责。可她也欢喜。尽管她身上带着种族的印记，她并不属于这些幽暗的、被隔离的人。她是不同的。她感觉得到。这不单是肤色的事。它更宽、更深，是那使人结为同类的东西。
如今她自由了。她要接受彼得舅父的钱和建议，去哥本哈根重访她的舅妈。童年那次到访的倏忽而逝的愉快记忆，飞过她兴奋的脑海。那时她只有八岁，却已经尝到了那种关注与赞赏的甜头——她那陌生的肤色和乌黑的鬈发，在那群粉白金发的人们眼里那样新奇，惹来了这样的目光。此刻她清清楚楚地记起，当年卡特琳舅妈曾恳求让她留下来。为什么，她纳闷，她母亲没有答应？在海尔加看来，那本可以是他们所有难题的解决办法——她母亲的、她继父的、她自己的。
到了家，在那间挂着印花布帘的大客厅清凉的幽暗里，她只披着一件轻飘飘的绿雪纺绸，纵身沉入对哥本哈根幸福未来的白日梦——那里没有黑人，没有难题，没有偏见——直到她带着一阵不安想起，今天正是安妮海边度假归来的日子。更糟的是，当晚还有一场为安妮接风的晚宴。海尔加叹了口气。她非去不可。安妮的晚宴，她怎么也推不掉，尽管她觉得这样热的夜里办这种宴会简直近乎造孽。若不是对安妮的那份情义，她定要推说头痛欲裂，好安安静静留在家里。
她的心思溜到了衣着这个头等大事上。穿什么好呢？白色？不，天太热，人人都会穿白。绿色？她摇摇头——安妮准会穿绿。那件蓝的？她恋恋不舍地打消了主意；她爱它，可是穿得太勤了。还有那件蛛网似的黑纱，缀着一点橙红，去年春天她一时奢侈买了回来，却从没穿过，因为拿回家后她和安妮都觉得它领口太低、样式太怪。安妮的话：“料子女少，就这么一点，穿上去活像只就要飞走的东西，”回到她耳边，她笑了，决定就穿那件黑纱。对她，这将是一个象征。她正要飞走了。

===Original===
She busied herself with some absurdly expensive roses which she had ordered sent in, spending an interminable time in their arrangement. At last she was satisfied with their appropriateness in some blue Chinese jars of great age. Anne *did* have such lovely things, she thought, as she began conscientiously to prepare for her return, although there was really little to do; Lillie seemed to have done everything. But Helga dusted the tops of the books, placed the magazines in ordered carelessness, redressed Anne’s bed in fresh-smelling sheets of cool linen, and laid out her best pale-yellow pajamas of crêpe de Chine. Finally she set out two tall green glasses and made a great pitcher of lemonade, leaving only the ginger-ale and claret to be added on Anne’s arrival. She was a little conscience-stricken, so she wanted to be particularly nice to Anne, who had been so kind to her when first she came to New York, a forlorn friendless creature. Yes, she was grateful to Anne; but, just the same, she meant to go. At once.
Her preparations over, she went back to the carved chair from which the thought of Anne’s homecoming had drawn her. Characteristically she writhed at the idea of telling Anne of her impending departure and shirked the problem of evolving a plausible and inoffensive excuse for its suddenness. “That,” she decided lazily, “will have to look out for itself; I can’t be bothered just now. It’s too hot.”
She began to make plans and to dream delightful dreams of change, of life somewhere else. Some place where at last she would be permanently satisfied. Her anticipatory thoughts waltzed and eddied about to the sweet silent music of change. With rapture almost, she let herself drop into the blissful sensation of visualizing herself in different, strange places, among approving and admiring people, where she would be appreciated, and understood.

===Chinese===
她忙着摆弄那些贵得离谱的玫瑰——是她叫花店送来的，插花就耗去了不知多少工夫。最后，它们插在几只年头久远的蓝色中国瓷罐里，那相衬的样子总算让她满意了。安妮*确实*有这么些可爱的东西，她想；接着她开始认认真真地为她的归来做准备，尽管其实没什么可做的了：莉莉（Lillie）似乎已把一切都弄妥了。但海尔加还是掸了掸书顶的灰，把杂志摆成看似随意的整齐，给安妮的床换上散发着清新气味的凉爽亚麻床单，又把她那套最好的淡黄色双绉睡衣摊开摆好。最后她摆出两只高脚绿杯，调了一大壶柠檬水，只留姜汁汽水和红酒，等安妮到时再兑进去。她有点于心不安，所以想对安妮格外好些——当初她初来纽约，孤苦伶仃、举目无亲，安妮待她实在不薄。是的，她感激安妮；可尽管如此，她还是决意要走。立刻就走。
准备停当，她回到那把雕花椅子上去——先前正是想到安妮回家，她才从那里起身。她素来如此：一想到要把即将离去的事告诉安妮，她就浑身别扭，索性把“怎样编一个说得过去、又不伤人的理由来解释这突如其来的决定”的难题也躲了过去。“那件事，”她懒懒地打定主意，“只好自己照管自己了；眼下我可顾不上。天太热了。”
她开始盘算起来，做起变迁的美梦、到别处去生活的美梦。一个她终将得到永久满足的地方。期待中的种种念头，随着变迁那甜美无声的音乐回旋、打转。她几乎是心醉神迷地任自己沉入那幸福的幻觉：看见自己置身于一个个不同的、陌生的地方，置身于称许她、赞赏她的人们中间——在那里，她将被珍视，被理解。

## XI / 十一

===Original===
It was night. The dinner-party was over, but no one wanted to go home. Half-past eleven was, it seemed, much too early to tumble into bed on a Saturday night. It was a sulky, humid night, a thick furry night, through which the electric torches shone like silver fuzz﻿—an atrocious night for cabareting, Helga insisted, but the others wanted to go, so she went with them, though half unwillingly. After much consultation and chatter they decided upon a place and climbed into two patiently waiting taxis, rattling things which jerked, wiggled, and groaned, and threatened every minute to collide with others of their kind, or with inattentive pedestrians. Soon they pulled up before a tawdry doorway in a narrow crosstown street and stepped out. The night was far from quiet, the streets far from empty. Clanging trolley bells, quarreling cats, cackling phonographs, raucous laughter, complaining motor-horns, low singing, mingled in the familiar medley that is Harlem. Black figures, white figures, little forms, big forms, small groups, large groups, sauntered, or hurried by. It was gay, grotesque, and a little weird. Helga Crane felt singularly apart from it all. Entering the waiting doorway, they descended through a furtive, narrow passage, into a vast subterranean room. Helga smiled, thinking that this was one of those places characterized by the righteous as a hell.
A glare of light struck her eyes, a blare of jazz split her ears. For a moment everything seemed to be spinning round; even she felt that she was circling aimlessly, as she followed with the others the black giant who led them to a small table, where, when they were seated, their knees and elbows touched. Helga wondered that the waiter, indefinitely carved out of ebony, did not smile as he wrote their order﻿—“four bottles of White Rock, four bottles of ginger-ale.” Bah! Anne giggled, the others smiled and openly exchanged knowing glances, and under the tables flat glass bottles were extracted from the women’s evening scarfs and small silver flasks drawn from the men’s hip pockets. In a little moment she grew accustomed to the smoke and din.
They danced, ambling lazily to a crooning melody, or violently twisting their bodies, like whirling leaves, to a sudden streaming rhythm, or shaking themselves ecstatically to a thumping of unseen tomtoms. For the while, Helga was oblivious of the reek of flesh, smoke, and alcohol, oblivious of the oblivion of other gyrating pairs, oblivious of the color, the noise, and the grand distorted childishness of it all. She was drugged, lifted, sustained, by the extraordinary music, blown out, ripped out, beaten out, by the joyous, wild, murky orchestra. The essence of life seemed bodily motion. And when suddenly the music died, she dragged herself back to the present with a conscious effort; and a shameful certainty that not only had she been in the jungle, but that she had enjoyed it, began to taunt her. She hardened her determination to get away. She wasn’t, she told herself, a jungle creature. She cloaked herself in a faint disgust as she watched the entertainers throw themselves about to the bursts of syncopated jangle, and when the time came again for the patrons to dance, she declined. Her rejected partner excused himself and sought an acquaintance a few tables removed. Helga sat looking curiously about her as the buzz of conversation ceased, strangled by the savage strains of music, and the crowd became a swirling mass. For the hundredth time she marveled at the gradations within this oppressed race of hers. A dozen shades slid by. There was sooty black, shiny black, taupe, mahogany, bronze, copper, gold, orange, yellow, peach, ivory, pinky white, pastry white. There was yellow hair, brown hair, black hair; straight hair, straightened hair, curly hair, crinkly hair, woolly hair. She saw black eyes in white faces, brown eyes in yellow faces, gray eyes in brown faces, blue eyes in tan faces. Africa, Europe, perhaps with a pinch of Asia, in a fantastic motley of ugliness and beauty, semi-barbaric, sophisticated, exotic, were here. But she was blind to its charm, purposely aloof and a little contemptuous, and soon her interest in the moving mosaic waned.

===Chinese===
夜已深。晚宴散了，可是谁也不想回家。星期六的晚上，十一点半，似乎还太早，早得很，哪能就这么钻进被窝。那是个阴沉郁闷的夜，潮湿黏糊的夜，一个毛茸茸的浓稠的夜，电灯的光柱穿射其中，仿佛银色的绒毛——海尔加一口咬定，这种夜跑去逛卡巴莱真是遭罪；可别人都想去，她也就跟着去了，虽然老大不情愿。商量了半天、叽叽喳喳了半天，他们终于选定一家，钻进两辆久候的出租车——那两个哐当作响的玩意儿，一路又是颠、又是扭、又是哼唧，时时刻刻都威胁着要跟同类撞个满怀，或是撞上心不在焉的行人。不多时，车在一条窄小的横街上一扇俗艳的门前停住，他们下了车。夜远远谈不上安静，街上远远谈不上空旷。叮当的有轨电车铃，吵架的猫，咯咯怪笑的留声机，刺耳的哄笑，呜呜抗议的汽车喇叭，低低的歌声，混成哈莱姆人所共知的那一片杂响。黑的身影，白的身影，小的个子，大的个子，一小群一小群的，一大群一大群的，或信步游荡，或匆匆而过。又快活，又怪诞，还有点诡谲。海尔加·克雷恩觉得自己同这一切格外隔膜。跨进那扇候客的门，他们顺一条鬼鬼祟祟的窄道往下走，走进一间巨大的地下室。海尔加笑了，心想：这正是那些正人君子指为地狱的那类地方之一。
一道强光刺进她的眼睛，一通爵士乐震裂她的耳膜。一时间，一切都仿佛在打转；连她自己也觉得正漫无目的地兜着圈子，跟着众人随那个引路的黑人大汉走去，来到一张小桌旁——落座之后，彼此的膝盖和胳膊肘都碰在一处。海尔加纳闷，那个仿佛用乌木胡乱雕出来的侍者，写下他们的点单时竟不露一丝笑意——“四瓶怀特罗克（White Rock）矿泉水，四瓶姜汁汽水”。呸！安妮咯咯地笑，其他人也微笑着，心照不宣地交换眼色；桌子底下，扁扁的玻璃酒瓶从女人们的晚装披巾里抽了出来，小小的银酒壶也从男人们的后裤袋里摸了出来。不大一会儿，她就习惯了这烟雾与喧嚣。
他们跳起舞来：随着低回的旋律懒洋洋地缓步摆荡，或随着陡然奔涌的节奏像打旋的落叶般猛力拧转身躯，或随着听不见的 tom-tom 鼓的咚咚声狂喜地浑身抖动。那一段时间里，海尔加浑然忘了肉体、烟、酒的臭味，忘了其他旋转着的舞侣们的浑然忘我，忘了这一切的色彩、噪声，以及那整幅被放大、被扭曲的孩子气。她被这非凡的音乐麻醉了、托举了、支撑住了，被这支欢腾、狂野、幽暗的乐队吹胀了、撕开了、敲打出来了。生命的本质仿佛就是身体的动作。而当音乐戛然而止，她才费力地把自己拖回眼前；一种可耻的确信开始啮咬她：她不但进过丛林，而且还乐在其中。她把逃开的决心又下狠了几分。她可不是，她对自己说，丛林里的畜生。她裹上一层淡淡的厌恶，看那些卖艺的人应着切分音的铿锵乱响东倒西歪地扭掷着身体；等到又轮到客人下场跳舞时，她婉拒了。被她回绝的舞伴道了声失陪，去找隔着几张桌子的一位熟人。谈话的嗡嗡声被野蛮的乐声扼住、止息，人群重新旋成一团，海尔加坐在那里好奇地打量四周。她第一百次惊叹于她这个受压迫的种族内部的层层色阶。十几种色调从眼前滑过：煤烟黑，油亮黑，灰褐，桃花心木褐，古铜，赤铜，金黄，橙黄，明黄，桃色，象牙色，粉白，糕点白。有黄头发，棕头发，黑头发；直发，拉直的头发，鬈发，波纹发，绒毛般的头发。她看见白脸上的黑眼睛，黄脸上的棕眼睛，棕脸上的灰眼睛，晒黄脸上的蓝眼睛。非洲，欧洲，也许再添一撮亚洲，以丑与美拼成的奇异的斑驳杂色，半开化，又世故，异国情调，全都聚在这里。可她对这魅力视而不见，刻意地冷淡，还带着几分鄙夷，不久，她对这幅流动的马赛克的兴味便淡了。

===Original===
She had discovered Dr. Anderson sitting at a table on the far side of the room, with a girl in a shivering apricot frock. Seriously he returned her tiny bow. She met his eyes, gravely smiling, then blushed, furiously, and averted her own. But they went back immediately to the girl beside him, who sat indifferently sipping a colorless liquid from a high glass, or puffing a precariously hanging cigarette. Across dozens of tables, littered with corks, with ashes, with shriveled sandwiches, through slits in the swaying mob, Helga Crane studied her.
She was pale, with a peculiar, almost deathlike pallor. The brilliantly red, softly curving mouth was somehow sorrowful. Her pitch-black eyes, a little aslant, were veiled by long, drooping lashes and surmounted by broad brows, which seemed like black smears. The short dark hair was brushed severely back from the wide forehead. The extreme décolleté of her simple apricot dress showed a skin of unusual color, a delicate, creamy hue, with golden tones. “Almost like an alabaster,” thought Helga.
Bang! Again the music died. The moving mass broke, separated. The others returned. Anne had rage in her eyes. Her voice trembled as she took Helga aside to whisper: “There’s your Dr. Anderson over there, with Audrey Denney.”

===Chinese===
她发现了安德森博士，坐在屋子那头的一张桌旁，同一个穿着轻薄得发颤的杏色连衣裙的姑娘在一起。他郑重地还了她那一点头。她迎上他的目光，庄重地微笑，随即涨红了脸，红得厉害，忙把眼移开。可那目光又立刻回到他身旁的姑娘身上：她正漫不经心地从一只高脚杯里啜着一种无色的液体，或是喷着一支悬悬欲坠的香烟。隔着几十张堆满瓶塞、烟灰和干瘪三明治的桌子，从摇晃的人堆的缝隙里，海尔加·克雷恩细细打量她。
她脸色苍白，一种奇特的、近乎死灰的苍白。那鲜红丰润、曲线柔和的嘴唇，不知怎么透着哀愁。她那双漆黑的、微微斜挑的眼睛，被低垂的长睫毛掩着，上面横着两道宽眉，仿佛两抹黑痕。乌黑的短发一丝不苟地从宽阔的额头向后梳去。那件素净的杏色连衣裙领口开得极低，露出一种颜色少见的皮肤，一种细腻的奶油色泽，泛着金调。“简直像雪花石膏。”海尔加想。
砰！音乐又断了。流动的人群裂开、散开。同伴们回来了。安妮眼里含着怒火。她把海尔加拉到一边，声音发颤地低声说：“你那位安德森博士在那边呢，跟奥德丽·德纳姆（Audrey Denney）在一起。”
