男人厚实的上唇颤动了。他抹了抹前额，那里的金发如今平平地贴着，苍白，没有光泽。他的眼睛仍旧避开面前高背椅里的姑娘。海尔加感到一阵悔意的震颤。有一瞬她后悔自己没有稍稍和气些。可是，残忍到底不正是最大的仁慈吗？然而她的语气温柔了些，冷淡少了些：“你要明白，我嫁不了一个白人。就是嫁不了。不光是因你这个人，不关私人恩怨，你懂的。这比那更深，更广。这是种族的事。也许有朝一日你会庆幸。谁也说不准，你知道；倘若我们结了婚，你也许到头来会以我为耻，会恨我，会恨一切深色皮肤的人。我母亲就是那样做的。”
“我向你求婚，海尔加·克雷恩，你却拿什么种族呀耻辱呀的怪话来回答我。这算什么胡说？”
海尔加没有接这个话茬，因为她觉得，没法解释。那太难，也太伤自尊。她找不出任何字眼，能恰如其分地、又不剐伤她的骄傲地，向他传达那一个个他们一失足便可能跌进去的陷阱。“我也许，”她说，“当年考虑过——我刚来的时候。可你呢，指望的是一桩更不拘形数的安排，等得太久了。你错过了那个时刻。我有了工夫思索。如今我不可能了。什么都值不得冒这个险。我们到头来会彼此仇恨的。这一套我经历过了，或是类似的情形。我知道。我做不出来。而且我庆幸。”
她站起身，伸出手去，暗暗庆幸他依旧沉默。“日安，”她规规矩矩地说。“不胜荣幸——”

===Original===
“A tragedy,” he corrected, barely touching her hand with his moist fingertips.
“Why?” Helga countered, and for an instant felt as if something sinister and internecine flew back and forth between them like poison.
“I mean,” he said, and quite solemnly, “that though I don’t entirely understand you, yet in a way I do too. And﻿—” He hesitated. Went on. “I think that my picture of you is, after all, the true Helga Crane. Therefore﻿—a tragedy. For someone. For me? Perhaps.”
“Oh, the picture!” Helga lifted her shoulders in a little impatient motion.
Ceremoniously Axel Olsen bowed himself out, leaving her grateful for the urbanity which permitted them to part without too much awkwardness. No other man, she thought, of her acquaintance could have managed it so well﻿—except, perhaps, Robert Anderson.

===Chinese===
“一桩悲剧，”他纠正道，湿漉漉的指尖只堪堪碰了碰她的手。
“为什么？”海尔加反问，有一瞬，她仿佛觉得某种阴毒的、两败俱伤的东西，像毒液一样在两人之间飞来飞去。
“我的意思是，”他说，而且说得相当郑重，“虽然我并不完全了解你，可从某种意义上说，我也了解。还有——”他踌躇了一下。又接下去。“我认为，我画的那幅你，毕竟才是真正的海尔加·克雷恩。所以——是一桩悲剧。对某个人。对我？也许。”
“哦，那幅画！”海尔加不耐烦地轻轻耸了耸肩。
阿克塞尔·奥尔森彬彬有礼地鞠躬告退了，丢下她独自感激这番温文，容得他们不太难堪地道了别。她认识的男人里，她想，没有谁能把这一幕应付得这样漂亮——也许，除了罗伯特·安德森。

===Original===
“I’m glad,” she declared to herself in another moment, “that I refused him. And,” she added honestly, “I’m glad that I had the chance. He took it awfully well, though﻿—for a tragedy.” And she made a tiny frown.
The picture﻿—she had never quite, in spite of her deep interest in him, and her desire for his admiration and approval, forgiven Olsen for that portrait. It wasn’t, she contended, herself at all, but some disgusting sensual creature with her features. Herr and Fru Dahl had not exactly liked it either, although collectors, artists, and critics had been unanimous in their praise and it had been hung on the line at an annual exhibition, where it had attracted much flattering attention and many tempting offers.
Now Helga went in and stood for a long time before it, with its creator’s parting words in mind: “… a tragedy﻿ ﻿… my picture is, after all, the true Helga Crane.” Vehemently she shook her head. “It isn’t, it isn’t at all,” she said aloud. Bosh! Pure artistic bosh and conceit. Nothing else. Anyone with half an eye could see that it wasn’t, at all, like her.
“Marie,” she called to the maid passing in the hall, “do you think this is a good picture of me?”

===Chinese===
“我高兴，”过了一会儿她对自己宣称，“我拒绝了他。而且，”她诚实地补了一句，“我也高兴有过这个机会。不过他承受得实在漂亮——对一桩悲剧来说。”她微微皱了一下眉。
那幅画——尽管她对他兴味深长，渴望他的赞赏与认可，可她始终没有全然原谅奥尔森画的那幅肖像。那根本不是她，她一口咬定，而是某个顶着她的五官、令人作呕的肉感造物。达尔先生和达尔太太也谈不上喜欢它，虽说收藏家、艺术家和批评家们众口一词地赞美，它还在一年一度的画展上挂在了正线，招来了许多谀辞和许多诱人的出价。
这时海尔加走进去，在它面前站了许久，脑子里转着它的创作者临别的话：“……一桩悲剧……我那幅画，毕竟才是真正的海尔加·克雷恩。”她激烈地摇头。“它不是，根本不是，”她大声说。胡扯！地道的艺术家的胡扯和自负。除此之外什么都不是。随便哪个长了半只眼睛的人都看得出，它一点儿也不像她。
“玛丽，”她招呼走过厅堂的女仆，“你觉得这张我的画像画得好吗？”

===Original===
Marie blushed. Hesitated. “Of course, Frøken, I know Herr Olsen is a great artist, but no, I don’t like that picture. It looks bad, wicked. Begging your pardon, Frøken.”
“Thanks, Marie, I don’t like it either.”
Yes, anyone with half an eye could see that it wasn’t she.

===Chinese===
玛丽红了脸。犹豫了。“当然，小姐（Frøken），我知道奥尔森先生是位大画家，可是不，我不喜欢那张画。它看上去凶，邪性。恕我直言，小姐。”
“谢谢，玛丽，我也不喜欢它。”
是啊，随便哪个长了半只眼睛的人都看得出，那不是她。

## XVI / 十六

===Original===
Glad though the Dahls may have been that their niece had had the chance of refusing the hand of Axel Olsen, they were anything but glad that she had taken that chance. Very plainly they said so, and quite firmly they pointed out to her the advisability of retrieving the opportunity, if, indeed, such a thing were possible. But it wasn’t, even had Helga been so inclined, for, they were to learn from the columns of *Politikken*, Axel Olsen had gone off suddenly to some queer place in the Balkans. To rest, the newspapers said. To get Frøken Crane out of his mind, the gossips said.
Life in the Dahl ménage went on, smoothly as before, but not so pleasantly. The combined disappointment and sense of guilt of the Dahls and Helga colored everything. Though she had resolved not to think that they felt that she had, as it were, “let them down,” Helga knew that they did. They had not so much expected as hoped that she would bring down Olsen, and so secure the link between the merely fashionable set to which they belonged and the artistic one after which they hankered. It was of course true that there were others, plenty of them. But there was only one Olsen. And Helga, for some idiotic reason connected with race, had refused him. Certainly there was no use in thinking, even, of the others. If she had refused him, she would refuse any and all for the same reason. It was, it seemed, all-embracing.
“It isn’t,” Uncle Poul had tried to point out to her, “as if there were hundreds of mulattoes here. That, I can understand, might make it a little different. But there’s only you. You’re unique here, don’t you see? Besides, Olsen has money and enviable position. Nobody’d dare to say, or even to think anything odd or unkind of you or him. Come now, Helga, it isn’t this foolishness about race. Not here in Denmark. You’ve never spoken of it before. It can’t be just that. You’re too sensible. It must be something else. I wish you’d try to explain. You don’t perhaps like Olsen?”
Helga had been silent, thinking what a severe wrench to Herr Dahl’s ideas of decency was this conversation. For he had an almost fanatic regard for reticence, and a peculiar shrinking from what he looked upon as indecent exposure of the emotions.
“Just what is it, Helga?” he asked again, because the pause had grown awkward, for him.
“I can’t explain any better than I have,” she had begun tremulously, “it’s just something﻿—something deep down inside of me,” and had turned away to hide a face convulsed by threatening tears.
===Chinese===
尽管达尔一家或许曾为他们外甥女有机会回绝阿克塞尔·奥尔森的求婚而感到高兴，可对于她竟然把握住了这个机会，他们却丝毫也高兴不起来。他们把话明白地说了出来，还相当坚定地向她指出，倘若此事当真还有挽回的可能，那么把这门亲事重新拾起来才是明智之举。然而这是不可能的了，即使海尔加有意回头也不可能，因为他们从《政治报》（*Politikken*）的专栏里得知，阿克塞尔·奥尔森已突然动身，到巴尔干某个古怪的地方去了。报纸说，是去休养。爱传闲话的人说，是为了把克雷恩小姐（Frøken Crane）从心头忘掉。
达尔家的日子照旧平顺地过下去，却不再那么愉快。达尔一家与海尔加各自怀着失望与内疚，给一切都染上了颜色。虽然海尔加已下定决心不去想他们是否觉得她“让他们失望了”，但她心里明白，他们确实这么想。他们与其说是预期，不如说是盼望她能把奥尔森拿下来，从而把他们所属的那班仅仅时髦的人物，与他们一心向往的艺术圈子联结起来。当然，别的人也有的是。可奥尔森只有一个。而海尔加，竟为了某个与种族有关的荒唐理由拒绝了他。显然，再想别的什么人也是白想。既然拒绝了他，出于同一个理由，她就会拒绝任何一个、拒绝所有人。那理由，看来是无所不包的。
“这可不是，”波尔舅舅曾试图向她指出，“仿佛这儿有成百上千个黑白混血儿似的。那样的话，我可以理解，事情或许会有些不同。可这儿只有你一个。你在这儿是独一无二的，你不明白吗？再说，奥尔森有钱，又有令人羡慕的地位。没有人敢说你或他半句怪话，甚至连那样的念头都不敢有。好了，海尔加，这不该是那种关于种族的蠢念头。在丹麦可没有这一套。你以前从来没说起过这个。总不会单单是为了这个吧。你太明白事理了。一定另有缘故。我倒希望你试着解释解释。莫非你不喜欢奥尔森？”
海尔加一直沉默着，心想这番谈话对达尔先生的体面观念是何等严重的折磨。因为他对含蓄缄默几乎怀着虔诚的敬重，而对他视为情感之不体面暴露的东西，则有一种特殊的退避。
“到底是为了什么，海尔加？”他又问了一遍，因为沉默延续下去，已使他觉得尴尬了。
“我没法解释得比已经说的更明白，”她颤声开口道，“那是一种东西——一种深埋在我心底的东西。”说完便转过身去，掩住一张被欲坠的泪水搅得抽动起来的脸。

===Original===
But that, Uncle Poul had remarked with a reasonableness that was wasted on the miserable girl before him, was nonsense, pure nonsense.
With a shaking sigh and a frantic dab at her eyes, in which had come a despairing look, she had agreed that perhaps it was foolish, but she couldn’t help it. “Can’t you, won’t you understand, Uncle Poul?” she begged, with a pleading look at the kindly worldly man who at that moment had been thinking that this strange exotic niece of his wife’s was indeed charming. He didn’t blame Olsen for taking it rather hard.
The thought passed. She was weeping. With no effort at restraint. Charming, yes. But insufficiently civilized. Impulsive. Imprudent. Selfish.
“Try, Helga, to control yourself,” he had urged gently. He detested tears. “If it distresses you so, we won’t talk of it again. You, of course, must do as you yourself wish. Both your aunt and I want only that you should be happy.” He had wanted to make an end of this fruitless wet conversation.
Helga had made another little dab at her face with the scrap of lace and raised shining eyes to his face. She had said, with sincere regret: “You’ve been marvelous to me, you and Aunt Katrina. Angelic. I don’t want to seem ungrateful. I’d do anything for you, anything in the world but this.”
===Chinese===
可是，波尔舅舅用一种全然浪费在眼前这个可怜姑娘身上的通情达理评论道，那全是胡说，纯粹的胡说。
她颤颤地叹了口气，慌乱地在眼睛上按了按——眼里已蒙上一层绝望的神色——承认这也许很愚蠢，可她克制不了。“您就不能、也不肯体谅我吗，波尔舅舅？”她恳求道，哀告似的望着这位和气而圆滑世故的男人；而就在那一刻，他心里正想着，他妻子的这个奇特的、异国情调的外甥女的确迷人。他不怪奥尔森为这事这般想不开。
这个念头一闪而过。她在哭。毫不克制地哭。迷人，不错。可教化得不够。冲动。莽撞。自私。
“试着克制一下自己，海尔加。”他温和地劝她。他厌恶眼泪。“如果这事让你这样难过，我们就不再谈它了。当然，你必须照自己的意愿去做。你舅妈和我只希望你幸福。”他想赶紧了结这场毫无结果、泪水涟涟的谈话。
海尔加又拿那一小片花边轻轻按了按脸，抬起泪光闪闪的眼睛望着他的脸。她带着真诚的歉意说：“你们对我太好了，您和卡特琳舅妈（Katrina）。简直像天使。我不想显得忘恩负义。我愿意为你们做任何事，世上任何事，唯独这件不行。”

===Original===
Herr Dahl had shrugged. A little sardonically he had smiled. He had refrained from pointing out that this was the only thing she could do for them, the only thing that they had asked of her. He had been too glad to be through with the uncomfortable discussion.
So life went on. Dinners, coffees, theaters, pictures, music, clothes. More dinners, coffees, theaters, clothes, music. And that nagging aching for America increased. Augmented by the uncomfortableness of Aunt Katrina’s and Uncle Poul’s disappointment with her, that tormenting nostalgia grew to an unbearable weight. As spring came on with many gracious tokens of following summer, she found her thoughts straying with increasing frequency to Anne’s letter and to Harlem, its dirty streets, swollen now, in the warmer weather, with dark, gay humanity.
Until recently she had had no faintest wish ever to see America again. Now she began to welcome the thought of a return. Only a visit, of course. Just to see, to prove to herself that there was nothing there for her. To demonstrate the absurdity of even thinking that there could be. And to relieve the slight tension here. Maybe when she came back﻿—
Her definite decision to go was arrived at with almost bewildering suddenness. It was after a concert at which Dvořák’s *New World Symphony* had been wonderfully rendered. Those wailing undertones of “Swing Low, Sweet Chariot” were too poignantly familiar. They struck into her longing heart and cut away her weakening defenses. She knew at least what it was that had lurked formless and undesignated these many weeks in the back of her troubled mind. Incompleteness.
“I’m homesick, not for America, but for Negroes. That’s the trouble.”
===Chinese===
达尔先生耸了耸肩，颇为讥诮地笑了笑。他忍住没有指出：这是她唯一能替他们做的事，是他们唯一求过她的一件事。他只巴望这场不痛快的谈论就此结束。
日子就这样过下去。宴会、咖啡、剧院、电影、音乐、衣裳。更多的宴会、咖啡、剧院、衣裳、音乐。而对美国那种啮心的渴念日益增长。再加上卡特琳舅妈和波尔舅舅对她失望所造成的不自在，那折磨人的乡愁便膨胀成一副难以承受的重担。当春天带着随之而来的夏日的一连串温婉讯息临近时，她发觉自己的思绪越发频繁地飘向安妮的来信，飘向哈莱姆——它那些肮脏的街道，在转暖的天气里，正涨满着黑黑的、快活的人群。
直到不久以前，她连再见到美国一面的最微弱的念头都不曾有过。如今她开始乐于想起回去的事。当然只是小住一趟。去看看，向自己证明那里并没有她的什么。证明就连认为那里可能有她的什么，也是荒唐。也好缓解此地的一点紧张。也许等她回来——
她最终动身的决定下得近乎令人迷惑地突兀。那是在一场音乐会之后，会上德沃夏克（Dvořák）的《自新大陆》交响曲（*New World Symphony*）演奏得精妙绝伦。《低吟缓行，甜蜜灵车》（“Swing Low, Sweet Chariot”）那呜咽的低音实在刻骨地熟悉。它们刺入她渴望的心，扫荡着她节节溃败的防线。她至少明白了，这几个星期以来潜藏在她烦乱心底那个无形无名的东西究竟是什么。是残缺。
“我是在想家，不是想美国，是想黑人。症结就在这里。”

===Original===
For the first time Helga Crane felt sympathy rather than contempt and hatred for that father, who so often and so angrily she had blamed for his desertion of her mother. She understood, now, his rejection, his repudiation, of the formal calm her mother had represented. She understood his yearning, his intolerable need for the inexhaustible humor and the incessant hope of his own kind, his need for those things, not material, indigenous to all Negro environments. She understood and could sympathize with his facile surrender to the irresistible ties of race, now that they dragged at her own heart. And as she attended parties, the theater, the opera, and mingled with people on the streets, meeting only pale serious faces when she longed for brown laughing ones, she was able to forgive him. Also, it was as if in this understanding and forgiving she had come upon knowledge of almost sacred importance.
Without demur, opposition, or recrimination Herr and Fru Dahl accepted Helga’s decision to go back to America. She had expected that they would be glad and relieved. It was agreeable to discover that she had done them less than justice. They were, in spite of their extreme worldliness, very fond of her, and would, as they declared, miss her greatly. And they did want her to come back to them, as they repeatedly insisted. Secretly they felt as she did, that perhaps when she returned﻿—So it was agreed upon that it was only for a brief visit, “for your friend’s wedding,” and that she was to return in the early fall.
The last day came. The last goodbyes were said. Helga began to regret that she was leaving. Why couldn’t she have two lives, or why couldn’t she be satisfied in one place? Now that she was actually off, she felt heavy at heart. Already she looked back with infinite regret at the two years in the country which had given her so much, of pride, of happiness, of wealth, and of beauty.
Bells rang. The gangplank was hoisted. The dark strip of water widened. The running figures of friends suddenly grown very dear grew smaller, blurred into a whole, and vanished. Tears rose in Helga Crane’s eyes, fear in her heart.
Goodbye Denmark! Goodbye. Goodbye!
===Chinese===
海尔加·克雷恩生平头一次对那位父亲感到了同情，而非轻蔑与憎恨——她曾那样频繁、那样愤怒地责怪他遗弃了她的母亲。如今她明白了，他为什么拒斥、背弃她母亲所代表的那种拘谨的平静。她明白他的渴望，明白他对同族人那无穷的幽默与不息的希望之难以忍受的需要，明白他需要那些东西——那些非物质的、为一切黑人环境所固有的东西。她明白，也能够体谅他那轻易的屈服——屈服于种族间不可抗拒的纽带，因为这纽带如今正揪紧她自己的心。而当她出席宴会、看戏、听歌剧，在街上混迹于人群之中，渴望着棕色欢笑的面孔、遇上的却只有苍白严肃的脸时，她能够原谅他了。而且，仿佛就在这理解与宽恕之中，她获得了一种近乎神圣的重要认识。
达尔先生和达尔太太（Fru Dahl）毫无推托、毫无异议、也毫无责难地接受了海尔加回美国去的决定。她原以为他们会如释重负、暗自高兴。发现自己的确低估了他们，这倒令人愉快。他们虽然极其世故，却真心疼爱她，并且如他们自己所说，会非常想念她。他们也确实愿意她回到他们身边，这一点他们一再坚持。他们心里也和她一样，觉得也许等她回来之后——于是大家讲定，这只是一次短期探访，“来参加你朋友的婚礼”，她将于初秋返回。
最后一天到来了。最后的道别说过了。海尔加开始后悔此行。为什么她不能有两种人生，或者，为什么她竟不能在一个地方安于满足？如今真的要走了，她心头沉重。她已经带着无限的遗憾回望在这个国度度过的两年——它给了她那么多东西：骄傲、幸福、财富，还有美。
钟声响了。舷梯吊了起来。那道深色的水带渐渐拓宽。朋友们奔跑的身影——忽然间变得无比亲切的身影——越来越小，模糊成一片，终于消失。泪水涌上海尔加·克雷恩的眼眶，恐惧涌进她的心里。
再见，丹麦！再见。再见！

## XVII / 十七

===Original===
A summer had ripened and fall begun. Anne and Dr. Anderson had returned from their short Canadian wedding journey. Helga Crane, lingering still in America, had tactfully removed herself from the house in One Hundred and Thirty-ninth Street to a hotel. It was, as she could point out to curious acquaintances, much better for the newly-married Andersons not to be bothered with a guest, not even with such a close friend as she, Helga, had been to Anne.
Actually, though she herself had truly wanted to get out of the house when they came back, she had been a little surprised and a great deal hurt that Anne had consented so readily to her going. She might at least, thought Helga indignantly, have acted a little bit as if she had wanted her to stay. After writing for her to come, too.
Pleasantly unaware was Helga that Anne, more silently wise than herself, more determined, more selfish, and less inclined to leave anything to chance, understood perfectly that in a large measure it was the voice of Robert Anderson’s inexorable conscience that had been the chief factor in bringing about her second marriage﻿—his ascetic protest against the sensuous, the physical. Anne had perceived that the decorous surface of her new husband’s mind regarded Helga Crane with that intellectual and aesthetic appreciation which attractive and intelligent women would always draw from him, but that underneath that well-managed section, in a more lawless place where she herself never hoped or desired to enter, was another, a vagrant primitive groping toward something shocking and frightening to the cold asceticism of his reason. Anne knew also that though she herself was lovely﻿—more beautiful than Helga﻿—and interesting, with her he had not to struggle against that nameless and to him shameful impulse, that sheer delight, which ran through his nerves at mere proximity to Helga. And Anne intended that her marriage should be a success. She intended that her husband should be happy. She was sure that it could be managed by tact and a little cleverness on her own part. She was truly fond of Helga, but seeing how she had grown more charming, more aware of her power, Anne wasn’t so sure that her sincere and urgent request to come over for her wedding hadn’t been a mistake. She was, however, certain of herself. She could look out for her husband. She could carry out what she considered her obligation to him, keep him undisturbed, unhumiliated. It was impossible that she could fail. Unthinkable.
Helga, on her part, had been glad to get back to New York. How glad, or why, she did not truly realize. And though she sincerely meant to keep her promise to Aunt Katrina and Uncle Poul and return to Copenhagen, summer, September, October, slid by and she made no move to go. Her uttermost intention had been a six or eight weeks’ visit, but the feverish rush of New York, the comic tragedy of Harlem, still held her. As time went on, she became a little bored, a little restless, but she stayed on. Something of that wild surge of gladness that had swept her on the day when with Anne and Anderson she had again found herself surrounded by hundreds, thousands, of dark-eyed brown folk remained with her. *These* were her people. Nothing, she had come to understand now, could ever change that. Strange that she had never truly valued this kinship until distance had shown her its worth. How absurd she had been to think that another country, other people, could liberate her from the ties which bound her forever to these mysterious, these terrible, these fascinating, these lovable, dark hordes. Ties that were of the spirit. Ties not only superficially entangled with mere outline of features or color of skin. Deeper. Much deeper than either of these.
Thankful for the appeasement of that loneliness which had again tormented her like a fury, she gave herself up to the miraculous joyousness of Harlem. The easement which its heedless abandon brought to her was a real, a very definite thing. She liked the sharp contrast to her pretentious stately life in Copenhagen. It was as if she had passed from the heavy solemnity of a church service to a gorgeous carefree revel.

===Chinese===
一个夏天熟透了，秋天开始了。安妮和安德森博士已从他们短暂的加拿大新婚旅行中归来。海尔加·克雷恩仍滞留美国，已经知趣地把自己从第一百三十九街那栋宅子里挪了出去，搬进一家旅馆。她可以对好奇的熟人们这样解释：新婚的安德森夫妇还是别让客人搅扰为好，哪怕这客人曾像她海尔加同安妮那样亲近。
其实，虽然她自己当真想在两人回来时搬出那栋房子，可安妮竟这样爽快地答应她走，她还是有点意外，更多的是受伤。她起码，海尔加愤愤地想，可以稍微装出一点想留她的样子。何况还是她写了信叫她回来的呢。
海尔加全然不知、也乐得不知的是：安妮——她比自己更深沉地聪明，更坚决，更自私，也更不肯把任何事情交给运气——心里明镜似的，促成她这第二度婚姻的头号因素，很大程度上是罗伯特·安德森那不容置辩的良心的声音——那是他的禁欲主义对肉感、对肉身的一声抗议。安妮看得分明：她新婚丈夫的心智，在那层得体的表层上，是把海尔加·克雷恩当作知性的、审美的欣赏对象来看的——有魅力又有头脑的女人，向来总能从他那里博得这份欣赏；可是在那一层管理得井井有条的区块底下，在一个她自己从不指望、也无意踏入的更无法无天的地方，还蜷着另一个他，一个漂泊的原始之物，正朝某样东西摸索过去——那东西足以叫他理性的冷酷禁欲主义又惊又惧。安妮还知道，虽然她本人可爱——比海尔加更美——而且有趣，可同她在一起，他不必苦苦克制那股无以名状的、令他羞耻的冲动，那股纯粹的欢悦；只要一靠近海尔加，那股电流就会窜遍他的神经。而安妮打定主意，她的婚姻要成功。她打定主意，她的丈夫要幸福。她确信，只消她自己的圆滑加上一点小聪明，这事尽可安排妥当。她真心喜欢海尔加；可是眼看她出落得越发迷人、越发明白自己的力量，安妮不免犯嘀咕：自己那封情辞恳切的信，急急唤她回来参加婚礼，会不会是个失着。不过，她对自己是有把握的。她能看住自己的丈夫。她能履行她认为自己对他负有的义务，让他不受搅扰，不受羞辱。她不可能失败。不可想象。
海尔加呢，她那一面，回到纽约是高兴的。有多高兴，为什么高兴，她并没有真正意识到。虽然她诚心打算遵守对卡特琳舅妈和波尔舅舅的诺言，回哥本哈根去，可夏天、九月、十月，一晃就滑了过去，她毫无动身的表示。她的最大极限，本是住上六个或八个星期，可纽约那狂热的奔忙，哈莱姆那悲喜交织的活剧，还攥着她不放。随着时日推移，她有点腻了，有点烦了，可她还赖着不走。那一天——她同安妮和安德森在一起，重新发觉自己被成百上千的黑眼睛棕色人群包围——席卷过她的那阵狂野的欢喜，还剩下一些留在她身上。*这些*才是她的人民。如今她终于懂得，什么也改变不了这一点。奇怪的是，直到距离向她显示出这份亲缘的分量，她才真正掂出它的价值。她从前多么荒唐，竟以为另一个国度、另一群人，能把她从那些束缚中解放出来——它们把她永远缚在这些神秘的、可怖的、迷人的、可爱的黑色人群上。那是精神上的束缚。不只是同五官轮廓或皮肤颜色这些表面缠绕在一起的束缚。更深。比这两样都深得多。
那种孤单又曾像恶魔似的折磨她，如今得了平息，她心存感激，便纵情把自己交给哈莱姆那种奇迹般的欢腾。它那不管不顾的放纵带给她的舒解，是一种真实的、非常确凿的东西。她喜欢这同她在哥本哈根那矜贵庄严的生活的强烈对照。就像从教堂礼拜的沉重肃穆，一步跨进了一场绚烂忘忧的狂欢。

===Original===
Not that she intended to remain. No. Helga Crane couldn’t, she told herself and others, live in America. In spite of its glamour, existence in America, even in Harlem, was for Negroes too cramped, too uncertain, too cruel; something not to be endured for a lifetime if one could escape; something demanding a courage greater than was in her. No. She couldn’t stay. Nor, she saw now, could she remain away. Leaving, she would have to come back.
This knowledge, this certainty of the division of her life into two parts in two lands, into physical freedom in Europe and spiritual freedom in America, was unfortunate, inconvenient, expensive. It was, too, as she was uncomfortably aware, even a trifle ridiculous, and mentally she caricatured herself moving shuttle-like from continent to continent. From the prejudiced restrictions of the New World to the easy formality of the Old, from the pale calm of Copenhagen to the colorful lure of Harlem.
Nevertheless she felt a slightly pitying superiority over those Negroes who were apparently so satisfied. And she had a fine contempt for the blatantly patriotic black Americans. Always when she encountered one of those picturesque parades in the Harlem streets, the Stars and Stripes streaming ironically, insolently, at the head of the procession tempered for her, a little, her amusement at the childish seriousness of the spectacle. It was too pathetic.
But when mental doors were deliberately shut on those skeletons that stalked lively and in full health through the consciousness of every person of Negro ancestry in America﻿—conspicuous black, obvious brown, or indistinguishable white﻿—life was intensely amusing, interesting, absorbing, and enjoyable; singularly lacking in that tone of anxiety which the insecurities of existence seemed to ferment in other peoples.
Yet Helga herself had an acute feeling of insecurity, for which she could not account. Sometimes it amounted to fright almost. “I must,” she would say then, “get back to Copenhagen.” But the resolution gave her not much pleasure. And for this she now blamed Axel Olsen. It was, she insisted, he who had driven her back, made her unhappy in Denmark. Though she knew well that it wasn’t. Misgivings, too, rose in her. Why hadn’t she married him? Anne was married﻿—she would not say Anderson﻿—Why not she? It would serve Anne right if she married a white man. But she knew in her soul that she wouldn’t. “Because I’m a fool,” she said bitterly.

===Chinese===
倒不是她打算留下。不。海尔加·克雷恩没法，她对自己也对别人说，在美国生活。尽管它光华夺目，在美国的日子——哪怕在哈莱姆——对黑人来说也太局促、太无常、太残酷；那是能逃就该逃、不该忍受一辈子的东西；是要求一种比她身上更大的勇气的东西。不。她留不下来。可她如今也明白，她也走不脱。走了，还得回来。
这份知晓，这份确信——她的生命将分成两半、落在两片土地上，分成欧洲的肉体自由和美洲的精神自由——是不幸的，不便的，破费的。而且，正如她不安地意识到的，甚至还有点可笑；她在心里给自己画了幅漫画：像梭子一样在两大洲之间穿来穿去。从新世界偏见重重的束缚，到旧世界闲适的礼数；从哥本哈根苍白的宁静，到哈莱姆斑斓的诱惑。
尽管如此，对那些显然如此心满意足的黑人，她怀着一点怜悯的优越感。而对那些公然爱国的美国黑人，她更有一份漂亮的蔑视。每当她在哈莱姆街头撞见那一类花花绿绿的游行，星条旗讽刺地、盛气凌人地飘在队伍前头，她观看那场面孩子气的煞有介事时的兴味，总要被冲淡几分。实在太可悲了。
不过，只要把心智的大门故意朝那些骷髅关上——它们个个筋骨健旺、大摇大摆地游荡在每一个身负黑人血统的美国人意识里，无论是显眼的黑人、一望而知的棕色人，还是无从分辨的白皮肤——生活便有滋有味，有趣，迷人，惬意；独独缺了那种焦虑的调子——生活的朝不保夕，似乎在别的人群里总要酿出这东西。
然而海尔加自己却有一种尖锐的不安全感，她说不出个所以然来。有时几乎算得上惊惧了。“我必须，”每到这时她便说，“回哥本哈根去。”可这个决心没给她多少快乐。而这一点，她如今怪到阿克塞尔·奥尔森头上。她一口咬定，是他把她赶回来的，是他害她在丹麦不快活。虽然她心里明白并非如此。疑虑也在她心里升起。她怎么就没有嫁给他？安妮嫁了——她不肯说安德森——她怎么就不嫁？要是她嫁个白人，正好气气安妮。可她在灵魂深处知道她不会。“因为我是个傻瓜，”她苦涩地说。

## XVIII / 十八

===Original===
One November evening, impregnated still with the kindly warmth of the dead Indian summer, Helga Crane was leisurely dressing in pleasant anticipation of the party to which she had been asked for that night. It was always amusing at the Tavenors’. Their house was large and comfortable, the food and music always of the best, and the type of entertainment always unexpected and brilliant. The drinks, too, were sure to be safe.
And Helga, since her return, was more than ever popular at parties. Her courageous clothes attracted attention, and her deliberate lure﻿—as Olsen had called it﻿—held it. Her life in Copenhagen had taught her to expect and accept admiration as her due. This attitude, she found, was as effective in New York as across the sea. It was, in fact, even more so. And it was more amusing too. Perhaps because it was somehow a bit more dangerous.
In the midst of curious speculation as to the possible identity of the other guests, with an indefinite sense of annoyance she wondered if Anne would be there. There was of late something about Anne that was to Helga distinctly disagreeable, a peculiar half-patronizing attitude, mixed faintly with distrust. Helga couldn’t define it, couldn’t account for it. She had tried. In the end she had decided to dismiss it, to ignore it.
“I suppose,” she said aloud, “it’s because she’s married again. As if anybody couldn’t get married. Anybody. That is, if mere marriage is all one wants.”
Smoothing away the tiny frown from between the broad black brows, she got herself into a little shining, rose-colored slip of a frock knotted with a silver cord. The gratifying result soothed her ruffled feelings. It didn’t really matter, this new manner of Anne’s. Nor did the fact that Helga knew that Anne disapproved of her. Without words Anne had managed to make that evident. In her opinion, Helga had lived too long among the enemy, the detestable pale faces. She understood them too well, was too tolerant of their ignorant stupidities. If they had been Latins, Anne might conceivably have forgiven the disloyalty. But Nordics! Lynchers! It was too traitorous. Helga smiled a little, understanding Anne’s bitterness and hate, and a little of its cause. It was of a piece with that of those she so virulently hated. Fear. And then she sighed a little, for she regretted the waning of Anne’s friendship. But, in view of diverging courses of their lives, she felt that even its complete extinction would leave her undevastated. Not that she wasn’t still grateful to Anne for many things. It was only that she had other things now. And there would, forever, be Robert Anderson between them. A nuisance. Shutting them off from their previous confident companionship and understanding. “And anyway,” she said again, aloud, “he’s nobody much to have married. Anybody could have married him. Anybody. If a person wanted only to be married﻿—If it had been somebody like Olsen﻿—That would be different﻿—something to crow over, perhaps.”

===Chinese===
十一月的一个傍晚，空气中仍浸透着已逝的印第安夏和煦的暖意，海尔加·克雷恩正从容地更着衣，愉快地期待着今晚受邀参加的宴会。在塔文诺家（Tavenors）总是让人开心的。他们的宅子宽大舒适，饭菜和音乐永远是一流的，余兴节目的花样又总是出人意料、异彩纷呈。酒水呢，也必定靠得住。
而海尔加自回国以后，在宴会上比以往任何时候都更受欢迎。她那大胆的衣着吸引着人们的目光，而她那有意施展的诱惑力——奥尔森曾这样称呼它——又把目光牢牢留住。哥本哈根的生活教会了她把倾慕当作自己应得的东西去期待、去领受。她发觉，这种态度在纽约和在大洋彼岸同样灵验。事实上，还更加灵验。也更有趣些。也许因为它多少更带一点危险。
正当她饶有兴味地揣测其他宾客可能都是些什么人时，她怀着一种莫名的恼意，寻思安妮会不会到场。近来安妮身上有某种东西让海尔加分明觉得不快，一种半带屈尊俯就的怪异态度，还淡淡掺着一丝不信任。海尔加说不清那是什么，也道不明它的来由。她试过。到头来她决定不去理会它，对它置之不理。
“我猜，”她出声说道，“是因为她又结婚了。好像有谁结不成婚似的。谁都结得成。当然，这是说，倘若一个人图的仅仅是结婚本身。”
她抚平宽阔的黑眉间那一丝小小的蹙痕，把身子套进一条闪着微光的玫瑰色小吊带裙里，裙腰上系着一根银色细绳。这令人满意的效果抚平了她被搅乱的情绪。安妮这种新的做派实在算不了什么。海尔加知道安妮对她不以为然，这也同样算不了什么。安妮没用一个字，就让人把这一点看得明明白白。在她看来，海尔加在敌人——那些可憎的白面孔——中间住得太久了。她太了解他们了，对他们无知的愚蠢也太过宽容了。倘若那是拉丁人，安妮或许还未必不能原谅这种不忠。可竟是北欧人！动私刑的人！这未免太背叛了。海尔加微微一笑，她理解安妮的怨毒与仇恨，也多少理解这仇恨的缘由。这与她那般恶毒地仇恨着的那些人的怨毒如出一辙。恐惧。随即她轻轻叹了口气，因为她为安妮友谊的日渐消褪而惋惜。不过，念及两人人生道路的日趋分歧，她觉得哪怕这友谊彻底熄灭，也毁不掉她。倒不是她不再感激安妮的许多好处。只是她如今有了别的依托。而且，她们之间将永远横着一个罗伯特·安德森。一个讨厌的障碍。把她们从前那种推心置腹的情谊与理解隔断了。“再说，”她又出声说道，“他也不是什么了不起的结婚对象。谁都能嫁给他。谁都行。只要一个人想要的仅仅是结婚——倘若换的是奥尔森那样的人——那就不同了——或许还是桩值得夸耀的事呢。”

===Original===
The party was even more interesting than Helga had expected. Helen, Mrs. Tavenor, had given vent to a malicious glee, and had invited representatives of several opposing Harlem political and social factions, including the West Indian, and abandoned them helplessly to each other. Helga’s observing eyes picked out several great and near-great sulking or obviously trying hard not to sulk in widely separated places in the big rooms. There were present, also, a few white people, to the open disapproval or discomfort of Anne and several others. There too, poised, serene, certain, surrounded by masculine black and white, was Audrey Denney.
“Do you know, Helen,” Helga confided, “I’ve never met Miss Denney. I wish you’d introduce me. Not this minute. Later, when you can manage it. Not so﻿—er﻿—apparently by request, you know.”
Helen Tavenor laughed. “No, you wouldn’t have met her, living as you did with Anne Grey. Anderson, I mean. She’s Anne’s particular pet aversion. The mere sight of Audrey is enough to send her into a frenzy for a week. It’s too bad, too, because Audrey’s an awfully interesting person and Anne’s said some pretty awful things about her. *You’ll* like her, Helga.”
Helga nodded. “Yes, I expect to. And I know about Anne. One night﻿—” She stopped, for across the room she saw, with a stab of surprise, James Vayle. “Where, Helen did you get him?”
“Oh, that? That’s something the cat brought in. Don’t ask which one. He came with somebody, I don’t remember who. I think he’s shocked to death. Isn’t he lovely? The dear baby. I was going to introduce him to Audrey and tell her to do a good job of vamping on him as soon as I could remember the darling’s name, or when it got noisy enough so he wouldn’t hear what I called him. But you’ll do just as well. Don’t tell me you know him!” Helga made a little nod. “Well! And I suppose you met him at some shockingly wicked place in Europe. That’s always the way with those innocent-looking men.”

===Chinese===
这场宴会比海尔加预想的还要有意思。女主人海伦·塔文诺（Helen Tavenor）太太发泄了一通恶毒的兴致，把哈莱姆互相敌对的几个政治与社交派系的代表——连西印度人那一派也在内——一齐请了来，然后撒手不管，任他们彼此面面相觑。海尔加那双善于观察的眼睛，在大房间里彼此相距很远的各处，认出了几位大人物和准大人物，他们或在生闷气，或显然在拼命憋着不让自己露出来。在场的还有几个白人，这惹得安妮和其他几个人公开表示不快，或显得很不自在。那边，被黑皮肤的男人们和白皮肤的男人们簇拥着的，仪态从容、安详、笃定的，正是奥德丽·德纳姆（Audrey Denney）。
“你知道吗，海伦，”海尔加吐露心声道，“我从没见过德纳姆小姐。我希望你给我引见一下。不是这会儿。等晚些，等你腾出手来的时候。别弄得那么——呃——明摆着是应人之请，你懂的。”
海伦·塔文诺笑了。“可不，你是遇不上她的，谁让你从前跟安妮·格雷住在一块儿呢。我是说，跟安德森太太。奥德丽正是她心头头号的嫌恶。单是看见奥德丽一眼，就够让她发上一个星期的狂。这也太可惜了，因为奥德丽是个有意思透了的人，而安妮却说过她一些相当难听的话。*你*准会喜欢她的，海尔加。”
海尔加点了点头。“是，我料想会喜欢。安妮的事我也知道。有一天夜里——”她顿住了，因为她隔着屋子望见了詹姆斯·维尔，心里猛吃一惊。“海伦，你这是打哪儿把他捡来的？”
“哦，那个呀？那是猫叼进门来的东西。别问是哪只猫。他是跟什么人一块儿来的，是谁我记不得了。我看他都吓懵了。他多可爱呀？这亲爱的小娃娃。我本来打算把他介绍给奥德丽，叮嘱她好好对他施展一番魅功，只要我能想起这位宝贝的名字，或是等屋里吵得够响、他听不见我怎么称呼他的时候。不过你顶上去也一样。别告诉我你认识他！”海尔加轻轻点了点头。“好啊！我猜你是在欧洲哪个坏透了的地方认识他的。这种长相无辜的男人向来都是这一套。”

===Original===
“Not quite. I met him ages ago in Naxos. We were engaged to be married. Nice, isn’t he? His name’s Vayle. James Vayle.”
“Nice,” said Helen throwing out her hands in a characteristic dramatic gesture﻿—she had beautiful hands and arms﻿—“is exactly the word. Mind if I run off? I’ve got somebody here who’s going to sing. *Not* spirituals. And I haven’t the faintest notion where he’s got to. The cellar, I’ll bet.”
James Vayle hadn’t, Helga decided, changed at all. Someone claimed her for a dance and it was some time before she caught his eyes, half questioning, upon, her. When she did, she smiled in a friendly way over her partner’s shoulder and was rewarded by a dignified little bow. Inwardly she grinned, flattered. He hadn’t forgotten. He was still hurt. The dance over, she deserted her partner and deliberately made her way across the room to James Vayle. He was for the moment embarrassed and uncertain. Helga Crane, however, took care of that, thinking meanwhile that Helen was right. Here he did seem frightfully young and delightfully unsophisticated. He must be, though, every bit of thirty-two or more.
“They say,” was her bantering greeting, “that if one stands on the corner of One Hundred and Thirty-fifth Street and Seventh Avenue long enough, one will eventually see all the people one has ever known or met. It’s pretty true, I guess. Not literally of course.” He was, she saw, getting himself together. “It’s only another way of saying that everybody, almost, some time sooner or later comes to Harlem, even you.”
He laughed. “Yes, I guess that is true enough. I didn’t come to stay, though.” And then he was grave, his earnest eyes searchingly upon her.

===Chinese===
“倒也不尽然。我老早以前在纳克索斯就认识他了。我们曾订过婚。他人不错，对吧？他姓维尔。詹姆斯·维尔。”
“‘不错’，”海伦说着，双手向外一摊，做了个她特有的戏剧性手势——她有一双美丽的手和手臂——“正是这个词儿。我走开一下你不介意吧？我这里请了个人要唱歌。*不是*灵歌。他上哪儿去了我半点儿摸不着头脑。在地下室，我敢打赌。”
詹姆斯·维尔一点也没变，海尔加这样断定。有人来邀她跳舞，过了一阵子，她才捕捉到他的目光——半含探问——正落在她身上。捕捉到了，她便隔着舞伴的肩头友好地一笑，得到的回报是庄重的一躬。她心里暗自发笑，又很受用。他没有忘。他还在委屈。一曲舞罢，她撇下舞伴，径自穿过屋子朝詹姆斯·维尔走去。他一时显得又窘又没把握。不过海尔加·克雷恩把这一切应付裕如，心里同时想着海伦说得对。他在这儿确实显得年轻得吓人，不谙世事得可爱。尽管如此，他少说也足足有三十二岁了，兴许还更多。
“人们说，”她半开玩笑地招呼道，“一个人只要在一三五街和第七大道的街角站得够久，到头来总会见到自己认识过、遇见过的所有人。我想这话差不多是真的。当然不是一字一句的真。”她看出，他正在重新定下神来。“这不过是换种说法：所有的人，差不多所有的人，迟早有一日会来哈莱姆，连你也一样。”
他笑了。“是啊，我想这话够真的了。不过我不是来久住的。”随即他郑重起来，一双诚挚的眼睛探询地望着她。

===Original===
“Well, anyway, you’re here now, so let’s find a quiet corner if that’s possible, where we can talk. I want to hear all about you.”
For a moment he hung back and a glint of mischief shone in Helga’s eyes. “I see,” she said, “you’re just the same. However, you needn’t be anxious. This isn’t Naxos, you know. Nobody’s watching us, or if they are, they don’t care a bit what we do.”
At that he flushed a little, protested a little, and followed her. And when at last they had found seats in another room, not so crowded, he said: “I didn’t expect to see you here. I thought you were still abroad.”
“Oh, I’ve been back some time, ever since Dr. Anderson’s marriage. Anne, you know, is a great friend of mine. I used to live with her. I came for the wedding. But, of course, I’m not staying. I didn’t think I’d be here this long.”
“You don’t mean that you’re going to live over there? Do you really like it so much better?”
“Yes and no, to both questions. I was awfully glad to get back, but I wouldn’t live here always. I couldn’t. I don’t think that any of us who’ve lived abroad for any length of time would ever live here altogether again if they could help it.”

===Chinese===
“好吧，无论如何，你如今人已在这里，那就找个僻静角落吧——要是找得到的话——咱们可以聊聊。我想听听你的种种近况。”
他迟疑了片刻，海尔加眼里闪过一丝狡黠。“我明白了，”她说，“你还是老样子。不过，你用不着紧张。这儿可不是纳克索斯，你知道。没有人盯着我们，就算有，我们做什么他们也毫不在意。”
听了这话，他微微红了脸，小声抗议了一下，还是跟着她走了。等他们终于在另一间不那么拥挤的房间里寻到位子坐下，他说：“没想到会在这儿见到你。我还以为你仍在国外呢。”
“哦，我回来有些时候了，安德森博士一结婚我就回来了。安妮你知道的，是我的挚友。我从前同她住在一起。我是回来参加婚礼的。当然，我不打算留下。没想到一待就这么久。”
“你该不是说要住到那边去吧？你真的那么喜欢那边，喜欢得多得多？”
“两个问题，答案都是既是又不是。回来我高兴得很，可我不愿意永远住在这儿。我做不到。我看我们这些在国外住过些时日的人，但凡有法子，谁也不会再回美国来长住。”

===Original===
“Lot of them do, though,” James Vayle pointed out.
“Oh, I don’t mean tourists who rush over to Europe and rush all over the continent and rush back to America thinking they know Europe. I mean people who’ve actually lived there, actually lived among the people.”
“I still maintain that they nearly all come back here eventually to live.”
“That’s because they can’t help it,” Helga Crane said firmly. “Money, you know.”
“Perhaps, I’m not so sure. I was in the war. Of course, that’s not really living over there, but I saw the country and the difference in treatment. But, I can tell you, I was pretty darn glad to get back. All the fellows were.” He shook his head solemnly. “I don’t think anything, money or lack of money, keeps us here. If it was only that, if we really wanted to leave, we’d go all right. No, it’s something else, something deeper than that.”
“And just what do you think it is?”

===Chinese===
“可偏偏这么干的人多着呢。”詹姆斯·维尔指出。
“哦，我说的可不是那种旅行家，匆匆赶去欧洲，匆匆跑遍大陆，又匆匆赶回美国，便自以为认识欧洲了。我说的是实实在在在那里住过、实实在在生活在当地人中间的人。”
“我仍然坚持，他们几乎全都到头来还是要回这里来住。”
“那是因为他们没有法子，”海尔加·克雷恩斩钉截铁地说，“钱的缘故，你懂的。”
“也许吧，我不敢肯定。我打过仗。当然，那不算真正在那边生活，可我见识了那个国家，也见识了待遇上的差别。不过，跟你说，能回来我高兴得很。弟兄们个个都高兴。”他郑重其事地摇了摇头。“我不认为有什么东西——有钱也罢，没钱也罢——把我们留在这儿。倘若单是这么回事，倘若我们真心想走，我们走就是了。不，是别的什么，比这更深的东西。”
“那你觉得那究竟是什么呢？”

===Original===
“I’m afraid it’s hard to explain, but I suppose it’s just that we like to be together. I simply can’t imagine living forever away from colored people.”
A suspicion of a frown drew Helga’s brows. She threw out rather tartly: “I’m a Negro too, you know.”
“Well, Helga, you were always a little different, a little dissatisfied, though I don’t pretend to understand you at all. I never did,” he said a little wistfully.
And Helga, who was beginning to feel that the conversation had taken an impersonal and disappointing tone, was reassured and gave him her most sympathetic smile and said almost gently: “And now let’s talk about you. You’re still at Naxos?”
“Yes I’m still there. I’m assistant principal now.”
Plainly it was a cause for enthusiastic congratulation, but Helga could only manage a tepid “How nice!” Naxos was to her too remote, too unimportant. She did not even hate it now.

===Chinese===
“恐怕很难说清，不过我想，只是因为我们喜欢彼此待在一起。我简直无法想象永远远离有色人种生活。”
一丝若有若无的蹙眉拢上海尔加的双眉。她相当尖利地甩出一句：“我也是黑人，你别忘了。”
“是啊，海尔加，你总是有点儿与众不同，有点儿不知足，尽管我并不假装懂得你。我从来就不曾懂过。”他略带怅惘地说。
海尔加正开始觉得这场谈话变得空泛而令人失望，这一下放了心，向他报以最体贴的微笑，近乎温柔地说：“现在谈谈你吧。你还在纳克索斯？”
“是的，我还在那儿。我如今是副校长了。”
这显然是桩值得热烈道贺的事，可海尔加只挤得出一句不冷不热的“真好！”纳克索斯于她已太遥远、太无足轻重。她如今甚至连恨也谈不上了。

===Original===
How long, she asked, would James be in New York?
He couldn’t say. Business, important business for the school, had brought him. It was, he said, another tone creeping into his voice, another look stealing over his face, awfully good to see her. She was looking tremendously well. He hoped he would have the opportunity of seeing her again.
But of course. He must come to see her. Any time, she was always in, or would be for him. And how did he like New York, Harlem?
He didn’t, it seemed, like it. It was nice to visit, but not to live in. Oh, there were so many things he didn’t like about it, the rush, the lack of home life, the crowds, the noisy meaninglessness of it all.
On Helga’s face there had come that pityingly sneering look peculiar to imported New Yorkers when the city of their adoption is attacked by alien Americans. With polite contempt she inquired: “And is that all you don’t like?”
At her tone the man’s bronze face went purple. He answered coldly, slowly, with a faint gesture in the direction of Helen Tavenor, who stood conversing gayly with one of her white guests: “And I don’t like that sort of thing. In fact I detest it.”

===Chinese===
她问，詹姆斯打算在纽约待多久？
他说不准。是公务，为学校办的重要公务，把他带来的。他说，见到她实在太好了——说这话时，另一种语调悄悄爬进他的嗓音，另一种神色悄悄浮上他的脸。她气色好极了。他希望能有机会再见到她。
那当然。他一定得来看她。任何时候都行，她总在家，为了他总会在的。还有，他喜不喜欢纽约，喜不喜欢哈莱姆？
看来他不喜欢。来观光不错，住可不合适。哦，他不喜欢的多了去了：那股匆忙劲儿，家庭生活的缺失，拥挤的人群，还有这一切喧嚣的毫无意义。
海尔加脸上浮起了那种外来纽约客特有的又怜悯又讥诮的表情——每当他们归属的那座城市遭到外地美国人的攻击时，他们总会露出这种神色。她带着礼貌的轻蔑问道：“你不喜欢的就是这些了吗？”
听到这语气，男人那张古铜色的脸涨成了紫色。他冷冷地、缓缓地答道，一面朝海伦·塔文诺的方向轻轻做了个手势——她正与一位白人宾客谈笑风生：“还有，我不喜欢那一套。说实话，我厌恶透顶。”

===Original===
“Why?” Helga was striving hard to be casual in her manner.
James Vayle, it was evident, was beginning to be angry. It was also evident that Helga Crane’s question had embarrassed him. But he seized the bull by the horns and said: “You know as well as I do, Helga, that it’s the colored girls these men come up here to see. They wouldn’t think of bringing their wives.” And he blushed furiously at his own implication. The blush restored Helga’s good temper. James was really too funny.
“That,” she said softly, “is Hugh Wentworth, the novelist, you know.” And she indicated a tall olive-skinned girl being whirled about to the streaming music in the arms of a towering black man. “And that is his wife. She isn’t colored, as you’ve probably been thinking. And now let’s change the subject again.”
“All right! And this time let’s talk about you. You say you don’t intend to live here. Don’t you ever intend to marry, Helga?”
“Some day, perhaps. I don’t know. Marriage﻿—that means children, to me. And why add more suffering to the world? Why add any more unwanted, tortured Negroes to America? Why *do* Negroes have children? Surely it must be sinful. Think of the awfulness of being responsible for the giving of life to creatures doomed to endure such wounds to the flesh, such wounds to the spirit, as Negroes have to endure.”
James was aghast. He forgot to be embarrassed. “But Helga! Good heavens! Don’t you see that if we﻿—I mean people like us﻿—don’t have children, the others will still have. That’s one of the things that’s the matter with us. The race is sterile at the top. Few, very few Negroes of the better class have children, and each generation has to wrestle again with the obstacles of the preceding ones, lack of money, education, and background. I feel very strongly about this. We’re the ones who must have the children if the race is to get anywhere.”

===Chinese===
“为什么？”海尔加竭力让自己的举止显得漫不经心。
显然，詹姆斯·维尔开始动气了。同样显然的是，海尔加·克雷恩的问题让他难堪了。可他毅然决然迎难而上，说道：“海尔加，你跟我一样清楚，这些男人专程跑到这儿来看的，就是有色姑娘。带太太来？他们想都不会想。”话一出口，他话里的暗示让他自己窘得满脸通红。这红脸恢复了海尔加的好脾气。詹姆斯实在太好笑了。
“那一位，”她轻声说，“是休·温特沃斯（Hugh Wentworth），那位小说家，你知道的。”她指了指一个高挑的橄榄色皮肤姑娘，她正随着奔泻的乐声，在一个魁伟黑人的臂弯里旋转。“那是他的妻子。她不是有色人，虽然你多半一直这么以为。好了，咱们再换个话题吧。”
“好啊！这回谈谈你。你说你不打算住在这里。那你到底打不打算结婚呢，海尔加？”
“也许哪一天吧。我不知道。结婚——对我而言，那意味着孩子。何必再给这世界添几分苦难？何必再给美国添几个没人要的、受折磨的黑人？黑人到底*为什么*要生孩子？那想必是罪孽。想想吧，要为赋予一群生灵生命负责，而他们注定要忍受黑人不得不忍受的那些肉体的创伤、灵魂的创伤——这是何等可怕的事。”
詹姆斯骇然了，连难堪都忘了。“可是海尔加！老天爷！你难道不明白，要是我们——我是说我们这样的人——不生孩子，别人照样会生的。这正是我们身上的病症之一。这个种族在上层是不育的。上层阶级的黑人生孩子的寥寥无几，于是一代又一代人不得不重新同前代人的种种障碍搏斗：缺钱、缺教育、缺背景。这一点我感受极深。这个种族要想有点出息，就必须由我们来生孩子。”

===Original===
“Well, I for one don’t intend to contribute any to the cause. But how serious we are! And I’m afraid that I’ve really got to leave you. I’ve already cut two dances for your sake. Do come to see me.”
“Oh, I’ll come to see you all right. I’ve got several things that I want to talk to you about and one thing especially.”
“Don’t,” Helga mocked, “tell me you’re going to ask me again to marry you.”
“That,” he said, “is just what I intend to do.”

===Chinese===
“得了，我个人是不打算为这项事业贡献什么的。可瞧咱们多较真啊！恐怕我真得离开你了。为了你，我已经荒了两支舞了。千万来看我。”
“哦，我准会去看你的。我有好几件事想同你谈谈，有一件尤其想谈。”
“别，”海尔加取笑道，“别告诉我你要再一次向我求婚。”
“那个，”他说，“正是我打算做的事。”

===Original===
Helga Crane was suddenly deeply ashamed and very sorry for James Vayle, so she told him laughingly that it was shameful of him to joke with her like that, and before he could answer, she had gone tripping off with a handsome coffee-colored youth whom she had beckoned from across the room with a little smile.
Later she had to go upstairs to pin up a place in the hem of her dress which had caught on a sharp chair corner. She finished the temporary repair and stepped out into the hall, and somehow, she never quite knew exactly just how, into the arms of Robert Anderson. She drew back and looked up smiling to offer an apology.
And then it happened. He stooped and kissed her, a long kiss, holding her close. She fought against him with all her might. Then, strangely, all power seemed to ebb away, and a long-hidden, half-understood desire welled up in her with the suddenness of a dream. Helga Crane’s own arms went up about the man’s neck. When she drew away, consciously confused and embarrassed, everything seemed to have changed in a space of time which she knew to have been only seconds. Sudden anger seized her. She pushed him indignantly aside and with a little pat for her hair and dress went slowly down to the others.

===Chinese===
海尔加·克雷恩蓦地深感羞惭，又为詹姆斯·维尔难过起来，于是她笑着对他说，这样同她开玩笑真是害臊，不等他答话，她已款款而去，挽上一位英俊的咖啡色皮肤青年——那是她隔着满屋的人用一个浅笑招过来的。
后来她不得不上楼去，用别针别住裙摆上被椅子尖角勾住的地方。她草草补缀完毕，跨进走廊，又不知怎么的——她始终没能弄清究竟是怎么发生的——一头撞进了罗伯特·安德森的怀抱。她退后一步，仰起脸微笑着，正要开口道歉。
接着，那件事就发生了。他俯下身吻了她，一个长吻，把她紧紧搂在怀里。她用尽全身力气抗拒他。随即，奇怪地，一切力气仿佛都退潮而去，一股深藏已久、半明半昧的欲望，带着梦一般的突然，从她心底涌了上来。海尔加·克雷恩自己的双臂也扬起，环上了那男人的脖颈。待她抽身退开时——心里明白自己又乱又窘——一切似乎都已改变，而她知道，其间不过短短几秒。骤然的怒意攫住了她。她愤然把他推开，拍了拍头发和衣裙，缓缓地下楼回到众人中间。

## XIX / 十九

===Original===
That night riotous and colorful dreams invaded Helga Crane’s prim hotel bed. She woke in the morning weary and a bit shocked at the uncontrolled fancies which had visited her. Catching up a filmy scarf, she paced back and forth across the narrow room and tried to think. She recalled her flirtations and her mild engagement with James Vayle. She was used to kisses. But none had been like that of last night. She lived over those brief seconds, thinking not so much of the man whose arms had held her as of the ecstasy which had flooded her. Even recollection brought a little onrush of emotion that made her sway a little. She pulled herself together and began to fasten on the solid fact of Anne and experienced a pleasant sense of shock in the realization that Anne was to her exactly what she had been before the incomprehensible experience of last night. She still liked her in the same degree and in the same manner. She still felt slightly annoyed with her. She still did not envy her marriage with Anderson. By some mysterious process the emotional upheaval which had racked her had left all the rocks of her existence unmoved. Outwardly nothing had changed.
Days, weeks, passed; outwardly serene; inwardly tumultuous. Helga met Dr. Anderson at the social affairs to which often they were both asked. Sometimes she danced with him, always in perfect silence. She couldn’t, she absolutely couldn’t, speak a word to him when they were thus alone together, for at such times lassitude encompassed her; the emotion which had gripped her retreated, leaving a strange tranquillity, troubled only by a soft stir of desire. And shamed by his silence, his apparent forgetting, always after these dances she tried desperately to persuade herself to believe what she wanted to believe: that it had not happened, that she had never had that irrepressible longing. It was of no use.
As the weeks multiplied, she became aware that she must get herself out of the mental quagmire into which that kiss had thrown her. And she should be getting herself back to Copenhagen, but she had now no desire to go.
Abruptly one Sunday in a crowded room, in the midst of teacups and chatter, she knew that she couldn’t go, that she hadn’t since that kiss intended to go without exploring to the end that unfamiliar path into which she had strayed. Well, it was of no use lagging behind or pulling back. It was of no use trying to persuade herself that she didn’t want to go on. A species of fatalism fastened on her. She felt that, ever since that last day in Naxos long ago, somehow she had known that this thing would happen. With this conviction came an odd sense of elation. While making a pleasant assent to some remark of a fellow guest she put down her cup and walked without haste, smiling and nodding to friends and acquaintances on her way to that part of the room where he stood looking at some examples of African carving. Helga Crane faced him squarely. As he took the hand which she held out with elaborate casualness, she noted that his trembled slightly. She was secretly congratulating herself on her own calm when it failed her. Physical weariness descended on her. Her knees wobbled. Gratefully she slid into the chair which he hastily placed for her. Timidity came over her. She was silent. He talked. She did not listen. He came at last to the end of his long dissertation on African sculpture, and Helga Crane felt the intentness of his gaze upon her.

===Chinese===
那一夜，喧闹而斑斓的梦境侵入了海尔加·克雷恩旅馆里那张古板的床。早晨醒来，她疲惫不堪，又为自己夜里那些不受管束的遐思感到些许震惊。她抓起一条轻纱披巾，在狭窄的房间里来回踱步，想要理清思绪。她回想起自己那些调情往事，回想起与詹姆斯·维尔那段淡淡的婚约。她习惯了亲吻。可从没有哪一个像昨夜那一吻。她一遍遍重温那短短几秒，想着的与其说是那个拥抱过她的男人，不如说是那阵淹没过她的迷狂。就连回忆也带来一阵小小的情感涌动，使她微微摇晃。她定下神来，开始把自己牢牢系在安妮这个坚实的事实上，并带着一阵惬意的震惊意识到：经过昨夜那场难以索解的经历，安妮于她竟仍是从前那个安妮，分毫不差。她依然以同样的程度、同样的方式喜欢她。依然对她微微感到恼怒。依然不羡慕她与安德森的婚姻。那场震撼过她的情感剧变，经过某种神秘的程序，竟没有撼动她生命中的任何一块基石。表面上，一切都未改变。
一天天，一星期一星期地过去；外表平静，内心翻腾。海尔加在两人常被一同邀去的社交场合遇见安德森博士。有时她同他跳舞，总是默然无语。两人这样单独在一起时，她不能，绝对不能，对他说一个字，因为每逢这种时刻，一阵倦怠便将她裹住；攫住过她的那股情感退去了，留下一种奇异的宁静，只有欲望的一丝轻搅使之不安。而他的沉默、他表面的忘却又令她羞惭，每次跳完这样的舞，她都拼命想说服自己去相信她愿意相信的东西：那件事没有发生过，她从不曾有过那股压抑不住的渴望。可这毫无用处。
随着星期一个接一个地累积，她意识到自己必须从那一吻把她抛入的精神泥沼里爬出来。她也该动身回哥本哈根去了，可她如今一点也没有走的意思。
一个星期日，在一间拥挤的屋子里，在茶杯与闲谈之间，她蓦然明白：她走不成了；自从那一吻之后，她就从没打算不把那条误入的陌生小路探到尽头便一走了之。好吧，磨蹭着不动或是往回撤都没有用。想说服自己说她不想继续往前，也没有用。一种宿命感攫住了她。她觉得，自遥远的纳克索斯最后那一天起，她就不知怎么一直知道这件事终将发生。随着这个确信而来的，是一种奇异的振奋。她愉快地应了一位宾客的什么话，放下茶杯，不慌不忙地朝房间那一头走去——他正站在那里端详几件非洲雕刻——一路向朋友和熟人微笑颔首。海尔加·克雷恩坦然地面对着他。当他握住她刻意装出漫不经心的样子伸出去的手时，她察觉到他的手微微发颤。她正暗自为自己的镇定得意，镇定却弃她而去。一阵身体的倦怠压了下来。她的膝盖发软。她感激地坐进他急忙为她拉开的椅子。一阵羞怯袭上她。她沉默着。他说着话。她没有听。他终于讲完了那篇关于非洲雕塑的长篇大论，海尔加·克雷恩感到他专注的目光落在自己身上。

===Original===
“Well?” she questioned.
“I want very much to see you, Helga. Alone.”
She held herself tensely on the edge of her chair, and suggested: “Tomorrow?”
He hesitated a second and then said quickly: “Why, yes, that’s all right.”
“Eight o’clock?”
“Eight o’clock,” he agreed.

===Chinese===
“怎样？”她问。
“我很想见见你，海尔加。单独见。”
她紧张地坐在椅子边上，提议道：“明天？”
他迟疑了一秒，随即飞快地说：“哦，好啊，没问题。”
“八点？”
“八点。”他应道。

===Original===
Eight o’clock tomorrow came. Helga Crane never forgot it. She had carried away from yesterday’s meeting a feeling of increasing elation. It had seemed to her that she hadn’t been so happy, so exalted, in years, if ever. All night, all day, she had mentally prepared herself for the coming consummation; physically too, spending hours before the mirror.
Eight o’clock had come at last and with it Dr. Anderson. Only then had uneasiness come upon her and a feeling of fear for possible exposure. For Helga Crane wasn’t, after all, a rebel from society, Negro society. It did mean something to her. She had no wish to stand alone. But these late fears were overwhelmed by the hardiness of insistent desire; and she had got herself down to the hotel’s small reception room.
It was, he had said, awfully good of her to see him. She instantly protested. No, she had wanted to see him. He looked at her surprised. “You know, Helga,” he had begun with an air of desperation, “I can’t forgive myself for acting such a swine at the Tavenors’ party. I don’t at all blame you for being angry and not speaking to me except when you had to.”
But that, she exclaimed, was simply too ridiculous. “I wasn’t angry a bit.” And it had seemed to her that things were not exactly going forward as they should. It seemed that he had been very sincere, and very formal. Deliberately. She had looked down at her hands and inspected her bracelets, for she had felt that to look at him would be, under the circumstances, too exposing.
“I was afraid,” he went on, “that you might have misunderstood; might have been unhappy about it. I could kick myself. It was, it must have been, Tavenor’s rotten cocktails.”

===Chinese===
明天的八点钟到来了。海尔加·克雷恩永远忘不了它。从昨天的会面里，她带走了一种不断增长的振奋。她觉得，多年以来——如果曾经有过的话——她不曾这样幸福，这样意气昂扬。整整一夜，整整一天，她在精神上为即将到来的圆满作着准备；肉体上也是，在镜子前一待就是几个钟头。
八点钟终于到了，安德森博士也一同到了。直到那时，不安才袭上她心头，还有对可能败露的恐惧。因为海尔加·克雷恩毕竟不是社会的叛逆者——黑人社会的。那社会对她终究是有分量的。她无意孤身自立。但这些迟来的恐惧，都被那股执拗欲望的顽强压倒了；她终于让自己下了楼，走进旅馆的小会客室。
他说，她肯见他，真是太好了。她立刻表示反对。不，是她自己想见他。他惊讶地望着她。“你要知道，海尔加，”他带着一种孤注一掷的神情开了口，“在塔文诺家的宴会上做出那样猪一般的行径，我不能原谅自己。你生了气，除了不得已从不理我，我一点都不怪你。”
可那个，她叫起来，简直荒唐透顶。“我一点也没生气。”而她已觉出事情的进展不太对头，不像应有的样子。他显得极为诚恳，又极为拘谨。是故意的。她低头看自己的手，端详手镯，因为她觉得，在此情此景之下，看他一眼都未免太暴露了。
“我原来担心，”他接着说，“你会误会；会为此不快。我真想踹自己一脚。那一定是——一定是塔文诺家那些劣质鸡尾酒闹的。”

===Original===
Helga Crane’s sense of elation had abruptly left her. At the same time she had felt the need to answer carefully. No, she replied, she hadn’t thought of it at all. It had meant nothing to her. She had been kissed before. It was really too silly of him to have been at all bothered about it. “For what,” she had asked, “is one kiss more or less, these days, between friends?” She had even laughed a little.
Dr. Anderson was relieved. He had been, he told her, no end upset. Rising, he said: “I see you’re going out. I won’t keep you.”
Helga Crane too had risen. Quickly. A sort of madness had swept over her. She felt that he had belittled and ridiculed her. And thinking this, she had suddenly savagely slapped Robert Anderson with all her might, in the face.
For a short moment they had both stood stunned, in the deep silence which had followed that resounding slap. Then, without a word of contrition or apology, Helga Crane had gone out of the room and upstairs.

===Chinese===
海尔加·克雷恩的振奋感骤然弃她而去。同时她感到必须小心作答。没有，她答道，她压根儿没把那事故在心上。那对她毫无意义。她以前也被吻过。他为这个竟烦恼不安，实在太可笑了。“如今朋友之间，”她问道，“多一个吻少一个吻，又算得了什么呢？”她甚至还笑了笑。
安德森博士如释重负。他告诉她，他一直心乱得不得了。他站起身来，说：“我看你要出门了。我就不多留你了。”
海尔加·克雷恩也站了起来。很快。一阵疯魔般的冲动席卷了她。她觉得他贬低了她，嘲弄了她。这样想着，她突然抡起全部力气，狠狠一记耳光，扇在罗伯特·安德森的脸上。
在那记响亮耳光之后的深深寂静里，两人都呆立了片刻。然后，海尔加·克雷恩没有说一句悔罪或道歉的话，径自走出房间，上楼去了。

===Original===
She had, she told herself, been perfectly justified in slapping Dr. Anderson, but she was not convinced. So she had tried hard to make herself very drunk in order that sleep might come to her, but had managed only to make herself very sick.
Not even the memory of how all living had left his face, which had gone a taupe gray hue, or the despairing way in which he had lifted his head and let it drop, or the trembling hands which he had pressed into his pockets, brought her any scrap of comfort. She had ruined everything. Ruined it because she had been so silly as to close her eyes to all indications that pointed to the fact that no matter what the intensity of his feelings or desires might be, he was not the sort of man who would for any reason give up one particle of his own good opinion of himself. Not even for her. Not even though he knew that she had wanted so terribly something special from him.
Something special. And now she had forfeited it forever. Forever. Helga had an instantaneous shocking perception of what forever meant. And then, like a flash, it was gone, leaving an endless stretch of dreary years before her appalled vision.

===Chinese===
她对自己说，扇安德森博士那一耳光完全有理，可她自己并不信服。于是她拼命把自己灌得大醉，好让睡眠降临，结果只是把自己弄得大吐特吐。
就连这样的记忆——生气如何从他脸上褪尽，那张脸如何变成灰褐的颜色；他如何绝望地抬起头又垂下去；他如何把颤抖的双手插进口袋——也带不来一丝一毫的安慰。她把一切都毁了。之所以毁掉，是因为她愚蠢到闭眼不看一切迹象，那些迹象都表明：不论他的感情或欲望有多炽烈，他都不是那种男人——会为任何缘故放弃一丁点自我欣赏的男人。哪怕为了她也不会。哪怕他知道她曾那样渴切地想从他那里得到一点特别的东西，也不会。
一点特别的东西。如今她已永远地失掉了它。永远。海尔加在刹那间震惊地领悟到“永远”意味着什么。随即，像一道闪光，那领悟消逝了，只在她惊骇的眼前留下无穷无尽、萧索荒凉的岁月。

## XX / 二十

===Original===
The day was a rainy one. Helga Crane, stretched out on her bed, felt herself so broken physically, mentally, that she had given up thinking. But back and forth in her staggered brain wavering, incoherent thoughts shot shuttle-like. Her pride would have shut out these humiliating thoughts and painful visions of herself. The effort was too great. She felt alone, isolated from all other human beings, separated even from her own anterior existence by the disaster of yesterday. Over and over, she repeated: “There’s nothing left but to go now.” Her anguish seemed unbearable.

For days, for weeks, voluptuous visions had haunted her. Desire had burned in her flesh with uncontrollable violence. The wish to give herself had been so intense that Dr. Anderson’s surprising, trivial apology loomed as a direct refusal of the offering. Whatever outcome she had expected, it had been something else than this, this mortification, this feeling of ridicule and self-loathing, this knowledge that she had deluded herself. It was all, she told herself, as unpleasant as possible.

Almost she wished she could die. Not quite. It wasn’t that she was afraid of death, which had, she thought, its picturesque aspects. It was rather that she knew she would not die. And death, after the debacle, would but intensify its absurdity. Also, it would reduce her, Helga Crane, to unimportance, to nothingness. Even in her unhappy present state, that did not appeal to her. Gradually, reluctantly, she began to know that the blow to her self-esteem, the certainty of having proved herself a silly fool, was perhaps the severest hurt which she had suffered. It was her self-assurance that had gone down in the crash. After all, what Dr. Anderson thought didn’t matter. She could escape from the discomfort of his knowing gray eyes. But she couldn’t escape from sure knowledge that she had made a fool of herself. This angered her further and she struck the wall with her hands and jumped up and began hastily to dress herself. She couldn’t go on with the analysis. It was too hard. Why bother, when she could add nothing to the obvious fact that she had been a fool?

“I can’t stay in this room any longer. I must get out or I’ll choke.” Her self-knowledge had increased her anguish. Distracted, agitated, incapable of containing herself, she tore open drawers and closets trying desperately to take some interest in the selection of her apparel.

===Chinese===
那是个下雨天。海尔加·克雷恩直挺挺躺在床上，只觉得身心俱已破碎，索性不再思考了。可是在她天旋地转的脑海里，犹疑而紊乱的思绪仍如梭子般穿来穿去。她的自尊本该把这些羞辱的念头、这些刺痛的自我景象挡在外面。可那太费力了。她感到孤独，与一切世人隔绝，甚至被昨天的灾祸与自己过往的生活隔开。她一遍又一遍地重复着：“如今什么都没有了，只有走了。”她的痛苦似乎难以忍受。

一连多少天，多少星期，旖旎的幻象纠缠着她。欲望以无法遏制的暴烈在她肉体里燃烧。献出自身的愿望是那样强烈，以致安德森博士那句出人意料的、轻描淡写的道歉，在她看来竟成了对这份奉献的当面回绝。无论她原本期待的是怎样的结局，总归不该是这样——这样屈辱，这样可笑而自我厌憎，这样明白自己欺骗了自己。这一切，她对自己说，糟糕得无以复加。

她几乎希望自己能死掉。也并不尽然。倒不是她怕死——死，她想，自有其悲壮动人的一面。毋宁说，她知道自己死不了。惨败之后再死，只会使这败局显得更荒唐。而且，那还会把她，海尔加·克雷恩，贬成无足轻重，贬成一团虚无。即便在眼下这不快的境地，这也引不起她的兴致。渐渐地，她不情愿地开始明白：自尊心受的这一击，确证了自己是个傻丫头，这或许是她所受的最重的创伤。在这场崩塌中垮掉的，正是她的自信。说到底，安德森博士怎么想，都无关紧要。他那一双洞悉一切的灰眼睛带来的不安，她可以躲开。可她摆脱不掉这个确凿的认识：她出了自己的丑。这让她愈发恼怒，她用双手捶墙，跳起身来，匆匆穿起衣服。她没法再这样剖析下去了。太难受了。既然那个明白的事实——她就是个傻瓜——已无可增补，又何必费这个神呢？

“我在这间屋里再也待不下去了。必须出去，不然我要憋死了。”这份自我认识加深了她的痛苦。她心烦意乱，焦躁不宁，按捺不住，把抽屉和衣橱翻得乱七八糟，拼命想让自己对挑选衣裳生出一点兴致。

===Original===
It was evening and still raining. In the streets, unusually deserted, the electric lights cast dull glows. Helga Crane, walking rapidly, aimlessly, could decide on no definite destination. She had not thought to take umbrella or even rubbers. Rain and wind whipped cruelly about her, drenching her garments and chilling her body. Soon the foolish little satin shoes which she wore were sopping wet. Unheeding these physical discomforts, she went on, but at the open corner of One Hundred and Thirty-eighth Street a sudden more ruthless gust of wind ripped the small hat from her head. In the next minute the black clouds opened wider and spilled their water with unusual fury. The streets became swirling rivers. Helga Crane, forgetting her mental torment, looked about anxiously for a sheltering taxi. A few taxis sped by, but inhabited, so she began desperately to struggle through wind and rain toward one of the buildings, where she could take shelter in a store or a doorway. But another whirl of wind lashed her and, scornful of her slight strength, tossed her into the swollen gutter.

Now she knew beyond all doubt that she had no desire to die, and certainly not there nor then. Not in such a messy wet manner. Death had lost all of its picturesque aspects to the girl lying soaked and soiled in the flooded gutter. So, though she was very tired and very weak, she dragged herself up and succeeded finally in making her way to the store whose blurred light she had marked for her destination.

She had opened the door and had entered before she was aware that, inside, people were singing a song which she was conscious of having heard years ago﻿—hundreds of years it seemed. Repeated over and over, she made out the words:

> … Showers of blessings,
 

 Showers of blessings﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
天黑了，雨还在下。街上异乎寻常地冷清，电灯投下昏暗的光晕。海尔加·克雷恩快步走着，漫无目的，定不下一个明确去处。她没想到带伞，连套鞋也没穿。风雨无情地抽打着她，浇透她的衣衫，浸凉她的身体。不多久，她脚上那双可笑的小缎鞋就湿透了。她全不理会这些身体上的不适，继续走着，可到了一三八街开阔的街角，一阵更加狂暴的风骤然把她头上的小帽掀走。紧接着，乌云豁开更宽的口子，以异样的狂怒倾下雨水。街道成了打旋的河流。海尔加·克雷恩忘了心头的折磨，焦急地四下张望，想找一辆能避雨的出租车。几辆车飞驰而过，里面都载着人，于是她绝望地在风雨里挣扎着朝一排房子挪去，想随便躲进哪家店铺或门洞。可又一阵旋风抽打过来，全然藐视她那点微弱的力气，把她掼进了涨满水的阴沟。

此刻她确凿无疑地知道了：她并不想死，而且肯定不是死在此地、此时。不是死在这种湿漉漉的狼狈样子里。对这个浑身湿透、泡在积水阴沟里的姑娘来说，死已失去了它全部悲壮动人的色彩。于是，尽管她疲惫不堪、虚弱至极，还是拖着自己站起来，终于挪到了那家店铺——那团朦胧的灯光，她早认定是自己的目的地。

她推开门，走了进去，这才发觉里面的人正唱着一支歌——她记得多年以前听到过，恍惚已是几百年前的事了。那歌翻来覆去地唱着，她听清了歌词：

> ……福雨倾盆，
 

 福雨倾盆﻿ ﻿…

===Original===
She was conscious too of a hundred pairs of eyes upon her as she stood there, drenched and disheveled, at the door of this improvised meetinghouse.

> … Showers of blessings﻿ ﻿…

The appropriateness of the song, with its constant reference to showers, the ridiculousness of herself in such surroundings, was too much for Helga Crane’s frayed nerves. She sat down on the floor, a dripping heap, and laughed and laughed and laughed.

It was into a shocked silence that she laughed. For at the first hysterical peal the words of the song had died in the singers’ throats, and the wheezy organ had lapsed into stillness. But in a moment there were hushed solicitous voices; she was assisted to her feet and led haltingly to a chair near the low platform at the far end of the room. On one side of her a tall angular black woman under a queer hat sat down, on the other a fattish yellow man with huge outstanding ears and long, nervous hands.

The singing began again, this time a low wailing thing:

===Chinese===
她还意识到上百双眼睛正盯着自己——她浑身湿透、头发零乱地站在这座临时礼拜堂的门口。

> ……福雨倾盆﻿ ﻿…

这支歌竟是如此应景，翻来覆去都是倾盆的雨；而她自己置身此间的滑稽相，对海尔加·克雷恩濒于崩溃的神经来说实在受不了了。她一屁股坐到地上，成了一摊滴水的烂泥，笑了又笑，笑了又笑。

她笑进了一片惊愕的寂静里。因为她的第一阵歇斯底里的大笑一起，歌词便噎在了唱歌人的喉咙里，那架哮喘的风琴也归于沉寂。但片刻之后，响起了压低的关切话语；她被搀起来，踉踉跄跄地被领到屋子那头低矮讲台边的一把椅子前。她的一边坐下了一个戴古怪帽子的高瘦黑女人，另一边是个微胖的黄皮肤男人，长着一对招风大耳和一双细长的、神经质的手。

歌声重新响起，这一回是一支低回哀泣的曲子：

===Original===
> Oh, the bitter shame and sorrow
 

 That a time could ever be,
 

 When I let the Savior’s pity
 

 Plead in vain, and proudly answered:
 

 “All of self and none of Thee,
 

 All of self and none of Thee.”
> Yet He found me, I beheld Him,
 

 Bleeding on the cursed tree;
 

 Heard Him pray: “Forgive them, Father.”
 

 And my wistful heart said faintly,
 

 “Some of self and some of Thee,
 

 Some of self and some of Thee.”

There were, it appeared, endless moaning verses. Behind Helga a woman had begun to cry audibly, and soon, somewhere else, another. Outside, the wind still bellowed. The wailing singing went on:

> … Less of self and more of Thee,
 

 Less of self and more of Thee.

===Chinese===
> 哦，那苦楚的羞愧与悲伤
 

 竟会有那样的时辰，
 

 当我任凭救主的怜悯
 

 徒然哀恳，却傲然回答：
 

 “全是自我，没有你，
 

 全是自我，没有你。”
> 然而他寻见了我，我仰望他
 

 流血钉在受咒的树上；
 

 听见他祷告：“父啊，赦免他们。”
 

 我渴慕的心怯怯地说：
 

 “有些自我，也有些你，
 

 有些自我，也有些你。”

这支歌仿佛有无穷无尽的呻吟的段落。海尔加身后一个女人已经出了声地哭起来，不一会儿，别处又有一个。外面，风还在咆哮。哀泣的歌声继续着：

> ……少些自我，多些你，
 

 少些自我，多些你。

===Original===
Helga too began to weep, at first silently, softly; then with great racking sobs. Her nerves were so torn, so aching, her body so wet, so cold! It was a relief to cry unrestrainedly, and she gave herself freely to soothing tears, not noticing that the groaning and sobbing of those about her had increased, unaware that the grotesque ebony figure at her side had begun gently to pat her arm to the rhythm of the singing and to croon softly: “Yes, chile, yes, chile.” Nor did she notice the furtive glances that the man on her other side cast at her between his fervent shouts of “Amen!” and “Praise God for a sinner!”

She did notice, though, that the tempo, the atmosphere of the place, had changed, and gradually she ceased to weep and gave her attention to what was happening about her. Now they were singing:

> … Jesus knows all about my troubles﻿ ﻿…

Men and women were swaying and clapping their hands, shouting and stamping their feet to the frankly irreverent melody of the song. Without warning the woman at her side threw off her hat, leaped to her feet, waved her long arms, and shouted shrilly: “Glory! Hallelujah!” and then, in wild, ecstatic fury jumped up and down before Helga clutching at the girl’s soaked coat, and screamed: “Come to Jesus, you pore los’ sinner!” Alarmed for the fraction of a second, involuntarily Helga had shrunk from her grasp, wriggling out of the wet coat when she could not loosen the crazed creature’s hold. At the sight of the bare arms and neck growing out of the clinging red dress, a shudder shook the swaying man at her right. On the face of the dancing woman before her a disapproving frown gathered. She shrieked: “A scarlet ’oman. Come to Jesus, you pore los’ Jezebel!”

===Chinese===
海尔加也哭了起来，起初无声地、轻轻地；随后便是撕心裂肺的大恸。她的神经已被撕得那样碎、那样痛，她的身体已那样湿、那样冷！无所顾忌地痛哭是一种解脱，她任凭自己痛痛快快地流着抚慰的泪，没有发觉周围呻吟呜咽的声音愈来愈高，也没有察觉身边那个乌木雕般的怪影已开始随着歌声的节拍轻轻拍她的手臂，柔声哼着：“是的，孩子，是的，孩子。”她也没有注意到另一边的那个男人，在热烈高喊“阿们！”和“为罪人赞美上帝！”的间隙，正向她偷觑过来的一道道目光。

不过她确实注意到，这地方的节奏、气氛变了，于是她渐渐止住哭泣，把注意力投向四周正在发生的事情。这时他们唱的是：

> ……耶稣知道我一切的苦难﻿ ﻿…

男男女女随着那支毫不掩饰其狂放的曲调摇曳着、拍着手、叫喊着、跺着脚。冷不防，她身旁那个女人甩掉帽子，一跃而起，挥舞着长长的手臂，尖声喊道：“荣耀！哈利路亚！”随即带着狂乱的、心醉神迷的激愤，在海尔加面前又蹦又跳，一把揪住姑娘湿透的外衣，尖叫着：“来归耶稣吧，你这可怜迷途的罪人！”海尔加惊惶了一瞬，不由自主地想挣脱她的手，见掰不开这疯女人死攥的手指，便从湿外衣里扭身滑了出来。看到从紧贴身子的红裙里露出的赤裸手臂和脖颈，她右边那个摇曳着的男人一阵战栗。而她面前那个手舞足蹈的女人脸上聚起一道不以为然的皱眉，尖声叫道：“一个红衣淫妇。来归耶稣吧，你这可怜迷途的耶洗别！”

===Original===
At this the short brown man on the platform raised a placating hand and sanctimoniously delivered himself of the words: “Remembah de words of our Mastah: ‘Let him that is without sin cast de first stone.’ Let us pray for our errin’ sistah.”

Helga Crane was amused, angry, disdainful, as she sat there, listening to the preacher praying for her soul. But though she was contemptuous, she was being too well entertained to leave. And it was, at least, warm and dry. So she stayed, listening to the fervent exhortation to God to save her and to the zealous shoutings and groanings of the congregation. Particularly she was interested in the writhings and weepings of the feminine portion, which seemed to predominate. Little by little the performance took on an almost Bacchic vehemence. Behind her, before her, beside her, frenzied women gesticulated, screamed, wept, and tottered to the praying of the preacher, which had gradually become a cadenced chant. When at last he ended, another took up the plea in the same moaning chant, and then another. It went on and on without pause with the persistence of some unconquerable faith exalted beyond time and reality.

Fascinated, Helga Crane watched until there crept upon her an indistinct horror of an unknown world. She felt herself in the presence of a nameless people, observing rites of a remote obscure origin. The faces of the men and women took on the aspect of a dim vision. “This,” she whispered to herself, “is terrible. I must get out of here.” But the horror held her. She remained motionless, watching, as if she lacked the strength to leave the place﻿—foul, vile, and terrible, with its mixture of breaths, its contact of bodies, its concerted convulsions, all in wild appeal for a single soul. Her soul.

And as Helga watched and listened, gradually a curious influence penetrated her; she felt an echo of the weird orgy resound in her own heart; she felt herself possessed by the same madness; she too felt a brutal desire to shout and to sling herself about. Frightened at the strength of the obsession, she gathered herself for one last effort to escape, but vainly. In rising, weakness and nausea from last night’s unsuccessful attempt to make herself drunk overcame her. She had eaten nothing since yesterday. She fell forward against the crude railing which enclosed the little platform. For a single moment she remained there in silent stillness, because she was afraid she was going to be sick. And in that moment she was lost﻿—or saved. The yelling figures about her pressed forward, closing her in on all sides. Maddened, she grasped at the railing, and with no previous intention began to yell like one insane, drowning every other clamor, while torrents of tears streamed down her face. She was unconscious of the words she uttered, or their meaning: “Oh God, mercy, mercy. Have mercy on me!” but she repeated them over and over.

From those about her came a thunderclap of joy. Arms were stretched toward her with savage frenzy. The women dragged themselves upon their knees or crawled over the floor like reptiles, sobbing and pulling their hair and tearing off their clothing. Those who succeeded in getting near to her leaned forward to encourage the unfortunate sister, dropping hot tears and beads of sweat upon her bare arms and neck.

The thing became real. A miraculous calm came upon her. Life seemed to expand, and to become very easy. Helga Crane felt within her a supreme aspiration toward the regaining of simple happiness, a happiness unburdened by the complexities of the lives she had known. About her the tumult and the shouting continued, but in a lesser degree. Some of the more exuberant worshipers had fainted into inert masses, the voices of others were almost spent. Gradually the room grew quiet and almost solemn, and to the kneeling girl time seemed to sink back into the mysterious grandeur and holiness of far-off simpler centuries.

===Chinese===
听到这话，台上那个矮个子棕色男人举起一只安抚的手，一本正经地诵道：“要记住咱们主的话：‘你们中间谁是没有罪的，谁就先拿石头打她。’让咱们为迷途的姐妹祈祷吧。”

海尔加·克雷恩坐在那里，听着传道人为她的灵魂祈祷，只觉又好笑、又气恼、又鄙夷。可她虽然满心轻蔑，这出戏却让她看得津津有味，舍不得走。何况这里至少又暖和又干燥。于是她留了下来，听着那求上帝拯救她的热烈劝勉，听着会众热切的呼喊与呻吟。她尤其感兴趣的是女信徒们的扭动与哭泣——她们似乎占了多数。渐渐地，这场表演带上了一种近乎酒神祭的狂烈。她身后、身前、身旁，狂乱的女人们随着传道人的祷告比划着、尖叫着、哭泣着、踉跄着——那祷告已渐渐变成一种抑扬的吟诵。他终于停下来，另一个又用同样的哀吟腔调接续下去，然后又一个。就这样无休无止，带着某种不可征服的信仰的执拗，超越于时间与现实之外，高扬着。

海尔加·克雷恩看得入了迷，直到一种对未知世界的朦胧恐怖悄悄爬上她的心头。她感到自己置身于一群无名无姓的人们中间，旁观着一套渊源邈远而晦暗的仪式。男男女女的脸渐渐显出一片幽暗幻影的模样。“这，”她喃喃自语，“太可怕了。我必须离开这里。”可那恐怖攫住了她。她一动不动地留在原地望着，仿佛已没有力气离开这个地方——污浊、鄙俗而可怕，混杂的呼吸，肉体的相触，众人齐发的痉挛，全都狂野地为一个灵魂呼求。她自己的灵魂。

就这样看着、听着，一种奇异的影响渐渐渗入了海尔加的身体；她感到那狂野狂欢的回声在她自己心里轰响；她感到自己也被同样的疯魔攫住；她也生出一股凶蛮的欲望，想喊叫，想把身子甩来甩去。这魔力的强劲吓坏了她，她攒起全身力气作最后一次逃脱的努力，却是徒劳。刚一站起，昨夜灌醉未成留下的虚弱和恶心便压倒了她。她从昨天起粒米未进。她朝前一栽，扑在围住小讲台的粗糙栏杆上。有一瞬间她默默无声地停在那里，因为她怕自己要吐。就在那一瞬间，她沉沦了——或者说，得救了。周围喊叫的身影向前涌来，把她团团围住。她疯了似的抓住栏杆，全无半点先前的打算，像疯人一样喊叫起来，盖过一切喧嚣，泪水如注地淌下脸颊。她说出的字句及其意义，她全无所觉：“哦上帝，怜悯，怜悯。怜悯我吧！”只是翻来覆去地念着。

四周响起一声欢乐的霹雳。一条条手臂带着狂野的激奋向她伸来。女人们拖着身子跪行，或者像爬虫一样在地板上爬，抽噎着、扯着头发、撕着衣裳。挨近她的人俯身向前，给这不幸的姐妹打气，滚烫的泪珠和汗珠滴在她裸露的手臂和脖颈上。

那一切变成了真的。一种奇迹般的平静降临到她身上。生命仿佛舒展开来，变得轻省了。海尔加·克雷恩心里涌起一种至高的渴望，渴望重新获得单纯的幸福——一种不被她所经历过的种种复杂人生所拖累的幸福。她四周的喧腾与喊叫仍在继续，只是声势渐弱。几个格外狂热的信徒已昏厥成瘫软的一团，其余人的声音也近乎耗尽。屋子渐渐安静下来，近乎肃穆，对跪着的姑娘来说，时间仿佛沉入了遥远而质朴的世纪那种神秘的庄严与神圣之中。

## XXI / 二十一

===Original===
On leaving the mission Helga Crane had started straight back to her room at the hotel. With her had gone the fattish yellow man who had sat beside her. He had introduced himself as the Reverend Mr. Pleasant Green in proffering his escort for which Helga had been grateful because she had still felt a little dizzy and much exhausted. So great had been this physical weariness that as she had walked beside him, without attention to his verbose information about his own “field,” as he called it, she had been seized with a hateful feeling of vertigo and obliged to lay firm hold on his arm to keep herself from falling. The weakness had passed as suddenly as it had come. Silently they had walked on. And gradually Helga had recalled that the man beside her had himself swayed slightly at their close encounter, and that frantically for a fleeting moment he had gripped at a protruding fence railing. That man! Was it possible? As easy as that?
Instantly across her still half-hypnotized consciousness little burning darts of fancy had shot themselves. No. She couldn’t. It would be too awful. Just the same, what or who was there to hold her back? Nothing. Simply nothing. Nobody. Nobody at all.
Her searching mind had become in a moment quite clear. She cast at the man a speculative glance, aware that for a tiny space she had looked into his mind, a mind striving to be calm. A mind that was certain that it was secure because it was concerned only with things of the soul, spiritual things, which to him meant religious things. But actually a mind by habit at home amongst the mere material aspect of things, and at that moment consumed by some longing for the ecstasy that might lurk behind the gleam of her cheek, the flying wave of her hair, the pressure of her slim fingers on his heavy arm. An instant’s flashing vision it had been and it was gone at once. Escaped in the aching of her own senses and the sudden disturbing fear that she herself had perhaps missed the supreme secret of life.
After all, there was nothing to hold her back. Nobody to care. She stopped sharply, shocked at what she was on the verge of considering. Appalled at where it might lead her.

===Chinese===
离开布道会后，海尔加·克雷恩便径直动身回旅馆的房间去。那个坐在她身旁的微胖的黄皮肤男人陪她一起走了。他在提出护送她时，曾自我介绍说自己是普莱森特·格林牧师（Reverend Mr. Pleasant Green）；对此海尔加心存感激，因为她仍感到有点头晕，而且疲惫不堪。身体的倦怠是那样沉重，以致走在他身边时，她对他絮絮叨叨讲着自己那个“工场”——他是这么称呼的——的种种，全没听进去，一阵可恶的眩晕突然袭来，她不得不紧紧抓住他的手臂，才没有跌倒。那阵虚弱来得突然，去得也突然。两人默默地继续走着。渐渐地，海尔加回想起，在两人挨得极近的那一霎，她身旁这个男人自己也微微晃过一晃，还慌乱地抓了一下面前突出的栅栏。这个人！可能吗？就这么容易？
刹那间，一支支灼热的小小遐想的飞镖，射过她仍处于半催眠状态的意识。不。她不能。那太可怕了。尽管如此，又有什么、有什么人能拉住她呢？没有。什么都没有。没有人。一个人也没有。
她那苦苦搜寻的心智霎时间变得十分清明。她朝那男人投去探究的一瞥，意识到自己曾在极短的一瞬窥进过他的头脑——一个竭力想保持镇定的头脑。一个自以为万无一失的头脑，因为它只关切灵魂的事、精神的事，而那在他就等于宗教的事。可实际上，那是个习惯上安于事物纯然物质一面的头脑，而就在那一刻，它正被某种渴望煎熬着——渴望她脸颊的光泽背后、她飞扬的鬈发背后、她纤细手指压在他粗壮手臂上的压力背后，或许潜藏着的销魂。那不过是一瞬闪现的幻象，随即消逝了。消逝在她自己感官的酸痛里，消逝在突然袭来的不安的恐惧里——她恐怕已经错过了生命的至高奥秘。
毕竟，没有什么能拉住她。没有人在意。她猛然停下脚步，为自己正要考虑的事惊呆了。为这可能把她引向的去处吓坏了。

===Original===
The man﻿—what was his name?﻿—thinking that she was almost about to fall again, had reached out his arms to her. Helga Crane had deliberately stopped thinking. She had only smiled, a faint provocative smile, and pressed her fingers deep into his arms until a wild look had come into his slightly bloodshot eyes.
The next morning she lay for a long while, scarcely breathing, while she reviewed the happenings of the night before. Curious. She couldn’t be sure that it wasn’t religion that had made her feel so utterly different from dreadful yesterday. And gradually she became a little sad, because she realized that with every hour she would get a little farther away from this soothing haziness, this rest from her long trouble of body and of spirit; back into the clear bareness of her own small life and being, from which happiness and serenity always faded just as they had shaped themselves. And slowly bitterness crept into her soul. Because, she thought, all I’ve ever had in life has been things﻿—except just this one time. At that she closed her eyes, for even remembrance caused her to shiver a little.
Things, she realized, hadn’t been, weren’t, enough for her. She’d have to have something else besides. It all came back to that old question of happiness. Surely this was it. Just for a fleeting moment Helga Crane, her eyes watching the wind scattering the gray-white clouds and so clearing a speck of blue sky, questioned her ability to retain, to bear, this happiness at such cost as she must pay for it. There was, she knew, no getting round that. The man’s agitation and sincere conviction of sin had been too evident, too illuminating. The question returned in a slightly new form. Was it worth the risk? Could she take it? Was she able? Though what did it matter﻿—now?
And all the while she knew in one small corner of her mind that such thinking was useless. She had made her decision. Her resolution. It was a chance at stability, at permanent happiness, that she meant to take. She had let so many other things, other chances, escape her. And anyway there was God, He would perhaps make it come out all right. Still confused and not so sure that it wasn’t the fact that she was “saved” that had contributed to this after feeling of well-being, she clutched the hope, the desire to believe that now at last she had found some One, some Power, who was interested in her. Would help her.
She meant, however, for once in her life to be practical. So she would make sure of both things, God and man.

===Chinese===
那男人——他叫什么名字来着？——以为她又要跌倒，向她伸出了双臂。海尔加·克雷恩存心停止了思考。她只是嫣然一笑，一个若有若无的撩人的微笑，一面把手指深深掐进他的手臂，直到他那双微带血丝的眼睛里燃起一缕狂野的神色。
第二天早晨，她久久地躺着，几乎不敢呼吸，回想前一天夜里发生的一切。奇怪。她不能肯定，让她感到与可怕的昨天截然两样的，是否正是宗教。渐渐地她有点忧伤起来，因为她明白，随着每一个钟点的过去，她都要离这片抚慰的朦胧、这番漫长身心煎熬后的安歇更远一点；重新回到她自己那份渺小人生的赤裸清明里——在那里，幸福与安宁总是在刚刚成形之际便消褪。苦涩慢慢地渗进她的灵魂。因为，她想，我这一生所拥有的向来只是东西——唯独这一次例外。想到这里她闭上了眼睛，因为就连回忆也让她微微战栗。
东西，她明白了，过去不曾、如今也不能使她满足。她还必须拥有点别的。一切又绕回那个关于幸福的老问题。想必就是它了。海尔加·克雷恩望着风把灰白色的云吹散、吹出一小片蓝天，有那么一闪而逝的片刻，怀疑自己是否有能力留住、承受这份幸福——以她必须付出的那样的代价。她知道，这一点无从回避。那男人的激动，和他对罪孽真诚的悔信，实在太过分明，太有启发性了。问题换了个稍稍新鲜的形式回来了。这险值得冒吗？她冒得起吗？她行吗？不过如今——这又有什么要紧？
而自始至终，她心里有个小小的角落明白，这样想毫无用处。她已经作出了决定。下定了决心。这是一个通往安稳、通往长久幸福的机遇，她决意抓住它。她已经放跑过太多别的东西、别的机会。再说还有上帝在，说不定他会让一切有个好结果。她仍是一片迷乱，也不敢肯定这种事后的康泰之感不是因为她“得救”了的缘故；她紧紧抓住那希望，那种相信的渴望——相信如今她终于找到了某一位、某种力量，是关切她的。会帮助她的。
不过，她生平头一回打算务求实际。所以两样东西她都要抓牢：上帝，和男人。

===Original===
Her glance caught the calendar over the little white desk. The tenth of November. The steamer *Oscar II* sailed today. Yesterday she had half thought of sailing with it. Yesterday. How far away!
With the thought of yesterday came the thought of Robert Anderson and a feeling of elation, revenge. She had put herself beyond the need of help from him. She had made it impossible for herself ever again to appeal to him. Instinctively she had the knowledge that he would be shocked. Grieved. Horribly hurt even. Well, let him!
The need to hurry suddenly obsessed her. She must. The morning was almost gone. And she meant, if she could manage it, to be married today. Rising, she was seized with a fear so acute that she had to lie down again. For the thought came to her that she might fail. Might not be able to confront the situation. That would be too dreadful. But she became calm again. How could he, a naive creature like that, hold out against her? If she pretended to distress? To fear? To remorse? He couldn’t. It would be useless for him even to try. She screwed up her face into a little grin, remembering that even if protestations were to fail, there were other ways.
And, too, there was God.

===Chinese===
她的目光落在小白桌上方的日历上。十一月十日。轮船“奥斯卡二世”号（*Oscar II*）今天启航。昨天她还半心半意地想搭它走的。昨天。多么遥远！
随着对昨天的回想，罗伯特·安德森也浮上心头，还伴着一种昂扬之感，复仇之感。她已使自己落到无需他帮助的地步。她已使自己去永远不再向他求告。她本能地知道，他会震惊。会痛心。甚至会伤得很重。好啊，随他去！
一种刻不容缓的念头突然缠住了她。她必须抓紧。上午快过完了。她打算——如果办得到的话——今天就把婚结了。可她一起身，一阵剧烈的恐惧便攫住了她，迫使她重新躺下。因为她想到，她可能会失败。可能会无力面对那个局面。那就太可怕了。但她又平静下来。他那样一个天真的人，怎么可能招架得住她？倘若她装出可怜？装出害怕？装出悔恨？他不可能。他连试都不必试，试也是白费。她扭起脸，咧嘴一笑，心想纵然恳求不成，也还有别的法子。
再说，还有上帝呢。

## XXII / 二十二

===Original===
And so in the confusion of seductive repentance Helga Crane was married to the grandiloquent Reverend Mr. Pleasant Green, that rattish yellow man, who had so kindly, so unctuously, proffered his escort to her hotel on the memorable night of her conversion. With him she willingly, even eagerly, left the sins and temptations of New York behind her to, as he put it, “labor in the vineyard of the Lord” in the tiny Alabama town where he was pastor to a scattered and primitive flock. And where, as the wife of the preacher, she was a person of relative importance. Only relative.
Helga did not hate him, the town, or the people. No. Not for a long time.
As always, at first the novelty of the thing, the change, fascinated her. There was a recurrence of the feeling that now, at last, she had found a place for herself, that she was really living. And she had her religion, which in her new status as a preacher’s wife had of necessity become real to her. She believed in it. Because in its coming it had brought this other thing, this anaesthetic satisfaction for her senses. Hers was, she declared to herself, a truly spiritual union. This one time in her life, she was convinced, she had not clutched a shadow and missed the actuality. She felt compensated for all previous humiliations and disappointments and was glad. If she remembered that she had had something like this feeling before, she put the unwelcome memory from her with the thought: “This time I know I’m right. This time it will last.”

===Chinese===
就这样，在诱人的悔罪的迷乱中，海尔加·克雷恩嫁给了夸夸其谈的普莱森特·格林牧师——那个獐头鼠目的黄皮肤男人，在她皈依的那个难忘的夜晚，是他那样殷勤、那样油滑地提出护送她回旅馆。她心甘情愿、甚至满怀热切地随他抛下了纽约的罪恶与诱惑，照他的说法，到阿拉巴马小镇去“在主的葡萄园里劳作”；在那座小镇上，他牧养着一群零散而未开化的羊。而作为传道人的妻子，她在那里是个相对要紧的人物。只是相对而已。
海尔加并不恨他，不恨那小镇，也不恨那里的人。不恨。在很长一段时间里都不恨。
一如既往，起初这件事的新奇、这种变化令她着迷。那种感觉又回来了：如今她终于为自己找到了一个位置，她是在真正地生活。她还有了她的宗教——以传道人妻子这新的身份，这宗教对她而言必然成了实实在在的东西。她信仰它。因为在它降临之时，还带来了另一样东西，一份使她感官餍足的麻醉。她对自己宣称，这是一场真正灵性的结合。她相信，生平这一次，她抓住的不是影子，错过的不是实体。她觉得此前一切的羞辱与失意都得到了补偿，于是她高兴。倘若记起从前也曾有过类似的感觉，她便用一个念头把这不请自来的记忆推开：“这一次我知道自己是对的。这一次会天长地久。”

===Original===
Eagerly she accepted everything, even that bleak air of poverty which, in some curious way, regards itself as virtuous, for no other reason than that it is poor. And in her first hectic enthusiasm she intended and planned to do much good to her husband’s parishioners. Her young joy and zest for the uplifting of her fellow men came back to her. She meant to subdue the cleanly scrubbed ugliness of her own surroundings to soft inoffensive beauty, and to help the other women to do likewise. Too, she would help them with their clothes, tactfully point out that sunbonnets, no matter how gay, and aprons, no matter how frilly, were not quite the proper things for Sunday church wear. There would be a sewing circle. She visualized herself instructing the children, who seemed most of the time to run wild, in ways of gentler deportment. She was anxious to be a true helpmate, for in her heart was a feeling of obligation, of humble gratitude.
In her ardor and sincerity Helga even made some small beginnings. True, she was not very successful in this matter of innovations. When she went about to try to interest the women in what she considered more appropriate clothing and in inexpensive ways of improving their homes according to her ideas of beauty, she was met, always, with smiling agreement and good-natured promises. “Yuh all is right, Mis’ Green,” and “Ah suttinly will, Mis’ Green,” fell courteously on her ear at each visit.
She was unaware that afterwards they would shake their heads sullenly over their washtubs and ironing boards.
