And that among themselves they talked with amusement, or with anger, of “dat uppity, meddlin’ No’the’nah,” and “pore Reve’end,” who in their opinion “would ’a done bettah to a ma’ied Clementine Richards.” Knowing, as she did, nothing of this, Helga was unperturbed. But even had she known, she would not have been disheartened. The fact that it was difficult but increased her eagerness, and made the doing of it seem only the more worth while. Sometimes she would smile to think how changed she was.
And she was humble too. Even with Clementine Richards, a strapping black beauty of magnificent Amazon proportions and bold shining eyes of jet-like hardness. A person of awesome appearance. All chains, strings of beads, jingling bracelets, flying ribbons, feathery neckpieces, and flowery hats. Clementine was inclined to treat Helga with an only partially concealed contemptuousness, considering her a poor thing without style, and without proper understanding of the worth and greatness of the man, Clementine’s own adored pastor, whom Helga had somehow had the astounding good luck to marry. Clementine’s admiration of the Reverend Mr. Pleasant Green was open. Helga was at first astonished. Until she learned that there was really no reason why it should be concealed. Everybody was aware of it. Besides, open adoration was the prerogative, the almost religious duty, of the female portion of the flock. If this unhidden and exaggerated approval contributed to his already oversized pomposity, so much the better. It was what they expected, liked, wanted. The greater his own sense of superiority became, the more flattered they were by his notice and small attentions, the more they cast at him killing glances, the more they hung enraptured on his words.

===Chinese===
她热切地接受了一切，甚至接受了贫穷那副冷清的做派——它以某种奇妙的方式自视为有德，仅仅因为它穷。在最初那阵火热的兴致里，她打算并且筹划着为丈夫的教区居民做许多好事。她那份年轻人的欢乐、那份提振同胞的热忱，又回到了她身上。她打算把自己周围那种洗刷得干干净净的丑陋，驯化为一种柔和而不刺目的美，并帮助别的女人也照样办。还有，她要在衣着上帮她们，婉转地指出：遮阳帽不管多鲜亮，围裙不管多花哨，终究不是星期日进教堂的正经装束。还要办一个缝纫会。她想象自己教导那些孩子——他们多半时候仿佛都在撒野——学些温和的举止。她渴望做一个真正的贤内助，因为她心中怀着一种亏欠之感，一种谦卑的感激。
凭着这份热忱与真诚，海尔加甚至真的开了几个小小的头。不错，在革新一事上她并不很成功。当她四处奔走，想引起女人们对更得体的衣着的兴趣，想教她们照她的美的观念花不了几个钱来改善各自的家时，她遇到的永远是微笑的赞同和好脾气的许诺。每次登门，“您说得对，格林太太”“俺一定照办，格林太太”总是客客气气地落进她耳朵里。
她不知道，过后她们会在洗衣盆和烫衣板前阴沉沉地摇头。也不知道她们私下里带着好笑、或带着气恼，议论“那个自以为了不起、爱管闲事的北方佬”，还有“可怜的牧师”，说照她们看，他“还不如娶了克莱门蒂娜·理查兹（Clementine Richards）”。海尔加对这一切一无所知，所以安之若素。可即便她知道了，也不会气馁。事情难办，反倒更添她的热切，使做成它显得愈发值得。有时她想到自己竟变了这么多，会微微一笑。
她还是谦卑的。甚至在克莱门蒂娜·理查兹面前也是如此。那是个健壮的黑美人，一副魁伟的亚马逊身量，一双大胆发亮的、黑玉般坚硬的眼睛。一个模样吓人的人物。浑身上下都是链子、珠串、叮当作响的手镯、飞舞的缎带、羽毛围领和鲜花似的帽子。克莱门蒂娜待海尔加总带着一份只遮掩了一半的轻蔑，觉得她是个没有款段的东西，也不懂得那个男人、她自己敬爱的牧师的价值与伟大——而海尔加不知怎的竟有天大的福气嫁给了他。克莱门蒂娜对普莱森特·格林牧师的倾慕是公开的。海尔加起初很吃惊。直到她明白，这倾慕实在没有遮掩的必要。人人心里都有数。再说，公开的崇拜本是羊群中女性那一半的特权，几乎是宗教义务。这种毫不掩饰的、添油加醋的推崇若是给他那本已超标的自负再添几分，那倒更好。这正是她们所指望、所喜欢、所乐见的。他自己的优越感越膨胀，她们就越为他的一顾和些微小殷勤而受宠若惊，就越朝他抛去致命的眼风，就越如醉如痴地悬在他的话头上。

===Original===
In the days before her conversion, with its subsequent blurring of her sense of humor, Helga might have amused herself by tracing the relation of this constant ogling and flattering on the proverbially large families of preachers; the often disastrous effect on their wives of this constant stirring of the senses by extraneous women. Now, however, she did not even think of it.
She was too busy. Every minute of the day was full. Necessarily. And to Helga this was a new experience. She was charmed by it. To be mistress in one’s own house, to have a garden, and chickens, and a pig; to have a husband﻿—and to be “right with God”﻿—what pleasure did that other world which she had left contain that could surpass these? Here, she had found, she was sure, the intangible thing for which, indefinitely, always she had craved. It had received embodiment.
Everything contributed to her gladness in living. And so for a time she loved everything and everyone. Or thought she did. Even the weather. And it was truly lovely. By day a glittering gold sun was set in an unbelievably bright sky. In the evening silver buds sprouted in a Chinese blue sky, and the warm day was softly soothed by a slight, cool breeze. And night! Night, when a languid moon peeped through the wide-opened windows of her little house, a little mockingly, it may be. Always at night’s approach Helga was bewildered by a disturbing medley of feelings. Challenge. Anticipation. And a small fear.
In the morning she was serene again. Peace had returned. And she could go happily, inexpertly, about the humble tasks of her household, cooking, dish-washing, sweeping, dusting, mending, and darning. And there was the garden. When she worked there, she felt that life was utterly filled with the glory and the marvel of God.
Helga did not reason about this feeling, as she did not at that time reason about anything. It was enough that it was there, coloring all her thoughts and acts. It endowed the four rooms of her ugly brown house with a kindly radiance, obliterating the stark bareness of its white plaster walls and the nakedness of its uncovered painted floors. It even softened the choppy lines of the shiny oak furniture and subdued the awesome horribleness of the religious pictures.

===Chinese===
在皈依之前——皈依随之而来的，是她幽默感的钝化——海尔加或许会拿这个来取乐：探究这种没完没了的媚眼与逢迎，同传道人家里出了名的人丁兴旺之间有什么关系；探究外面的女人这样没完没了地撩动他们的感官，对他们的妻子又常有怎样的灾殃。然而如今，她连想都不想它了。
她太忙了。白天的每一分钟都排得满满的。必然如此。对海尔加来说，这是一种崭新的体验。她为之着迷。在自己家里做主妇，有一个园子，有鸡，有一口猪；有一个丈夫——还“与上帝和好”了——她抛下的那个世界里，还有什么快乐能胜过这些？在这里，她确信自己终于找到了那件无形的东西，那件她一直说不清地渴求着的东西。它有了血肉之躯。
一切都在为她的生之欢乐添彩。于是有一段日子，她爱一切、爱每一个人。或者自以为爱。连天气也爱。天气也真是可爱。白天，一轮金光灿灿的太阳镶在亮得叫人不敢相信的天空里。傍晚，银色的花蕾在瓷蓝色的天空中绽出，白日的暖热被一缕轻轻的凉风温柔抚平。还有夜！夜，当一轮慵懒的月亮从小屋敞开的窗口窥进来——也许还带着几分揶揄。每当夜幕将临，海尔加总被一团纷乱的情绪弄得心旌摇荡。挑战。期待。还有一点小小的恐惧。
到了早晨她又安详了。平静回来了。她可以快快活活、笨手笨脚地操持家中的粗活：做饭、洗碗、扫地、掸灰、缝补、织补。还有园子。在园子里劳作时，她感到生命里满满充盈着上帝的荣耀与神奇。
海尔加不去推究这种感觉，正如她那时对任何事都不加推究。它在，就够了，给她的全部思想和行动都染上色彩。它给她那丑陋的褐色房子里的四个房间镀上一层慈祥的光辉，抹掉了白灰墙刺眼的空裸和没有铺盖的漆地板的赤裸。它甚至柔化了亮闪闪的橡木家具那些生硬的线条，压下了宗教画那种令人生畏的丑陋。

===Original===
And all the other houses and cabins shared in this illumination. And the people. The dark undecorated women unceasingly concerned with the actual business of life, its rounds of births and christenings, of loves and marriages, of deaths and funerals, were to Helga miraculously beautiful. The smallest, dirtiest, brown child, barefooted in the fields or muddy roads, was to her an emblem of the wonder of life, of love, and of God’s goodness.
For the preacher, her husband, she had a feeling of gratitude, amounting almost to sin. Beyond that, she thought of him not at all. But she was not conscious that she had shut him out from her mind. Besides, what need to think of him? He was there. She was at peace, and secure. Surely their two lives were one, and the companionship in the Lord’s grace so perfect that to think about it would be tempting providence. She had done with soul-searching.
What did it matter that he consumed his food, even the softest varieties, audibly? What did it matter that, though he did no work with his hands, not even in the garden, his fingernails were always rimmed with black? What did it matter that he failed to wash his fat body, or to shift his clothing, as often as Helga herself did? There were things that more than outweighed these. In the certainty of his goodness, his righteousness, his holiness, Helga somehow overcame her first disgust at the odor of sweat and stale garments. She was even able to be unaware of it. Herself, Helga had come to look upon as a finicky, showy thing of unnecessary prejudices and fripperies. And when she sat in the dreary structure, which had once been a stable belonging to the estate of a wealthy horse-racing man and about which the odor of manure still clung, now the church and social center of the Negroes of the town, and heard him expound with verbal extravagance the gospel of blood and love, of hell and heaven, of fire and gold streets, pounding with clenched fists the frail table before him or shaking those fists in the faces of the congregation like direct personal threats, or pacing wildly back and forth and even sometimes shedding great tears as he besought them to repent, she was, she told herself, proud and gratified that he belonged to her. In some strange way she was able to ignore the atmosphere of self-satisfaction which poured from him like gas from a leaking pipe.
And night came at the end of every day. Emotional, palpitating, amorous, all that was living in her sprang like rank weeds at the tingling thought of night, with a vitality so strong that it devoured all shoots of reason.

===Chinese===
其余所有的房子和木屋也都分享着这份光照。人也是。那些黑黑的、不加修饰的女人，无休无止地操劳着人生实际的事务——一轮又一轮的出生与洗礼、恋爱与婚嫁、死亡与丧葬——在海尔加眼里美得如同奇迹。最小、最脏的棕色孩子，光着脚在田里或泥路上跑，在她看来也是生命之奇迹、爱之奇迹、上帝之仁慈的表征。
对这位传道人，她的丈夫，她怀有一种感激，深得近乎罪过。除此之外，她根本不去想他。可她并不觉得自己已把他关在心门之外。再说，想他做什么？他在那儿。她安宁，而且稳当。她们两人的生命无疑已融为一体，在主的恩典里的这份相伴如此圆满，再去细想反倒是对天意的试探。她已同灵魂的自剖一刀两断了。
他吃东西，连最软和的也要出声地嚼，有什么要紧？他从来不动手干活，连园子里也不弄一下，指甲缝里却总是黑着一圈，有什么要紧？他不像海尔加自己那样勤地洗他那一身肥肉、那样勤地换衣裳，有什么要紧？有的是东西，分量远远压过这些。凭着对他的善良、他的正直、他的圣洁的确信，海尔加不知怎么竟克服了最初对汗味和馊衣味的恶心。她甚至能够对它毫无知觉。至于她自己，海尔加已渐渐看成一个挑剔的、花哨的东西，一身多余的偏见和浮华。当她坐在那座阴沉沉的建筑里——它原是有钱赛马人庄园的一座马厩，四周至今萦绕着粪肥的气味，如今是镇上黑人的教堂和社交中心——听他言辞夸张地阐讲血与爱、地狱与天堂、烈火与黄金街的福音，用攥紧的拳头捶打面前那张单薄的桌子，或是把拳头挥到会众的脸前，像直接的私人威胁，或是发疯似的来回踱步，有时甚至淌着大颗大颗的眼泪恳求他们悔改——这时她对自己说，他属于她，她骄傲而且满足。凭着某种奇异的法子，她竟能对他周身散发的那股自满之气视而不见，那气息就像漏气管子里冒出的煤气。
每一天结束时，夜就来了。多情、悸动、炽爱——想到夜，她体内一切活着的东西便像疯长的野草一般蹿起来，生命力那样强横，把理性的嫩芽吞噬得一干二净。

## XXIII / 二十三

===Original===
After the first exciting months Helga was too driven, too occupied, and too sick to carry out any of the things for which she had made such enthusiastic plans, or even to care that she had made only slight progress toward their accomplishment. For she, who had never thought of her body save as something on which to hang lovely fabrics, had now constantly to think of it. It had persistently to be pampered to secure from it even a little service. Always she felt extraordinarily and annoyingly ill, having forever to be sinking into chairs. Or, if she was out, to be pausing by the roadside, clinging desperately to some convenient fence or tree, waiting for the horrible nausea and hateful faintness to pass. The light, carefree days of the past, when she had not felt heavy and reluctant or weak and spent, receded more and more and with increasing vagueness, like a dream passing from a faulty memory.
The children used her up. There were already three of them, all born within the short space of twenty months. Two great healthy twin boys, whose lovely bodies were to Helga like rare figures carved out of amber, and in whose sleepy and mysterious black eyes all that was puzzling, evasive, and aloof in life seemed to find expression. No matter how often or how long she looked at these two small sons of hers, never did she lose a certain delicious feeling in which were mingled pride, tenderness, and exaltation. And there was a girl, sweet, delicate, and flower-like. Not so healthy or so loved as the boys, but still miraculously her own proud and cherished possession.
So there was no time for the pursuit of beauty, or for the uplifting of other harassed and teeming women, or for the instruction of their neglected children.

===Chinese===
最初那几个月的兴奋过去之后，海尔加已被驱使得太急、被占得太满、病得太重，再也办不成她曾那样兴致勃勃计划过的任何一件事，甚至连自己进展甚微也顾不上在意了。因为她这个从不把身体当作别的、只当它是挂漂亮料子的架子的人，如今不得不时时惦着它。得一个劲儿地娇惯它，才能从它那里挤出一点服务。她总感到异乎寻常、令人气恼的难受，动不动就得跌坐进椅子里。若是出了门，就得在路边停下来，死死抓住手边方便的栅栏或树，等着那可怕的恶心和可憎的晕眩过去。从前那些轻快无忧的日子——那时她不曾感到沉重怠惰，也不曾虚弱乏力——越来越远，越来越模糊，像一场正从残缺记忆中消逝的梦。
孩子们把她耗干了。眼下已经有三个了，全都是在短短二十个月里生下的。两个健壮可爱的小孪生男孩，他们那可爱的身体在海尔加眼里就像用琥珀雕出的珍品，而在他们惺忪而神秘的黑眼睛里，人生中一切费解的、闪避的、超然的东西仿佛都找到了表情。不论她看这两个小儿子多少遍、看多久，从不曾失去那种美妙的感觉——里面掺和着骄傲、温柔与狂喜。还有一个女儿，甜美、纤弱、像花一般。不如男孩们壮实，也不如男孩们受宠，却仍奇迹般是她自己引以为傲的心爱之物。
于是，追寻美也好，提振别些操劳而多产的女人也好，教导她们失于管教的孩子也好，都再没有时间了。

===Original===
Her husband was still, as he had always been, deferentially kind and incredulously proud of her﻿—and verbally encouraging. Helga tried not to see that he had rather lost any personal interest in her, except for the short spaces between the times when she was preparing for or recovering from childbirth. She shut her eyes to the fact that his encouragement had become a little platitudinous, limited mostly to “The Lord will look out for you,” “We must accept what God sends,” or “My mother had nine children and was thankful for every one.” If she was inclined to wonder a little just how they were to manage with another child on the way, he would point out to her that her doubt and uncertainty were a stupendous ingratitude. Had not the good God saved her soul from hellfire and eternal damnation? Had He not in His great kindness given her three small lives to raise up for His glory? Had He not showered her with numerous other mercies (evidently too numerous to be named separately)?
“You must,” the Reverend Mr. Pleasant Green would say unctuously, “trust the Lord more fully, Helga.”
This pabulum did not irritate her. Perhaps it was the fact that the preacher was, now, not so much at home that even lent to it a measure of real comfort. For the adoring women of his flock, noting how with increasing frequency their pastor’s house went unswept and undusted, his children unwashed, and his wife untidy, took pleasant pity on him and invited him often to tasty orderly meals, specially prepared for him, in their own clean houses.
Helga, looking about in helpless dismay and sick disgust at the disorder around her, the permanent assembly of partly emptied medicine bottles on the clock-shelf, the perpetual array of drying baby-clothes on the chairbacks, the constant debris of broken toys on the floor, the unceasing litter of half-dead flowers on the table, dragged in by the toddling twins from the forlorn garden, failed to blame him for the thoughtless selfishness of these absences. And, she was thankful, whenever possible, to be relieved from the ordeal of cooking. There were times when, having had to retreat from the kitchen in lumbering haste with her sensitive nose gripped between tightly squeezing fingers, she had been sure that the greatest kindness that God could ever show to her would be to free her forever from the sight and smell of food.

===Chinese===
她的丈夫一如既往，依旧恭恭敬敬地和气，依旧难以置信地为她骄傲——嘴上也依旧鼓励。海尔加尽量不去看这一点：他对她本人已相当失去兴趣，只剩下她临产前与产后将养的那几小段空当。她闭眼不看这个事实：他的鼓励已变得有点陈腐，多半不外乎“主会照应你的”“上帝赐什么，我们就受什么”，或者“我母亲生了九个孩子，每一个都感恩”。倘若她忍不住稍稍琢磨一下，又有一个孩子在肚里，日子可怎么撑，他便向她指出，她的疑惑和不安是一种天大的忘恩。仁慈的上帝不是把她的灵魂从地狱之火和永罚里救出来了么？他不是以他的大慈大悲赐给她三条小性命，让她养育成人、彰显他的荣耀么？他不是还赐给她数不清的其他恩典（显然多得不计其数、无法一一列举）么？
“你必须，”普莱森特·格林牧师会油腔滑调地说，“更全心全意地信靠主，海尔加。”
这类精神食粮并不惹恼她。也许正因为传道人如今不大在家，反倒给这些话添了几分实在的安慰。因为他羊群里那些崇拜他的女人，眼见牧师的家越来越多日不扫、不掸，孩子不洗，太太蓬头垢面，便满心怜惜地常请他到自己干净的家里，吃特意为他做的可口齐整的饭食。
海尔加无望而厌恶地环顾四周的凌乱——钟架上一排永远不散的半空药瓶，椅背上常年晾着的婴儿衣裳，地板上层出不穷的破玩具残骸，桌上没完没了的半死不活的花，那是蹒跚学步的双胞胎从荒芜的园子里拖进来的——却怪不得他这些抛下家的粗心自私。而且只要能免掉做饭这场酷刑，她还感激不尽呢。有时她不得不捏紧手指夹住敏感的鼻子，笨拙而仓皇地从厨房里败退出来，那时她确信，上帝能赐给她的最大的仁慈，就是让她永远摆脱食物的形与味。

===Original===
How, she wondered, did other women, other mothers, manage? Could it be possible that, while presenting such smiling and contented faces, they were all always on the edge of health? All always worn out and apprehensive? Or was it only she, a poor weak city-bred thing, who felt that the strain of what the Reverend Mr. Pleasant Green had so often gently and patiently reminded her was a natural thing, an act of God, was almost unendurable?
One day on her round of visiting﻿—a church duty, to be done no matter how miserable one was﻿—she summoned up sufficient boldness to ask several women how they felt, how they managed. The answers were a resigned shrug, or an amused snort, or an upward rolling of eyeballs with a mention of “de Lawd” looking after us all.
“ ’Tain’t nothin’, nothin’ at all, chile,” said one, Sary Jones, who, as Helga knew, had had six children in about as many years. “Yuh all takes it too ha’d. Jes’ remembah et’s natu’al fo’ a ’oman to hab chilluns an’ don’ fret so.”
“But,” protested Helga, “I’m always so tired and half sick. That can’t be natural.”
“Laws, chile, we’s all ti’ed. An’ Ah reckons we’s all gwine a be ti’ed till kingdom come. Jes’ make de bes’ of et, honey. Jes’ make de bes’ yuh can.”

===Chinese===
她纳闷，别的女人、别的母亲，是怎么撑过来的？难道可能吗——她们摆出那一张张微笑知足的脸，其实人人都常年悬在健康的边缘？人人都精疲力竭、忧心忡忡？还是说，只有她，只有这个城里娇养出来的可怜的弱东西，才觉得普莱森特·格林牧师常常温言耐心提醒她说是天性使然、是上帝作为的那件事，那份煎熬几乎叫人受不住？
有一天，在她巡回探访的路上——这是教会的职分，人再难受也得做——她鼓足了胆子，问几个女人身子觉得怎样，日子怎么撑。回答她的，或是听天由命的一耸肩，或是好笑的一声嗤，或是眼珠往上一翻，提一句“主”自会照看咱们大家。
“没啥，压根儿没啥，孩子，”其中一个说，她叫萨丽·琼斯（Sary Jones），海尔加知道，她在差不多同样多的年头里生了六个孩子。“你把这事瞧得太重啦。只消记着，女人生孩子是天经地义，别这么愁。”
“可是，”海尔加不服，“我总是这么累、这么半病不恹的。那总不可能是天经地义吧。”
“哎呀，孩子，咱们都累。俺看咱们还得累到世界末日哩。将就着过吧，宝贝。能将就多好就将就多好。”

===Original===
Helga sighed, turning her nose away from the steaming coffee which her hostess had placed for her and against which her squeamish stomach was about to revolt. At the moment the compensations of immortality seemed very shadowy and very far away.
“Jes’ remembah,” Sary went on, staring sternly into Helga’s thin face, “we all gits ouah res’ by an’ by. In de nex’ worl’ we’s all recompense’. Jes’ put yo’ trus’ in de Sabioah.”
Looking at the confident face of the little bronze figure on the opposite side of the immaculately spread table, Helga had a sensation of shame that she should be less than content. Why couldn’t she be as trusting and as certain that her troubles would not overwhelm her as Sary Jones was? Sary, who in all likelihood had toiled every day of her life since early childhood except on those days, totalling perhaps sixty, following the birth of her six children. And who by dint of superhuman saving had somehow succeeded in feeding and clothing them and sending them all to school. Before her Helga felt humbled and oppressed by the sense of her own unworthiness and lack of sufficient faith.
“Thanks, Sary,” she said, rising in retreat from the coffee, “you’ve done me a world of good. I’m really going to try to be more patient.”

===Chinese===
海尔加叹了口气，把鼻子从女主人给她端上的冒着热气的咖啡那里转开——她那娇气的胃正要对它造反。此刻，不朽的报偿显得非常虚无缥缈，非常遥远。
“只消记着，”萨丽接着说，严厉地盯着海尔加瘦削的脸，“咱们早晚都能歇着。到了那个世界，咱们全都得着报偿。只管信靠救主吧。”
望着铺得干干净净的桌子对面那张小小的古铜色面孔上的笃定神气，海尔加羞愧起来，恨自己竟不知足。她为什么就不能像萨丽·琼斯那样信靠，那样确信苦难压不垮自己？萨丽，这一辈子多半从孩提起天天劳苦，只在生那六个孩子之后歇过几日，加起来兴许才六十天。她凭着超人的节俭，居然把孩子们喂大了、穿暖了，还个个送进了学堂。在她面前，海尔加只觉卑微压抑，为自己的不够格、为自己信心的不足而羞惭。
“谢谢你，萨丽。”她说着起身，从那杯咖啡边撤退，“你真是给我开了窍。我真要试着更耐心些。”

===Original===
So, though with growing yearning she longed for the great ordinary things of life, hunger, sleep, freedom from pain, she resigned herself to the doing without them. The possibility of alleviating her burdens by a greater faith became lodged in her mind. She gave herself up to it. It *did* help. And the beauty of leaning on the wisdom of God, of trusting, gave to her a queer sort of satisfaction. Faith was really quite easy. One had only to yield. To ask no questions. The more weary, the more weak, she became, the easier it was. Her religion was to her a kind of protective coloring, shielding her from the cruel light of an unbearable reality.
This utter yielding in faith to what had been sent her found her favor, too, in the eyes of her neighbors. Her husband’s flock began to approve and commend this submission and humility to a superior wisdom. The womenfolk spoke more kindly and more affectionately of the preacher’s Northern wife. “Pore Mis’ Green, wid all dem small chilluns at once. She suah do hab it ha’d. An’ she don’ nebah complains an’ frets no mo’e. Jes’ trus’ in de Lawd lak de Good Book say. Mighty sweet lil’ ’oman too.”
Helga didn’t bother much about the preparations for the coming child. Actually and metaphorically she bowed her head before God, trusting in Him to see her through. Secretly she was glad that she had not to worry about herself or anything. It was a relief to be able to put the entire responsibility on someone else.

===Chinese===
就这样，尽管她怀着与日俱增的渴望，渴念着人生那些寻常的大事——饥饿、睡眠、无病无痛——她还是认了命，将就着没有它们的日子。凭着更大的信心来减轻重负的可能性，在她心里扎了根。她把自己交给了它。它*确实*有用。倚仗上帝的智慧、凡事信靠的那种美，给了她一种古怪的满足。信仰实在很容易。人只须顺服。只须什么也不问。她越疲惫、越虚弱，就越容易。她的宗教之于她，成了一种保护色，把她遮在难以忍受的现实那残酷的光线之外。
这种对所受之命的彻底的信服顺受，也让她在邻里眼中得了欢心。她丈夫的羊群开始赞许并夸奖她对更高智慧的这种恭顺与谦卑。女人们说起传道人的北方太太，口吻也更和善、更亲热了。“可怜的格林太太，一下子摊上这么多小娃儿。她真够受的。可她再也不抱怨、不发愁了。就像圣书上说的，只管信靠主。也是个怪招人疼的小媳妇哩。”
对即将来临的孩子，海尔加不大费神张罗什么。无论实际上还是比喻意义上，她都在上帝面前低下了头，信靠他会保她渡过。她暗自庆幸，不必再为自己或任何事操心。能把全部责任推到别人身上，是一种解脱。

## XXIV / 二十四

===Original===
It began, this next childbearing, during the morning services of a breathless hot Sunday while the fervent choir soloist was singing: “Ah am freed of mah sorrow,” and lasted far into the small hours of Tuesday morning. It seemed, for some reason, not to go off just right. And when, after that long frightfulness, the fourth little dab of amber humanity which Helga had contributed to a despised race was held before her for maternal approval, she failed entirely to respond properly to this sop of consolation for the suffering and horror through which she had passed. There was from her no pleased, proud smile, no loving, possessive gesture, no manifestation of interest in the important matters of sex and weight. Instead she deliberately closed her eyes, mutely shutting out the sickly infant, its smiling father, the soiled midwife, the curious neighbors, and the tousled room.
A week she lay so. Silent and listless. Ignoring food, the clamoring children, the comings and goings of solicitous, kindhearted women, her hovering husband, and all of life about her. The neighbors were puzzled. The Reverend Mr. Pleasant Green was worried. The midwife was frightened.
On the floor, in and out among the furniture and under her bed, the twins played. Eager to help, the church-women crowded in and, meeting there others on the same laudable errand, stayed to gossip and to wonder. Anxiously the preacher sat, Bible in hand, beside his wife’s bed, or in a nervous half-guilty manner invited the congregated parishioners to join him in prayer for the healing of their sister. Then, kneeling, they would beseech God to stretch out His all-powerful hand on behalf of the afflicted one, softly at first, but with rising vehemence, accompanied by moans and tears, until it seemed that the God to whom they prayed must in mercy to the sufferer grant relief. If only so that she might rise up and escape from the tumult, the heat, and the smell.

===Chinese===
这一次分娩，是在一个闷热得喘不过气的星期日的晨间礼拜中开始的，那时唱诗班热情的独唱者正唱着：“我已脱离我的忧伤。”它一直持续到星期二凌晨的深夜。不知怎的，事情进行得似乎不太顺当。而当那漫长的恐怖终于过去，海尔加奉献给一个受轻蔑的种族的第四小块琥珀色血肉，被举到她面前请做母亲的认可时，她全然没能对这块慰藉的甜头作出应有的反应——为了她经受的痛苦与惊怖，这原是给她的补偿。她脸上没有欣慰骄傲的微笑，没有慈爱而据为己有的表示，对性别和体重这类重大事项也没有一点兴致的流露。她反而存心闭上了眼睛，默默地把那病弱的婴儿、微笑着的父亲、污迹斑斑的接生婆、好奇的邻人，还有那凌乱的房间，统统关在外面。
她这样躺了一个星期。沉默而倦怠。不理会饭食，不理会吵闹的孩子，不理会那些关切热肠的女人来来去去，不理会守在旁边的丈夫，也不理会她周围的一切生活。邻居们困惑了。普莱森特·格林牧师发愁了。接生婆吓坏了。
地板上，双胞胎在家具之间钻进钻出，在她床底下玩耍。急于帮忙的教会女眷们挤进屋来，遇见别的也是出于同样善举的人，便留下来闲话、惊叹。传道人手捧《圣经》，忧心忡忡地坐在妻子床边，或是带着紧张而近乎负疚的神气，请聚拢的教友同他一起为姐妹的痊愈祈祷。于是他们跪下来，恳求上帝向受难者伸出他全能的手，起初轻声，继而愈来愈激烈，伴着呻吟与眼泪，直到仿佛他们祈祷的那位上帝出于对受难者的怜悯，非应允不可。哪怕只为了让她能爬起来逃离这喧嚣、这闷热和这气味呢。

===Original===
Helga, however, was unconcerned, undisturbed by the commotion about her. It was all part of the general unreality. Nothing reached her. Nothing penetrated the kind darkness into which her bruised spirit had retreated. Even that red-letter event, the coming to see her of the old white physician from downtown, who had for a long time stayed talking gravely to her husband, drew from her no interest. Nor for days was she aware that a stranger, a nurse from Mobile, had been added to her household, a brusquely efficient woman who produced order out of chaos and quiet out of bedlam. Neither did the absence of the children, removed by good neighbors at Miss Hartley’s insistence, impress her. While she had gone down into that appalling blackness of pain, the ballast of her brain had got loose and she hovered for a long time somewhere in that delightful borderland on the edge of unconsciousness, an enchanted and blissful place where peace and incredible quiet encompassed her.
After weeks she grew better, returned to earth, set her reluctant feet to the hard path of life again.
“Well, here you are!” announced Miss Hartley in her slightly harsh voice one afternoon just before the fall of evening. She had for some time been standing at the bedside gazing down at Helga with an intent speculative look.
“Yes,” Helga agreed in a thin little voice, “I’m back.” The truth was that she had been back for some hours. Purposely she had lain silent and still, wanting to linger forever in that serene haven, that effortless calm where nothing was expected of her. There she could watch the figures of the past drift by. There was her mother, whom she had loved from a distance and finally so scornfully blamed, who appeared as she had always remembered her, unbelievably beautiful, young, and remote. Robert Anderson, questioning, purposely detached, affecting, as she realized now, her life in a remarkably cruel degree; for at last she understood clearly how deeply, how passionately, she must have loved him. Anne, lovely, secure, wise, selfish. Axel Olsen, conceited, worldly, spoiled. Audrey Denney, placid, taking quietly and without fuss the things which she wanted. James Vayle, snobbish, smug, servile. Mrs. Hayes-Rore, important, kind, determined. The Dahls, rich, correct, climbing. Flashingly, fragmentarily, other long-forgotten figures, women in gay fashionable frocks and men in formal black and white, glided by in bright rooms to distant, vaguely familiar music.

===Chinese===
然而海尔加漠不关心，周围的骚动扰不动她。这一切都是那普遍的虚幻的一部分。什么也触及不了她。什么也透不进那片慈祥的黑暗，她受伤的灵魂已退进那里去了。就连城里来的老白人医生亲自出诊——他在她丈夫面前郑重地谈了很久——这样的大事，也没能引出她一点兴味。接连许多天她也不知道家里添了一个陌生人，一位从莫比尔（Mobile）来的护士，一个风风火火、利落能干的女人，能从混乱中弄出秩序，从喧闹中弄出安静。孩子们被好心的邻居接走了——这是哈特利小姐（Miss Hartley）的主意——他们的不在也同样没在她心里留下印象。当她坠入那骇人的疼痛的黑渊时，她头脑里的压舱石松脱了，她久久悬浮在意识边缘那片美妙的边界地带，一个迷人而极乐的地方，那里安宁与难以置信的静谧将她团团裹住。
几个星期后她好些了，回到地上，把不愿举步的双脚重新踏上人生的崎岖之路。
“哟，你可回来了！”一天傍晚将临的下午，哈特利小姐用她那略带沙哑的声音宣布。她已经在床边站了好一会儿，目光专注而思量地俯视着海尔加。
“是的，”海尔加用细细的声音应道，“我回来了。”实情是，她回来已有好几个钟头了。她存心静静躺着不动，想永远流连在那安宁的避风港里，那无所期求的从容里——在那里，谁也不对她有任何要求。在那里她可以望着往昔的身影一一漂过。有她的母亲，那个她曾经远远爱着、最终又如此轻蔑地责怪的人，出现在眼前的仍是她记忆中一直的模样，美得叫人不敢相信，年轻，而遥远。罗伯特·安德森，探问的，故意超然的，如她如今才明白的，以一种惊人残忍的程度左右过她的生命；因为她终于清清楚楚地明白，自己曾多么深、多么炽烈地爱过他。安妮，可爱，安稳，聪明，自私。阿克塞尔·奥尔森，自负，世故，娇惯。奥德丽·德纳姆，恬静，悄悄地、不动声色地取走她想要的东西。詹姆斯·维尔，势利，自满，卑屈。海斯-罗尔太太，要紧，和善，果断。达尔一家，有钱，规矩，向上爬。另有一些久已淡忘的身影，闪闪烁烁、零零碎碎地滑过——穿鲜艳时兴衣裙的女人和穿黑白礼服的男人，在明亮的房间里，伴着遥远而依稀熟悉的音乐。

===Original===
It was refreshingly delicious, this immersion in the past. But it was finished now. It was over. The words of her husband, the Reverend Mr. Pleasant Green, who had been standing at the window looking mournfully out at the scorched melon-patch, ruined because Helga had been ill so long and unable to tend it, were confirmation of that.
“The Lord be praised,” he said, and came forward. It was distinctly disagreeable. It was even more disagreeable to feel his moist hand on hers. A cold shiver brushed over her. She closed her eyes. Obstinately and with all her small strength she drew her hand away from him. Hid it far down under the bedcovering, and turned her face away to hide a grimace of unconquerable aversion. She cared nothing, at that moment, for his hurt surprise. She knew only that, in the hideous agony that for interminable hours﻿—no, centuries﻿—she had borne, the luster of religion had vanished; that revulsion had come upon her; that she hated this man. Between them the vastness of the universe had come.
Miss Hartley, all-seeing and instantly aware of a situation, as she had been quite aware that her patient had been conscious for some time before she herself had announced the fact, intervened, saying firmly: “I think it might be better if you didn’t try to talk to her now. She’s terribly sick and weak yet. She’s still got some fever and we mustn’t excite her or she’s liable to slip back. And we don’t want that, do we?”
No, the man, her husband, responded, they didn’t want that. Reluctantly he went from the room with a last look at Helga, who was lying on her back with one frail, pale hand under her small head, her curly black hair scattered loose on the pillow. She regarded him from behind dropped lids. The day was hot, her breasts were covered only by a nightgown of filmy crêpe, a relic of prematrimonial days, which had slipped from one carved shoulder. He flinched. Helga’s petulant lip curled, for she well knew that this fresh reminder of her desirability was like the flick of a whip.
Miss Hartley carefully closed the door after the retreating husband. “It’s time,” she said, “for your evening treatment, and then you’ve got to try to sleep for a while. No more visitors tonight.”

===Chinese===
这样浸沉在往昔里，清爽而甘美。可如今它完结了。结束了。她丈夫普莱森特·格林牧师的话证实了这一点——他一直站在窗前，忧郁地望着外面那晒焦的瓜地，那地因为海尔加病了这么久、无力照料，已经荒了。
“赞美主。”他说着，走上前来。这已经分明地令人不快。感到他那湿漉漉的手压在自己手上，就更加令人不快。一阵寒战掠过她全身。她闭上眼睛。固执地、使出全部微弱的力气，把手从他那里抽了回来。深深藏进被褥底下，又把脸扭过去，掩住那无法克制的厌恶的怪相。那一刻，他受伤的惊讶她全不在意。她只知道，在那她忍受了难以计数的钟头——不，是几个世纪——的骇人苦痛中，宗教的光辉已经熄灭；憎厌已降临到她身上；她恨这个男人。她们之间，已横亘起宇宙的洪荒。
哈特利小姐无所不见，立刻觉察出了形势——正如她早就明明知道，病人恢复知觉已有些时候，只是等到她自己才宣布这个事实——她插了进来，语气坚决地说：“我想，您现在最好别试着同她说话。她还病得厉害，虚弱得很。还有点烧，我们决不能让她激动，不然她很容易倒回去。咱们可不想那样，对吧？”
是的，那男人，她的丈夫，答道，他们不想那样。他恋恋不舍地出了房间，临走又看了海尔加一眼——她仰卧着，一只纤弱苍白的手垫在小脑袋下，黑色鬈发蓬乱地散在枕上。她从半垂的眼睑后面目送他。天气很热，她身上只罩着一件薄纱绉的睡袍——婚前岁月的遗物——从一边雕塑般的肩头滑了下来。他一颤。海尔加任性的嘴唇卷了卷，因为她深知，自己诱人之处这一记新的明证，恰似鞭梢轻轻一挥。
哈特利小姐在退出去了的丈夫身后轻轻带上了门。“到点了，”她说，“该做晚间的治疗了，然后你得试着睡一会儿。今晚不许再会客。”

===Original===
Helga nodded and tried unsuccessfully to make a little smile. She was glad of Miss Hartley’s presence. It would, she felt, protect her from so much. She mustn’t, she thought to herself, get well too fast. Since it seemed she was going to get well. In bed she could think, could have a certain amount of quiet. Of aloneness.
In that period of racking pain and calamitous fright Helga had learned what passion and credulity could do to one. In her was born angry bitterness and an enormous disgust. The cruel, unrelieved suffering had beaten down her protective wall of artificial faith in the infinite wisdom, in the mercy, of God. For had she not called in her agony on Him? And He had not heard. Why? Because, she knew now, He wasn’t there. Didn’t exist. Into that yawning gap of unspeakable brutality had gone, too, her belief in the miracle and wonder of life. Only scorn, resentment, and hate remained﻿—and ridicule. Life wasn’t a miracle, a wonder. It was, for Negroes at least, only a great disappointment. Something to be got through with as best one could. No one was interested in them or helped them. God! Bah! And they were only a nuisance to other people.
Everything in her mind was hot and cold, beating and swirling about. Within her emaciated body raged disillusion. Chaotic turmoil. With the obscuring curtain of religion rent, she was able to look about her and see with shocked eyes this thing that she had done to herself. She couldn’t, she thought ironically, even blame God for it, now that she knew that He didn’t exist. No. No more than she could pray to Him for the death of her husband, the Reverend Mr. Pleasant Green. The white man’s God. And His great love for all people regardless of race! What idiotic nonsense she had allowed herself to believe. How could she, how could anyone, have been so deluded? How could ten million black folk credit it when daily before their eyes was enacted its contradiction? Not that she at all cared about the ten million. But herself. Her sons. Her daughter. These would grow to manhood, to womanhood, in this vicious, this hypocritical land. The dark eyes filled with tears.
“I wouldn’t,” the nurse advised, “do that. You’ve been dreadfully sick, you know. I can’t have you worrying. Time enough for that when you’re well. Now you must sleep all you possibly can.”

===Chinese===
海尔加点点头，想挤出一点微笑，却没有成功。有哈特利小姐在，她很高兴。她觉得，这会替她挡掉许多事。她心里想，可不能好得太快。既然看来是非好不可了。躺在床上她还能想事情，还能有一点清静。一点孤独。
在那段撕裂般的疼痛与灾难性的惊怖中，海尔加懂得了情欲与轻信能把人弄成什么样子。她心里生出了愤怒的苦涩和巨大的厌恶。那残忍的、无片刻缓解的苦难，把她那道人为的信仰之墙砸塌了——对上帝无穷智慧、对上帝慈悲的信仰。因为她不是在剧痛中呼唤过他么？而他没有听见。为什么？因为，她如今知道了，他不在那里。并不存在。随着那道墙，她对生命的奇迹与神奇的信念，也一并跌进了那道说不出的野蛮的豁口。剩下的只有轻蔑、怨愤和恨——还有讥诮。生命不是奇迹，不是神奇。至少对黑人而言，它只是一场大大的失望。一件只好勉力挨过去的事。没有谁把他们放在心上，没有谁帮助他们。上帝！呸！他们于别人，还只是一桩讨嫌。
她脑子里的一切又热又冷，翻腾打旋。她那消瘦的身体里奔突着幻灭。一片混沌的骚乱。宗教的遮目之幕一撕开，她便能环顾四周，用惊骇的眼睛看清自己对自己干下的事。她讥诮地想，如今既然知道上帝并不存在，她连怪罪上帝都办不到了。是的。就像她再也不能向他祈祷，求他叫她的丈夫普莱森特·格林牧师死掉一样。白人的上帝。还有他那不分种族、泽被众人的大爱！她竟容许自己信了这等愚蠢的胡话。她怎么会、任何人怎么会受这样的骗？一千万黑人又怎么可能信它，当它的反面每天都在他们眼前上演？倒不是她把这一千万放在心上。而是她自己。她的儿子们。她的女儿。他们要在这险恶的、伪善的国度里长大成人。那双黑眼睛盈满了泪水。
“你可别，”护士劝道，“这样。你知道，你病得不轻。我不能让你操心。等好了再操心也来得及。现在你要尽一切可能多睡。”

===Original===
Helga did sleep. She found it surprisingly easy to sleep. Aided by Miss Hartley’s rather masterful discernment, she took advantage of the ease with which this blessed enchantment stole over her. From her husband’s praisings, prayers, and caresses she sought refuge in sleep, and from the neighbors’ gifts, advice, and sympathy.
There was that day on which they told her that the last sickly infant, born of such futile torture and lingering torment, had died after a short week of slight living. Just closed his eyes and died. No vitality. On hearing it Helga too had just closed her eyes. Not to die. She was convinced that before her there were years of living. Perhaps of happiness even. For a new idea had come to her. She had closed her eyes to shut in any telltale gleam of the relief which she felt. One less. And she had gone off into sleep.
And there was that Sunday morning on which the Reverend Mr. Pleasant Green had informed her that they were that day to hold a special thanksgiving service for her recovery. There would, he said, be prayers, special testimonies, and songs. Was there anything particular she would like to have said, to have prayed for, to have sung? Helga had smiled from sheer amusement as she replied that there was nothing. Nothing at all. She only hoped that they would enjoy themselves. And, closing her eyes that he might be discouraged from longer tarrying, she had gone off into sleep.
Waking later to the sound of joyous religious abandon floating in through the opened windows, she had asked a little diffidently that she be allowed to read. Miss Hartley’s sketchy brows contracted into a dubious frown. After a judicious pause she had answered: “No, I don’t think so.” Then, seeing the rebellious tears which had sprung into her patient’s eyes, she added kindly: “But I’ll read to you a little if you like.”

===Chinese===
海尔加果然睡了。她发现入睡竟出奇地容易。凭着哈特利小姐颇为老练的识见，她顺水推舟地利用了这桩有福的魔法悄悄袭来时的轻易。丈夫的赞美、祈祷与爱抚，邻居们的馈赠、忠告与怜恤，她都躲进睡眠里去避难。
有那么一天，他们告诉她，那个最后的病弱婴儿——那场徒然折磨、缠绵苦楚的产物——在短短一星期的微弱生命之后死了。只是闭上了眼睛，就死了。没有生机。听了这话，海尔加也只是闭上了眼睛。不是去死。她确信面前还有经年的岁月。或许甚至还有幸福。因为她有了个新念头。她闭上眼睛，是为了关住任何一点泄露心绪的光——她感到的如释重负。少一个了。随后她便睡着了。
还有那个星期日的早晨，普莱森特·格林牧师告诉她，当天要为她的康复举行一次特别的感恩礼拜。他说，将有祈祷、特别的见证，还有歌。她可有什么特别想让人说的、想代祷的、想唱的么？海尔加纯粹出于好玩地笑了笑，回答说没有。什么也没有。她只希望他们自己尽兴。然后，为了让他知趣别再耽搁，她闭上了眼睛，又睡着了。
后来她醒来，听见欢腾狂放的宗教歌声从敞开的窗口飘进来，便有点怯怯地请求让她看看书。哈特利小姐那两道淡淡的眉毛蹙成一个迟疑的结。她审慎地停了停，答道：“不，我看不行。”随即看见病人眼里涌起的叛逆的泪水，又和气地补了一句：“不过要是你愿意，我可以念一点给你听。”

===Original===
That, Helga replied, would be nice. In the next room on a high-up shelf was a book. She’d forgotten the name, but its author was Anatole France. There was a story, “The Procurator of Judea.” Would Miss Hartley read that? “Thanks. Thanks awfully.”
“ ‘L. Aelius Lamia, born in Italy of illustrious parents,’ ” began the nurse in her slightly harsh voice.
Helga drank it in.
“ ‘… For to this day the women bring down doves to the altar as their victims.﻿ ﻿…’ ”
Helga closed her eyes.

===Chinese===
那敢情好，海尔加答道。隔壁房间高高的架子上有一本书。书名她忘了，作者是阿纳托尔·法朗士（Anatole France）。里面有一篇，《朱迪亚的总督》（“The Procurator of Judea”）。能不能请哈特利小姐念念那篇？“谢谢。多谢了。”
“‘L. 埃利乌斯·拉米亚（L. Aelius Lamia），生于意大利显赫的门第，’”护士用她那略带沙哑的声音念了起来。
海尔加如饮甘泉。
“‘……因为直到今天，女人们仍把鸽子带到祭坛前作为祭品。……’”
海尔加闭上了眼睛。

===Original===
“ ‘… Africa and Asia have already enriched us with a considerable number of gods.﻿ ﻿…’ ”
Miss Hartley looked up. Helga had slipped into slumber while the superbly ironic ending which she had so desired to hear was yet a long way off. A dull tale, was Miss Hartley’s opinion, as she curiously turned the pages to see how it turned out.
“ ‘Jesus?﻿ ﻿… Jesus﻿—of Nazareth? I cannot call him to mind.’ ”
“Huh!” she muttered, puzzled. “Silly.” And closed the book.

===Chinese===
“‘……非洲和亚洲已经给我们添上了为数可观的一批神祇。……’”
哈特利小姐抬起眼来。海尔加已经滑进了睡乡，而她那么想听的那个绝妙讥诮的结尾，还远在后头呢。依哈特利小姐看，这是个乏味的故事，她一面好奇地翻着书页，想看看结局如何。
“‘耶稣？……耶稣——拿撒勒的那位？我想不起来了。’”
“哼！”她困惑地嘟哝了一句。“蠢。”便合上了书。

## XXV / 二十五

===Original===
During the long process of getting well, between the dreamy intervals when she was beset by the insistent craving for sleep, Helga had had too much time to think. At first she had felt only an astonished anger at the quagmire in which she had engulfed herself. She had ruined her life. Made it impossible ever again to do the things that she wanted, have the things that she loved, mingle with the people she liked. She had, to put it as brutally as anyone could, been a fool. The damnedest kind of a fool. And she had paid for it. Enough. More than enough.
Her mind, swaying back to the protection that religion had afforded her, almost she wished that it had not failed her. An illusion. Yes. But better, far better, than this terrible reality. Religion had, after all, its uses. It blunted the perceptions. Robbed life of its crudest truths. Especially it had its uses for the poor﻿—and the blacks.
For the blacks. The Negroes.

===Chinese===
在漫长的康复过程中，在那阵阵梦也似的、睡意绵绵缠人的间歇里，海尔加有的是太多的时间去想。起初她只感到一种惊愕的愤怒，恨自己陷进去的这片泥沼。她把自己的生活毁了。弄得自己再也不能做想做的事、拥有心爱的东西、同喜欢的人往来。用再狠的话说，她是做了一回傻瓜。最最该死的那种傻瓜。而她也付出了代价。付够了。远不止够了。
她的心思又荡回宗教曾给过她的那片庇护，她几乎希望它不曾辜负她。一场幻梦。是的。可总比这可怕的现实好，好得多。宗教毕竟有它的用处。它磨钝人的知觉。把生活中最粗粝的真相偷走。对穷人——还有黑人——它尤其有用。
对黑人。对黑人们。

===Original===
And this, Helga decided, was what ailed the whole Negro race in America, this fatuous belief in the white man’s God, this childlike trust in full compensation for all woes and privations in “kingdom come.” Sary Jones’s absolute conviction, “In de nex’ worl’ we’s all recompense’,” came back to her. And ten million souls were as sure of it as was Sary. How the white man’s God must laugh at the great joke he had played on them! Bound them to slavery, then to poverty and insult, and made them bear it unresistingly, uncomplainingly almost, by sweet promises of mansions in the sky by and by.
“Pie in the sky,” Helga said aloud derisively, forgetting for the moment Miss Hartley’s brisk presence, and so was a little startled at hearing her voice from the adjoining room saying severely: “My goodness! No! I should say you can’t have pie. It’s too indigestible. Maybe when you’re better﻿—”
“That,” assented Helga, “is what I said. Pie﻿—by and by. That’s the trouble.”
The nurse looked concerned. Was this an approaching relapse? Coming to the bedside, she felt at her patient’s pulse while giving her a searching look. No. “You’d better,” she admonished, a slight edge to her tone, “try to get a little nap. You haven’t had any sleep today, and you can’t get too much of it. You’ve got to get strong, you know.”

===Chinese===
而这个，海尔加断定，正是美国的整个黑人种族的病根：这种对白人的上帝的痴愚信仰，这种孩子气的信赖——相信一切苦难与匮乏都将在“天国”得到十足的补偿。萨丽·琼斯那斩钉截铁的信条，“到了那个世界，咱们全都得着报偿”，又回到她心头。而一千万个灵魂都同萨丽一样对此笃信不疑。白人的上帝一定在心里笑话他跟他们开的这个大玩笑吧！先用枷锁绑他们为奴，再绑进贫穷与屈辱，又拿天上的华厦、来世的甜言，叫他们不反抗、几乎也不抱怨地忍受这一切。
“天上的馅饼。”海尔加出声讥诮道，一时忘了哈特利小姐利落的身影就在近旁，因此听见她从隔壁房间传来的严厉嗓音时不由小小一惊：“我的天！不行！我看你不能吃馅饼。太难消化了。兴许等你好些——”
“那个，”海尔加表示同意，“我就是这么说来着。馅饼——将来再说。毛病就出在这里。”
护士显出担忧的神色。这是又要反复的征兆么？她走到床边，一面给病人搭脉，一面用探究的眼光打量她。不是。“你最好，”她告诫道，语气里带一点锋芒，“小睡一会儿。你今天还没合过眼呢，多睡不嫌多。你得养壮实点儿，知道吧。”

===Original===
With this Helga was in full agreement. It seemed hundreds of years since she had been strong. And she would need strength. For in some way she was determined to get herself out of this bog into which she had strayed. Or﻿—she would have to die. She couldn’t endure it. Her suffocation and shrinking loathing were too great. Not to be borne. Again. For she had to admit that it wasn’t new, this feeling of dissatisfaction, of asphyxiation. Something like it she had experienced before. In Naxos. In New York. In Copenhagen. This differed only in degree. And it was of the present and therefore seemingly more reasonable. The other revulsions were of the past, and now less explainable.
The thought of her husband roused in her a deep and contemptuous hatred. At his every approach she had forcibly to subdue a furious inclination to scream out in protest. Shame, too, swept over her at every thought of her marriage. Marriage. This sacred thing of which parsons and other Christian folk ranted so sanctimoniously, how immoral﻿—according to their own standards﻿—it could be! But Helga felt also a modicum of pity for him, as for one already abandoned. She meant to leave him. And it was, she had to concede, all of her own doing, this marriage. Nevertheless, she hated him.
The neighbors and churchfolk came in for their share of her all-embracing hatred. She hated their raucous laughter, their stupid acceptance of all things, and their unfailing trust in “de Lawd.” And more than all the rest she hated the jangling Clementine Richards, with her provocative smirkings, because she had not succeeded in marrying the preacher and thus saving her, Helga, from that crowning idiocy.
Of the children Helga tried not to think. She wanted not to leave them﻿—if that were possible. The recollection of her own childhood, lonely, unloved, rose too poignantly before her for her to consider calmly such a solution. Though she forced herself to believe that this was different. There was not the element of race, of white and black. They were all black together. And they would have their father. But to leave them would be a tearing agony, a rending of deepest fibers. She felt that through all the rest of her lifetime she would be hearing their cry of “Mummy, Mummy, Mummy,” through sleepless nights. No. She couldn’t desert them.

===Chinese===
这话海尔加完全赞成。她壮实有力，仿佛已是几百年前的事了。而她将需要力气。因为她横下心要用什么法子把自己从这片误入的泥沼里拔出来。不然——她就只有死。她受不住了。她的窒息和她那蜷缩的憎恶太深重了。不能再承受一次了。因为她得承认，这种不满、这种窒息之感并不是新东西。类似的东西她从前也尝过。在纳克索斯。在纽约。在哥本哈根。这一次只是程度不同罢了。而它属于现在，因而显得更在情理。从前那几次嫌恶都属于过去，如今倒更说不清了。
一想到丈夫，她心里就激起一种深切的、鄙夷的恨。每当他走近，她都不得不强压住一声狂叫以示抗议的冲动。每想起自己的婚姻，羞耻也会漫过她全身。婚姻。这件牧师和别的基督徒们如此道貌岸然地鼓吹的圣事，按他们自己的标准看，竟能何等不道德！不过海尔加对他也还有一丝怜悯，像怜悯一个已被抛弃的人。她是要离开他的。而这桩婚姻，她不能不承认，全是她自己一手造成的。尽管如此，她恨他。
邻居们和教会里的人也分得了她那份无所不包的仇恨的一份。她恨他们刺耳的哄笑，恨他们对一切的愚蠢忍受，恨他们对“主”的不移的信赖。而比谁都更招她恨的，是那个浑身叮当响的克莱门蒂娜·理查兹，连同她那副撩人的假笑——因为她没能嫁给传道人，从而把海尔加从那桩登峰造极的蠢事里搭救出来。
孩子们，海尔加尽量不去想。她不想离开他们——倘若那有可能的话。她自己童年的回忆，孤独的、无人体爱的，太刺心地浮上眼前，使她无法冷静地考虑这样一条出路。虽然她强迫自己相信，这一回是不同的。这里没有种族的成分，没有黑白之分。他们全都是黑人。而且他们还有父亲。可是离开他们，将是一场撕割的酷刑，是血肉深处纤维的断裂。她觉得，往后余生，她都会在无眠的夜里听见他们“妈妈，妈妈，妈妈”的哭喊。不。她不能抛弃他们。

===Original===
How, then, was she to escape from the oppression, the degradation, that her life had become? It was so difficult. It was terribly difficult. It was almost hopeless. So for a while﻿—for the immediate present, she told herself﻿—she put aside the making of any plan for her going. “I’m still,” she reasoned, “too weak, too sick. By and by, when I’m really strong﻿—”
It was so easy and so pleasant to think about freedom and cities, about clothes and books, about the sweet mingled smell of Houbigant and cigarettes in softly lighted rooms filled with inconsequential chatter and laughter and sophisticated tuneless music. It was so hard to think out a feasible way of retrieving all these agreeable, desired things. Just then. Later. When she got up. By and by. She must rest. Get strong. Sleep. Then, afterwards, she could work out some arrangement. So she dozed and dreamed in snatches of sleeping and waking, letting time run on. Away.
---
And hardly had she left her bed and become able to walk again without pain, hardly had the children returned from the homes of the neighbors, when she began to have her fifth child.

===Chinese===
那么，她怎样才能逃脱她的生活已经变成的这重重压制、这层层屈辱呢？太难了。难极了。几乎无望。于是有一阵子——只是眼面前这一阵，她对自己说——她把出走计划的筹划搁下了。“我还，”她推理道，“太虚弱，病得太重。往后，等我真正壮起来——”
想想自由，想想都市，想想衣裳和书籍，想想柔和灯光的房间里乌比冈（Houbigant）香水与香烟交织的甜香，里面满是无关紧要的闲谈、笑语和世故的走了调的音乐——想想这些是何等轻易，何等惬意。要想出一条切实可行的路，把这些称心的、渴望的东西重新抓回来，又是何等艰难。眼下不行。回头吧。等她起来了。以后再说。她必须歇息。养壮。睡。到那时候，她自能想出个安排来。于是她在半睡半醒的片断里打着盹、做着梦，任时间往前跑。跑远。
---
她才刚下得床来，走起路来不再疼痛，孩子们才刚从邻居家里回来，她便开始怀她的第五个孩子了。
